2.17
) can be obtained by replacing Kesten’s estimate (
2.14
) with the trivial estimate

Q\bigg(\sum_{i\in[n]}X_{i},L\bigg)\leqslant\min_{i\in[n]}Q(X_{i},L)\,,

(2.18)

which follows immediately from the independence of the random variables
X_{i}
. Although this estimate lacks the factor
K/\sqrt{n}
from (
2.14
), it replaces the maximum with a minimum. Thus, an important ingredient in our recursive self-improving anticoncentration argument is an algorithm that determines which of (
2.14
) or (
2.18
) is to be used at any given vertex
x\in B_{r}(b)
. It relies on the notion of
robust vertices
.

Recursively, a vertex
x\in B_{r}(b)
is called
robust
if
x\in S_{r}(b)
or
S_{1}^{+}(x)
contains at least
d/2
robust vertices.
We denote the set of robust vertices by
\mathcal{R}
.
An important auxiliary result is that the root
b
is robust with high probability, which in particular implies that

S_{i}(b)\cap\mathcal{R}
is large for any
i\leqslant r
.
Therefore, we can restrict to
x\in S_{i}(b)\cap\mathcal{R}
and proceed similarly as in (
2.17
) to obtain

Q(g_{x}(z),(T^{2}d)^{-r+i})\leqslant Q\bigg(\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{R}}g_{y}(z),(T^{2}d)^{-r+i+1}\bigg)\leqslant\frac{K\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{d}}\max_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{R}}Q\big(g_{y}(z),(T^{2}d)^{-r+i+1}\big)\,,

where in the first step we used (
2.18
).
Thus, we obtain
Q(g_{x}(z),(T^{2}d)^{-r})\leqslant(K\sqrt{2d^{-1}})^{r-1}
for all
x\in S_{1}(b)

and, therefore, by choosing
r
such that
(T^{2}d)^{r+1}=N^{\eta}
, we arrive at
Q(g_{x}(z),N^{-\eta})\leqslant N^{-\eta/2+o(1)}
for all
x\in S_{1}(b)
.
This is the desired anticoncentration bound,
(b)
, which, as explained above, implies
(iii)
.

2.3.4. The three main exponents of
N

Throughout this paper we use the three exponents
\mu
,
\zeta
,
\eta>0
to control three central quantities of the argument. We summarize their roles here for easy reference.

•

N^{\mu}
is the typical size of the vertex sets
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
(cf. Proposition
3.2

(i)
),
as the parameter
\mu
is introduced to control
\alpha^{*}
(see (
1.6
)).
Consequently,
N^{\mu}
is also the typical number of eigenvalues of
H
satisfying (
1.7
).
Therefore, the factor
N^{\mu}
emerges from union bounds when a property is required for all
x\in\mathcal{V}
or
x\in\mathcal{W}
.

•

N^{-\zeta}
is the upper bound on the eigenvalue approximation that we establish in Proposition
2.3
, i.e. on the distance between
\lambda(x)
and the eigenvalues of
H
or, more precisely, the upper bound on
\lVert(H-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)\rVert
. Proposition
2.3
requires the condition
\zeta<1/2-3\mu/2
.

•

N^{-\eta}
is the lower bound on the eigenvalue spacing, or correspondingly
\min_{x\neq y\in\mathcal{W}}\lvert\lambda(x)-\lambda(y)\rvert
, that we prove in Proposition
2.4
. Since the typical eigenvalue spacing is
N^{-\mu}
in the interval we consider, we clearly need
\mu<\eta
.
In fact, our proof of Proposition
2.4
requires the stronger condition
8\mu<\eta
for technical reasons
as well as
\mu<1/24
.

To apply perturbation theory in the proof of Theorem
1.1
, we need that the error in the eigenvalue approximation
N^{-\zeta}
be smaller
than the eigenvalue spacing
N^{-\eta}
. This means that
\eta<\zeta
.

2.4.  Localization profile – proof of Theorem
1.6

Theorem
1.6
is an immediate consequence of the following result.

Proposition 2.9

(Local approximation for
\mathbf{u}(x)
)
.

Let
\mu\in[0,1/3)
.
Then, with high probability, the following holds for all
x\in\mathcal{W}
.
If
r\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies

\log d\ll r\leqslant\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{6}\frac{\log N}{\log d},\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{5}-\frac{\mu}{4},\frac{1}{3}-\mu\bigg\}\frac{\log N}{\log d}-2\bigg\}\,,

(2.19)

then

(i)

\mathbf{u}(x)=\mathbf{v}_{r}(x)+o(1)
,

(ii)

\mathbf{u}(x)=\mathbf{w}_{r}(x)+o(1)
.

Part
(i)
is proved in Section
3.2
below.
Part
(ii)
follows from Corollary
4.2
below, since
\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\geqslant 2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

From (
2.3
) in the proof of Theorem
1.1
, we know that
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{u}(x)+O(N^{-\varepsilon})

for a unique
x\in\mathcal{W}
and some small enough
\varepsilon>0
.
Therefore, Theorem
1.6
follows from Proposition
2.9
.
∎

2.5.  Mobility edge – proof of Theorem
1.7

Part
(i)
follows from Theorem
1.1
, since

\inf\{\alpha>0\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\mathbb{P}(\alpha_{1}\geqslant\alpha)\leqslant N^{-23/24}\}\leqslant 2
if
d\geqslant\frac{23}{24}b_{*}\log N
, by Lemma
D.1
below.
Part
(ii)
was proved in
(
ADK_delocalized
, Theorem 1.1 (ii))
.

2.6.  Localization length – proof of Theorem
1.8

For any eigenvector
\mathbf{w}
we introduce the function

q(u)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{y}\mathrm{d}(u,y)\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}\,,

so that
\ell(\mathbf{w})=\min_{u}q(u)
.

For the localized phase, suppose that
\lambda
is an eigenvalue satisfying
\lvert\lambda\rvert\geqslant 2+\kappa
with associated eigenvector
\mathbf{w}
. Denote by
x
the unique vertex associated with
\mathbf{w}
from Theorems
1.1
and
1.6
. Then the following estimates hold on the intersection of the high-probability events of these two theorems. By Theorem
1.1
, there exists a constant
R\equiv R_{\kappa}
such that

q(u)\geqslant\mathrm{d}(u,x)\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}\geqslant R\frac{\alpha_{x}-2}{2(\alpha_{x}-1)}+o(1)\geqslant\frac{\alpha_{x}}{\alpha_{x}-2}\qquad\text{for all }u\in B_{R}(x)^{c}\,,

(2.20)

where the third inequality holds for large enough constant
R
.
Moreover, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a constant
R^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
u\in B_{R}(x)
we have

\displaystyle q(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in B_{R^{\prime}}(x)}\mathrm{d}(u,y)\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}+\sum_{y\in B_{R^{\prime}}(x)^{c}}\mathrm{d}(u,y)\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in B_{R^{\prime}}(x)}\mathrm{d}(u,y)\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}+O(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in B_{R^{\prime}}(x)}\mathrm{d}(u,y)\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{v}_{r}(x)\rangle^{2}+O(\varepsilon)+o(1)\,,

(2.21)

where the second step follows from the estimate
\mathrm{d}(u,y)\leqslant R+\mathrm{d}(x,y)
and the exponential decay from Theorem
1.1
, and the third step from Theorem
1.6
with some
\log d\ll r\ll\frac{\log N}{\log d}
.

To analyse the sum, we abbreviate
k\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{d}(x,u)
and introduce the set
T_{i}(u,x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=S_{i}(x)\cap S_{i+k}(u)
for
0\leqslant i\leqslant r
, which is the set of vertices in
S_{i}(x)
whose geodesic to
x
does not pass through
u
.
By Proposition
3.2

(ii)
below, the graph
\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(x)}
is a tree, which implies

\mathrm{d}(u,y)=\begin{cases}k+i&\text{if }y\in T_{i}(u,x)\\
\lvert k-i\rvert&\text{if }y\in S_{i}(x)\setminus T_{i}(u,x)\,.\end{cases}

(2.22)

Next, we estimate
\lvert S_{i}(x)\setminus T_{i}(u,x)\rvert
. For
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
, the set
S_{i}(x)\setminus T_{i}(u,x)
consists of the unique vertex on the geodesic from
x
to
u
at distance
i
from
x
. For
i>k
, we have

S_{i}(x)\setminus T_{i}(u,x)=S_{i}(x)\cap S_{i-k}(u)
.
Here we used the tree structure of
\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(x)}
. Hence, we conclude that for
i>k
we have
\lvert S_{i}(x)\setminus T_{i}(u,x)\rvert\leqslant\lvert S_{i-k}(u)\rvert\lesssim(\log N)d^{i-k-1}
, by Proposition
3.1
.

Next, by
(
ADK20
, Lemma 5.4)
, with high probability we have
\lvert S_{i}(x)\rvert=\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert d^{i-1}(1+o(1))
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
.
Since
\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert\geqslant\alpha^{*}d\geqslant 2d
, we conclude that
\lvert S_{i}(x)\rvert\geqslant d^{i}
for
i\leqslant r
. Putting all of these estimates together, we conclude for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
that

\frac{\lvert S_{i}(x)\setminus T_{i}(u,x)\rvert}{\lvert S_{i}(x)\rvert}\lesssim\begin{cases}d^{-i}&\text{if }1\leqslant i\leqslant k\\
(\log N)d^{-k-1}&\text{if }k<i\leqslant r\,.\end{cases}

Since
T_{i}(u,x)=S_{i}(x)
for
k=0
or
i=0
, using the condition (
1.5
) we conclude that

\frac{\lvert S_{i}(x)\setminus T_{i}(u,x)\rvert}{\lvert S_{i}(x)\rvert}=o(1)

(2.23)

for all
u\in B_{R}(x)
and
0\leqslant i\leqslant r
.

Next, using (
2.22
) and recalling the definition (
1.11
), we write the above sum as

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in B_{R^{\prime}}(x)}\mathrm{d}(u,y)\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{v}_{r}(x)\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{R^{\prime}}\Biggl(\sum_{y\in T_{i}(u,x)}(k+i)\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{v}_{r}(x)\rangle^{2}+\sum_{y\in S_{i}(x)\setminus T_{i}(u,x)}\lvert k-i\rvert\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{v}_{r}(x)\rangle^{2}\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{R^{\prime}}\biggl((k+i)\,u_{i}(\alpha_{x})^{2}\frac{\lvert T_{i}(u,x)\rvert}{\lvert S_{i}(x)\rvert}+\lvert k-i\rvert\,u_{i}(\alpha_{x})^{2}\frac{\lvert S_{i}(x)\setminus T_{i}(u,x)\rvert}{\lvert S_{i}(x)\rvert}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{R^{\prime}}(k+i)u_{i}(\alpha_{x})^{2}+o(1)\,,

where in the last step we used (
2.23
), the fact that
R^{\prime}
is constant, and
\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}u_{i}(\alpha_{x})^{2}=1
.
The latter sum is clearly minimized for
k=0
.
Recalling (
2.21
), we therefore conclude that for any
u\in B_{R}(x)
we have

q(u)\geqslant q(x)+O(\varepsilon)+o(1)\,,\qquad q(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}iu_{i}(\alpha_{x})^{2}+O(\varepsilon)+o(1)

for large enough
R^{\prime}
depending on
\varepsilon
.
Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, and recalling (
2.20
), by taking the minimum over
u\in[N]
, it therefore suffices to show that

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}iu_{i}(\alpha_{x})^{2}=\frac{\alpha_{x}}{2(\alpha_{x}-2)}+o(1)=\frac{\lvert\lambda\rvert}{2\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4}}+o(1)\,.

(2.24)

The first equality of (
2.24
) is an elementary computation using the definition (
1.10
), recalling the normalization
\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}u_{i}(\alpha)^{2}=1
and that
r\gg 1
. The second equality of (
2.24
) follows from the estimate
\lvert\lambda\rvert=\Lambda(\alpha_{x})+o(1)
by Remark
1.2
, which can be inverted to obtain
\frac{1}{\alpha_{x}}=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(1-\frac{\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4}}{\lvert\lambda\rvert}\bigr)+o(1)
.

For the delocalized phase, suppose that
\lambda
is an eigenvalue satisfying
\lvert\lambda\rvert\leqslant 2-\kappa
with associated eigenvector
\mathbf{w}
. We use
(
ADK_delocalized
, Theorem 1.1 (ii))
to deduce that with probability
1-O(N^{-10})
we have
\lVert\mathbf{w}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\leqslant N^{-1+o(1)}
. Hence, with probability
1-O(N^{-9})
we have, for any
x\in[N]
and
r\geqslant 0
,

q(x)\geqslant\sum_{y\in B_{r}(x)}\mathrm{d}(x,y)\,\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}+r\sum_{y\in B_{r}(x)^{c}}\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}\\
\geqslant r\biggl(1-\sum_{y\in B_{r}(x)}\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}\biggr)\geqslant r\biggl(1-N^{-1+o(1)}\lvert B_{r}(x)\rvert\biggr)\,.

Next, we deduce from
(
ChungLu_diameter
, Lemma 1)
that for any constant
\varepsilon>0
there is a constant
\delta>0
such that, with high probability, if
r\leqslant(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\log N}{\log d}
then
\lvert B_{r}(x)\rvert\leqslant N^{1-\delta}
for all
x\in[N]
. Choosing
r\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\lfloor(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\log N}{\log d}\rfloor
, we conclude that, for any
\varepsilon>0
, with high probability, for all eigenvectors
\mathbf{w}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
satisfying
\lvert\lambda\rvert\leqslant 2-\kappa
, we have
\ell(\mathbf{w})\geqslant(1-\varepsilon+o(1))\frac{\log N}{\log d}
.
Since
\varepsilon>0
was an arbitrary constant, we conclude the stronger lower bound
\ell(\mathbf{w})\geqslant(1-o(1))\frac{\log N}{\log d}=\diam(\mathbb{G})(1+o(1))
, where the last step follows from
ChungLu_diameter
. The complementary upper bound
\ell(\mathbf{w})\leqslant\diam(\mathbb{G})
follows by definition of
\diam(\mathbb{G})
, since
\mathrm{d}(x,y)\leqslant\diam(\mathbb{G})
for all
x,y\in[N]
(here we used that under our assumption on
d
, the graph
\mathbb{G}
is with high probability connected). This concludes the proof.

3. Preliminaries

The rest of this paper is devoted to the proofs of Propositions
2.2
,

2.3
and
2.4
. We begin with a short section that collects some basic properties of the graph
\mathbb{G}
and its spectrum.

3.1.  Properties of the graph

In this subsection, we collect some basic local properties of the Erdős-Rényi graph
\mathbb{G}

around vertices in
\mathcal{V}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Suppose that
\sqrt{\log N}\ll d\leqslant 3\log N
.
With high probability, the following holds.

(i)

\max_{x\in[N]}|S_{1}(x)|\leqslant 10\log N
.

(ii)

|B_{i}(x)|\lesssim\max\{\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert,d\}d^{i-1}
for all
x\in[N]
and all
i\in\mathbb{N}
with
i\leqslant\frac{1}{3}\frac{\log N}{\log d}
.

Item
(i)
is a simple application of Bennett’s inequality and

(ii)
follows from
ADK19
;
ADK20
; a detailed proof is given in Section
E
below. In particular, by the assumption
d\gg\sqrt{\log N}
, we get from Proposition
3.1

(i)
that

\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\lesssim\sqrt{\alpha_{x}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{d}}\ll\sqrt{d}

(3.1)

for all
x\in[N]
on the high-probability event from Proposition
3.1
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\mu\in[0,1/3)
be a constant.
Suppose that
\sqrt{\log N}\ll d\leqslant 3\log N
.
With high probability, for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying

1\leqslant r\leqslant\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{5}-\frac{\mu}{4},\frac{1}{3}-\mu\bigg\}\frac{\log N}{\log d}\,,

(3.2)

the following holds.

(i)

|\mathcal{V}|\leqslant N^{\mu+o(1)}
.

(ii)

\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(x)}
is a tree for all
x\in\mathcal{V}
.

(iii)

B_{r}(x)\cap B_{r}(y)=\emptyset
for all
x,y\in{\mathcal{V}}
satisfying
x\neq y
.

(iv)

Let
\nu\in[0,1]
. If
1-\mu-\nu-r\frac{\log d}{\log N}\gtrsim 1
then
\lvert S_{1}(y)\rvert\leqslant\alpha^{*}(\nu)d
for all
y\in\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{V}}(B_{r}(x)\setminus\{x\})
.

These statements are all consequences of
ADK19
;
ADK21
; we explain the details of the proof of Proposition
3.2
in Section
E
below.

3.2.  Properties of the spectrum

In this subsection we collect basic spectral properties of
H
and some of its submatrices.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\mathbf{w}_{1}
be a normalized eigenvector of
H
with nonnegative entries associated with the largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}(H)
of
H
.

Note that, with high probability,
\mathbf{w}_{1}
is unique and coincides with the Perron-Frobenius eigenvector of the
giant component of
\mathbb{G}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Suppose (
1.5
). With high probability the following holds.

(i)

For any
\mu\in[0,1]
we have
\max\{\lambda_{2}(H^{(\mathcal{V})}),-\lambda_{N}(H^{(\mathcal{V})})\}\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+o(1)
.

(ii)

Fix
\mu\in[0,1/3)
.
If
X\subset\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{V}}B_{r}(x)
with
r\in\mathbb{N}
as in (
3.2
),
then
\lambda_{1}(H^{(X)})
and the corresponding eigenvector
\mathbf{w}
of
H^{(X)}
satisfy

\lambda_{1}(H^{(X)})=\sqrt{d}(1+o(1))\,,\qquad\qquad\biggl\lVert\mathbf{w}-\frac{\mathbf{1}_{X^{c}}}{\lvert X^{c}\rvert^{1/2}}\biggr\rVert=o(1)\,.

(iii)

Fix
\mu\in[0,1)
.
If
x\in\mathcal{V}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies
\log d\ll r\leqslant\frac{1}{6}\frac{\log N}{\log d}

and
X\subset[N]
satisfies
X\cap B_{r}(x)=\emptyset
, then

\lVert(H^{(X)}-\Lambda(\alpha_{x}))\mathbf{v}_{r}(x)\rVert=o(1)\,,

where
\mathbf{v}_{r}(x)
was defined in (
1.11
).

(iv)

Fix
\mu\in[0,4/5)
. For any
r\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
r\ll\frac{d}{\log\log N}
,
there is a normalized vector
\mathbf{q}
with
\supp\mathbf{q}\subset\big(\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{V}}B_{r+1}(x)\big)^{c}
such that

\lVert(H-\sqrt{d})\mathbf{q}\rVert\lesssim d^{-1/2}\,,\qquad\lVert\mathbf{w}_{1}-\mathbf{q}\rVert\lesssim d^{-1}\,,\qquad\lVert\mathbf{q}-N^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{[N]}\rVert\lesssim d^{-1/2}\,.

These results follow essentially from
ADK19
;
ADK20
;
ADK21
; the detailed proof is
presented in Section
C
below.

Definition 3.5
.

We denote by
\Omega
the intersection of the high-probability events of Propositions
3.1
,
3.2
and
3.4
.

In particular, on
\Omega
the estimate (
3.1
) holds.

Corollary 3.6
.

Fix
\mu\in[0,1/3)
. On
\Omega
, the following holds for all
x\in\mathcal{W}
and all
X\subset[N]
.
If

X\cap B_{r}(x)=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}\subset X\subset\bigcup_{y\in\mathcal{V}}B_{r}(y)
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying

\log d\ll r\leqslant\min\big\{\frac{1}{6},\frac{1}{5}-\frac{\mu}{4},\frac{1}{3}-\mu\big\}\frac{\log N}{\log d}

then

\lambda_{1}(H^{(X)})=\sqrt{d}(1+o(1))\,,\qquad\lambda_{2}(H^{(X)})=\Lambda(\alpha_{x})+o(1)\,,\qquad\lambda_{3}(H^{(X)})\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+o(1)\,.

Corollary
3.6
with
X=\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}
directly implies that, on
\Omega
,

\lambda(x)=\lambda_{2}(H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})})=\Lambda(\alpha_{x})+o(1)\,.

(3.3)

Proof of Corollary
3.6
.

The statement about
\lambda_{1}(H^{(X)})
is identical to Proposition
3.4

(ii)
. By eigenvalue interlacing (Lemma
D.3
), we have

\lambda_{3}(H^{(X)})\leqslant\lambda_{3}(H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus\{x\})})\leqslant\lambda_{2}(H^{({\mathcal{V}})})\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+o(1)\,,

where for the last inequality we used Proposition
3.4

(i)
.
Finally, Proposition
3.4

(iii)
implies that there exists an eigenvalue of
H^{(X)}
at distance
o(1)
from
\Lambda(\alpha_{x})
. Because of the estimates on
\lambda_{1}(H^{(X)})
and
\lambda_{3}(H^{(X)})
just proven, and because
\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa/2
for
x\in\mathcal{W}
(recall (
2.2
)) as well as
\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\ll\sqrt{d}
by (
3.1
), this eigenvalue has to be
\lambda_{2}(H^{(X)})
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.9

(i)
.

We show that the conclusion of Proposition
2.9

(i)
holds on
\Omega
.
The proof uses a spectral gap of
H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}
around
\lambda(x)=\lambda_{2}(H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})})
, that
\mathbf{v}_{r}(x)
is an approximate eigenvector
of
H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}
by Proposition
3.4

(iii)
, and perturbation theory.
Indeed, from Corollary
3.6
with
X=\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}
, recalling the definition of
\mathcal{W}
(see (
2.2
)), we obtain that
\lambda_{2}(H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})})
is separated from the other eigenvalues of
H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}
by a positive constant.
Owing to (
3.3
),
Proposition
3.4

(iii)
with
X=\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}

and Lemma
D.2
below imply
\lVert\mathbf{u}(x)-\mathbf{v}_{r}(x)\rVert=o(1)
, i.e. Proposition
2.9

(i)
.
∎

We conclude the following corollary from Proposition
2.9

(i)
and its proof.

Corollary 3.7
.

Fix
\mu\in[0,1/3)
.
On
\Omega
we have
\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{x}-2}{2(\alpha_{x}-1)}}+o(1)\gtrsim 1
for all
x\in\mathcal{W}
.

Proof.

We first note that for any
r\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
, we have
u_{0}(\alpha_{x})=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{x}-2}{2(\alpha_{x}-1)}}+o(1)
by (
1.10
).
By Proposition
2.9

(i)
and its proof,
\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle=\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{v}_{r}(x)\rangle+o(1)=u_{0}(\alpha_{x})+o(1)\gtrsim 1
on
\Omega
, where in second step we used the definition (
1.11
), and in the last step we used that
\alpha_{x}\geqslant 2+\kappa
, so that the sequence
(u_{i}(\alpha_{x}))_{i}
from (
1.10
) is exponentially decaying in
i
, uniformly in
r
.
∎

3.3.  Properties of the Green function

In this subsection, we fix
\mu\in[0,1/3)
.
Define

\mathcal{J}=[\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa/4,\sqrt{d}/2]\,.

We shall use that whenever
z\in\mathcal{J}
, all Green functions appearing in our proof are bounded, which is the content of the following result.

Lemma 3.8
.

Suppose (
1.5
) and (
3.2
).
On
\Omega
, for
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}
and
X\subset[N]
satisfying
\mathcal{V}\subset X\subset\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{V}}B_{r}(x)
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert(H^{(X)}-z)^{-1}\rVert

\displaystyle\leqslant{8}/\kappa\,,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\lVert(H^{(X)}-z)^{-1}-(H^{(X)}-z’)^{-1}\rVert

\displaystyle\leqslant(8/\kappa)^{2}\,|z-z’|\,.

(3.5)

Proof of Lemma
3.8
.

Eigenvalue interlacing (Lemma
D.3
) and
Proposition
3.4

(i)
and
(ii)
imply

\lambda_{2}(H^{(X)})\leqslant\lambda_{2}(H^{(\mathcal{V})})\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa/8\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lambda_{1}(H^{(X)})=\sqrt{d}(1+o(1))\geqslant\sqrt{d}/2+\kappa/8\,.

Therefore,
\text{dist}(z,\text{Spec}(H^{(X)}))\geqslant\kappa/8
for any
z\in\mathcal{J}
, which proves (
3.4
).
The Lipschitz bound (
3.5
) follows from (
3.4
) and the resolvent identity.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

Suppose (
1.5
) and (
3.2
).
On
\Omega
, for any
\mathcal{V}\subset X\subset\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{V}}B_{r}(x)
,
z\in\mathcal{J}
, and
y\notin X
, we have

-(H^{(X)}-z)^{-1}_{yy}\geqslant(3z)^{-1}\,.

Proof of Lemma
3.9
.

Denoting by
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\dots
and
\mathbf{w}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{2},\dots
the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
H^{(X)}
, respectively, we have

-(H^{(X)}-z)^{-1}_{yy}=\frac{\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}_{1}\rangle^{2}}{z-\lambda_{1}}+\sum_{2\leqslant i\leqslant N}\frac{\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}_{i}\rangle^{2}}{z-\lambda_{i}}\geqslant\frac{1}{z-\lambda_{N}}(1-\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}_{1}\rangle^{2})-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}_{1}\rangle^{2}}{\lambda_{1}-z}}}\right|\,,

(3.6)

where in the second step we used
z-\lambda_{N}\geqslant z-\lambda_{i}>0
for all
i\geqslant 2
,
which follows from eigenvalue interlacing (Lemma
D.3
), Proposition
3.4

(i)
, and the condition
z\in\mathcal{J}
.

To estimate the right-hand side of (
3.6
), we use Proposition
3.2

(i)
and Proposition
3.1

(ii)
as well as (
3.2
) to estimate
\lvert X\rvert\leqslant\sum_{x\in\mathcal{V}}\lvert B_{r}(x)\rvert\leqslant N^{1/3+o(1)}
on
\Omega
. From Proposition
3.4

(ii)
we therefore deduce that

\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}_{1}\rangle^{2}=\lvert X^{c}\rvert^{-1/2}+o(1)=o(1)\,.

(3.7)

We conclude that the first term on the right-hand side of (
3.6
) is bounded from below by
((2+o(1))z)^{-1}
,
as
z-\lambda_{N}\leqslant 2z
from Proposition
3.4

(i)
.
The second term on the right-hand side of (
3.6
)
is estimated using (
3.7
) as well as
\lvert\lambda_{1}-z\rvert\gtrsim\sqrt{d}\geqslant z

by Proposition
3.4

(ii)
and
z\in\mathcal{J}
.
∎

4. Exponential decay of
\mathbf{u}(x)
and proof of Proposition
2.2

In this section we establish the exponential decay of
\mathbf{u}(x)
around the vertex
x
.
In particular, Proposition
2.2
is a direct consequence of Proposition
4.1
below.
Moreover, we prove in Corollary
4.2
below that
\mathbf{u}(x)
is well approximated by
\mathbf{w}_{r}(x)
, the eigenvector of
H|_{B_{r}(x)}

corresponding to its largest eigenvalue. This implies
Proposition
2.9

(ii)
.

Throughout this section we use the high-probability event
\Omega
from Definition
3.5
.

4.1.  Simple exponential decay of
\mathbf{u}(x)

In this subsection we establish exponential decay at some positive but not optimal rate.

Proposition 4.1
.

Suppose that (
1.5
) holds. Then there is a constant
c\in(0,1)
such that,
for each fixed
\mu\in[0,1/3)
, on
\Omega
, for each
x\in\mathcal{W}
there exists
q_{x}>0
such that

\lVert\mathbf{u}(x)|_{B_{i}(x)^{c}}\rVert\lesssim\sqrt{\alpha_{x}}\,q_{x}^{i}\,,\qquad\qquad q_{x}=\frac{\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(1/2))+o(1)}{\lambda(x)}\leqslant 1-c\,,

(4.1)

for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
1\leqslant i\leqslant\min\big\{\frac{1}{5}-\frac{\mu}{4},\frac{1}{3}-\mu\big\}\frac{\log N}{\log d}-2
.

Proof.

We note that
\supp\mathbf{u}(x)\subset([N]\setminus\mathcal{V})\cup\{x\}
and decompose
\mathbf{u}(x)=\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle\mathbf{1}_{x}+Q\mathbf{u}(x)
,
where
Q\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{y\in[N]\setminus\mathcal{V}}\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\,\cdot\,\rangle\mathbf{1}_{y}
is the orthogonal projection on the coordinates in
[N]\setminus\mathcal{V}
.
We apply the projection
Q
to the eigenvalue-eigenvector relation

\lambda(x)\mathbf{u}(x)=H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}\mathbf{u}(x)=\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}/\sqrt{d}+H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}Q\mathbf{u}(x)\,,

solve for
Q\mathbf{u}(x)
, and obtain

Q\mathbf{u}(x)=\frac{\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle}{\sqrt{d}}\big(\lambda(x)-H^{(\mathcal{V})}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}\,,

(4.2)

where we used that
H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}\mathbf{1}_{x}=\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}/\sqrt{d}
and

QH^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}Q=H^{(\mathcal{V})}
.
We also used that
\lambda(x)-H^{(\mathcal{V})}
is invertible, which can be seen as follows.
From Proposition
3.4

(ii)
with
X=\mathcal{V}
and
r=0
, we conclude
\lambda_{1}(H^{(\mathcal{V})})=\sqrt{d}(1+o(1))

and, hence,
(
3.3
) and
Proposition
3.4

(i)
yield
that
\lambda(x)
is not an eigenvalue of
H^{(\mathcal{V})}
and

\dist(\lambda(x),\spec H^{(\mathcal{V})})\gtrsim 1\,.

(4.3)

Let

Q_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{y\in[N]\setminus(\mathcal{V}\cup B_{i}(x))}\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\,\cdot\,\rangle\mathbf{1}_{y}

the projection onto
the coordinates in
[N]\setminus(\mathcal{V}\cup B_{i}(x))
. As
\supp\mathbf{u}(x)\subset([N]\setminus\mathcal{V})\cup\{x\}

and
Q_{i}Q=Q_{i}
,
we conclude from (
4.2
) that

\mathbf{u}(x)|_{B_{i}(x)^{c}}=Q_{i}\mathbf{u}(x)=Q_{i}Q\mathbf{u}(x)=\frac{\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle}{\lambda(x)\sqrt{d}}Q_{i}\bigg(1-\frac{H^{(\mathcal{V})}}{\lambda(x)}\bigg)^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}\,.

(4.4)

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\bigg(1-\frac{H^{(\mathcal{V})}}{\lambda(x)}\bigg)^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigg(\frac{H^{(\mathcal{V})}}{\lambda(x)}\bigg)^{k}+\bigg(1-\frac{H^{(\mathcal{V})}}{\lambda(x)}\bigg)^{-1}\bigg(\frac{H^{(\mathcal{V})}}{\lambda(x)}\bigg)^{n+1}\,.

(4.5)

Since
H^{(\mathcal{V})}
is a local operator, we conclude that
Q_{i}(H^{(\mathcal{V})})^{k}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}=0

if
k+1\leqslant i
.
Hence, fixing
i\geqslant 1
and applying (
4.5
) with
n=i-1

to (
4.4
), we get

\mathbf{u}(x)|_{B_{i}(x)^{c}}=\frac{\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle}{\sqrt{d}}Q_{i}\big(\lambda(x)-H^{(\mathcal{V})}\big)^{-1}\bigg(\frac{H^{(\mathcal{V})}}{\lambda(x)}\bigg)^{i}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}=O\bigg(\frac{\lVert(H^{(\mathcal{V})})^{i}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}\rVert}{\sqrt{d}\lambda(x)^{i}}\bigg)\,.

(4.6)

Here, in the last step, we employed

\lvert\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle\rvert\leqslant 1
,

\lVert Q_{i}\rVert\leqslant 1
,
\lVert(\lambda(x)-H^{(\mathcal{V})})^{-1}\rVert\lesssim 1
by (
4.3
).

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
be the largest integer satisfying (
3.2
). We now claim that, on
\Omega
,

\lVert H^{(\mathcal{V})}\mathbf{v}\rVert\leqslant(\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(1/2))+o(1))\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert

(4.7)

for any
\mathbf{v}
such that
\supp\mathbf{v}\subset B_{r}(x)
. Before proving (
4.7
), we use it conclude the proof of (
4.1
).

For
i\leqslant r-2
we have
\supp(H^{(\mathcal{V})})^{i}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}\subset B_{r}(x)
, so that iterative applications of (
4.7
)
yield

\lVert(H^{(\mathcal{V})})^{i}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}\rVert\lesssim(\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(1/2))+o(1))^{i}\sqrt{\alpha_{x}}\sqrt{d}\,.

(4.8)

Hence, the first bound in (
4.1
) follows from (
4.6
).
We now show the second bound in (
4.1
).
If
\frac{\log N}{d}\geqslant T
for a sufficiently large constant
T
then
q_{x}\leqslant 1-c
for some constant
c>0

by using Corollary
A.3
with
\nu=1/2
,
\lambda(x)\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\mu))
,
\mu\leqslant 1/3
and possibly increasing its
T
.
If
\frac{\log N}{d}<T\lesssim 1
then
\alpha_{x}\lesssim 1
by Proposition
3.1

(i)

and the estimate
q_{x}\leqslant 1-c
for some constant
c>0
follows from

\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\leqslant 2\sqrt{\alpha_{x}}\lesssim 1
and
\lambda(x)=\Lambda(\alpha_{x})+o(1)\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa/4\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(1/2))+\kappa/4
,
which is a consequence of (
3.3
) and the definition of
\mathcal{W}
.
This completes the proof of the second bound in (
4.1
).

What remains is the proof of (
4.7
).
For any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{[N]}
, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality implies

\lVert(\mathbb{E}H)^{(\mathcal{V})}\mathbf{v}\rVert\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{d\lvert\supp\mathbf{v}\rvert}{N}}\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert
. By Proposition
3.1

(ii)
and (
3.2
), if
\supp\mathbf{v}\subset B_{r}(x)

then
\lvert\supp\mathbf{v}\rvert\leqslant N^{1/5+o(1)}
. Therefore,

\lVert H^{(\mathcal{V})}\mathbf{v}\rVert\leqslant\lVert(H-\mathbb{E}H)^{(\mathcal{V})}\mathbf{v}\rVert+o(\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert)\\
\leqslant\lVert(H-\mathbb{E}H)^{(\{y\,:\,\alpha_{y}\geqslant\alpha^{*}(1/2)\})}\mathbf{v}\rVert+o(\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert)\leqslant(\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(1/2))+o(1))\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert\,,

where in the second step, we used

\mathcal{V}^{c}\cap B_{r}(x)\subset\{y\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\alpha_{y}\geqslant\alpha^{*}(1/2)\}^{c}
by Proposition
3.2

(iv)

and, in the third step,

\lVert(H-\mathbb{E}H)^{(\{y\,:\,\alpha_{y}\geqslant\alpha^{*}(1/2)\})}\rVert\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(1/2))+o(1)
by
Lemma
C.1
8
8

8

Note that Lemma
C.1
holds on
\Omega
, since Lemma
C.1
is used to prove Proposition
3.4

(i)
.
.
This concludes the proof of (
4.7
).
∎

4.2.  Approximating
\mathbf{u}(x)
by
\mathbf{w}_{r}(x)

From Proposition
4.1
and its proof, we deduce the following result, which compares
\mathbf{u}(x)
and
\mathbf{w}_{r}(x)
from Definition
1.5
.

Corollary 4.2
.

Suppose that (
1.5
) holds and fix
\mu\in[0,1/3)
.
Then, on
\Omega
, for all
x\in\mathcal{W}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying (
2.19
),

\mathbf{u}(x)=\mathbf{w}_{r}(x)+O\big(\alpha_{x}q_{x}^{r}\Lambda(\alpha_{x})^{-1}\big)=\mathbf{w}_{r}(x)+o\big(\Lambda(\alpha_{x})^{-1}\big)\,,

where
q_{x}
is the same as in Proposition
4.1
.

Proof.

We shall apply Lemma
D.2
with
M=H|_{B_{r}(x)}
,
\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda(x)

and
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{u}(x)
. First, we check its conditions by studying the spectral gap
of
H|_{B_{r}(x)}
around its largest eigenvalue.
As
r\leqslant\frac{1}{6}\frac{\log N}{\log d}
, we conclude from
Proposition
3.4

(iii)
with
X=B_{r}(x)^{c}

that
H|_{B_{r}(x)}=H^{(B_{r}(x)^{c})}
has an eigenvalue
\Lambda(\alpha_{x})+o(1)
.
The bound (
4.7
) implies that
\lVert H|_{B_{r}(x)\setminus\{x\}}\rVert\leqslant\lVert H^{(\mathcal{V})}\rVert\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(1/2))+o(1)
, where in the first step we used Proposition
3.2

(iii)
. By eigenvalue interlacing (Lemma
D.3
), we therefore deduce that

\lambda_{1}(H|_{B_{r}(x)})=\Lambda(\alpha_{x})+o(1)\,,\qquad\qquad\lambda_{2}(H|_{B_{r}(x)})\leqslant\lambda_{1}(H|_{B_{r}(x)\setminus\{x\}})\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(1/2))+o(1)\,.

Thus, the definition of
\mathcal{W}
in (
2.2
) implies

\lambda_{1}(H|_{B_{r}(x)})-\lambda_{2}(H|_{B_{r}(x)})\gtrsim\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\big(1-\frac{\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(1/2))}{\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\mu))+\kappa/2}\big)\gtrsim\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\,,

where the last inequality follows from Corollary
A.3
if
\frac{\log N}{d}\geqslant T
for some large enough constant
T
, and from
\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(1/2))+\kappa/2\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\mu))+\kappa/2\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\lesssim 1
(see (
3.1
)) otherwise.
Hence, owing to (
3.3
),
there is
\Delta\gtrsim\Lambda(\alpha_{x})
such that
H|_{B_{r}(x)}
has precisely one eigenvalue in
[\lambda(x)-\Delta,\lambda(x)+\Delta]
.

Let
P_{r}
be the orthogonal projection onto the coordinates in
B_{r}(x)
.
The eigenvalue-eigenvector relation
(H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)=0

and

P_{r}H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}P_{r}=H|_{B_{r}(x)}

imply

(H|_{B_{r}(x)}-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)=-P_{r}H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}(1-P_{r})\mathbf{u}(x)-\lambda(x)(1-P_{r})\mathbf{u}(x)\,.

Therefore, since
(1-P_{r})\mathbf{u}(x)=\mathbf{u}(x)|_{B_{r}(x)^{c}}
, we get

\lVert(H|_{B_{r}(x)}-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)\rVert\leqslant\lambda(x)\lVert\mathbf{u}(x)|_{B_{r}(x)^{c}}\rVert+\lVert P_{r}H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}(\mathbf{u}(x)|_{B_{r}(x)^{c}})\rVert\lesssim\alpha_{x}q_{x}^{r}\,.

(4.9)

In the last step, we used
\lambda(x)\lesssim\alpha_{x}^{1/2}
and Proposition
4.1
to estimate the first term.
For the second term, we used that
P_{r}H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}(\mathbf{u}(x)|_{B_{r}(x)^{c}})=P_{r}H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}(\mathbf{u}(x)|_{S_{r+1}(x)})
by the locality of
H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}
as well as the identity

H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}(\mathbf{u}(x)|_{S_{r+1}(x)})=H^{(\mathcal{V})}(\mathbf{u}(x)|_{S_{r+1}(x)})
, which yield

\lVert H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}(\mathbf{u}(x)|_{S_{r+1}(x)})\rVert\lesssim\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(1/2))\lVert\mathbf{u}(x)|_{S_{r+1}(x)}\rVert\lesssim\alpha_{x}^{1/2}\lVert\mathbf{u}(x)|_{B_{r}(x)^{c}}\rVert\lesssim\alpha_{x}q_{x}^{r}

due to (
4.7
) as
r+1\leqslant\min\big\{\frac{1}{5}-\frac{\mu}{4},\frac{1}{3}-\mu\big\}\frac{\log N}{\log d}
,

\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(1/2))^{2}\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})^{2}\lesssim\alpha_{x}
, and Proposition
4.1
.

Since
\alpha_{x}\lesssim\frac{\log N}{d}
by Proposition
3.1

(i)
,

q_{x}\leqslant 1-c
for some constant
c>0
by Proposition
4.1
,

r\gg\log d\gtrsim\log\big(\frac{10\log N}{d}\big)
by (
1.5
)
and
\alpha_{x}\geqslant 2
, we obtain

\alpha_{x}q_{x}^{r}\ll 1\lesssim\alpha_{x}^{1/2}\,.

(4.10)

Therefore, owing to (
4.9
) and

\alpha_{x}^{1/2}\asymp\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\lesssim\Delta
,
we can now apply Lemma
D.2
with
M=H|_{B_{r}(x)}
,
\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda(x)

and
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{u}(x)

to conclude
\mathbf{u}(x)=\mathbf{w}_{r}(x)+O(\alpha_{x}q_{x}^{r}\Lambda(\alpha_{x})^{-1})
from (
4.9
) and
\Delta\gtrsim\Lambda(\alpha_{x})
as well as
\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle\geqslant 0
and

\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}_{r}(x)\rangle\geqslant 0
.
This proves the first equality in Corollary
4.2
.

Since
\alpha_{x}q_{x}^{r}\ll 1
by (
4.10
),
the last equality in Corollary
4.2

follows immediately.
∎

4.3.  Optimal exponential decay of
\mathbf{u}(x)

In this subsection we establish an explicit rate of exponential decay of
\mathbf{u}(x)
. It holds only for a smaller set of eigenvectors near the spectral edge, requiring
\mu
to be small enough. As pointed out in Remark
1.4
, up to the error
O(\varepsilon)
, this rate is optimal.

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose (
1.5
). Then the following holds.

(i)

(Subcritical regime) There are constants
T\geqslant 1
and
c>0
such that
if
\frac{\log N}{d}\geqslant T
then, on
\Omega
, for any small enough constant
\varepsilon>0
and
each
\mu\in[0,\varepsilon]
,

\lVert\mathbf{u}(x)|_{B_{i}(x)^{c}}\rVert\lesssim\sqrt{\alpha_{x}}\bigg(\frac{1+O(\varepsilon)}{\sqrt{\alpha_{x}-1}}\bigg)^{i+1}

for all
x\in\mathcal{W}
and
i\leqslant c\frac{\log N}{(\log d)\log\frac{10\log N}{d}}
.

(ii)

(Critical regime)
There is a constant
c>0
such that, for any constants
T\geqslant 1
and
\varepsilon>0
with
\varepsilon\leqslant c\kappa
,
if
\frac{\log N}{d}\leqslant T
and
\mu\in[0,cT^{-1}\varepsilon^{2}]
then, with high probability,

\lVert\mathbf{u}(x)|_{B_{i}(x)^{c}}\rVert\lesssim\sqrt{\alpha_{x}}\bigg(\frac{1+O(\varepsilon)}{\sqrt{\alpha_{x}-1}}\bigg)^{i+1}

for all
x\in\mathcal{W}
and
i\leqslant\frac{c\varepsilon^{2}}{T}\frac{\log N}{\log d}
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathcal{W}
.
For any
i
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we conclude from (
4.4
)
and (
4.5
) (see also (
4.6
)) that

\lVert\mathbf{u}(x)|_{B_{i}(x)^{c}}\rVert\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda(x)\sqrt{d}}\biggl\lVert Q_{i}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigg(\frac{H^{(\mathcal{V})}}{\lambda(x)}\bigg)^{k}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}\biggr\rVert+\frac{\lVert(H^{(\mathcal{V})})^{n+1}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}\rVert}{\sqrt{d}\lambda(x)^{n+1}}

since

\lvert\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle\rvert\lesssim 1
,
\lVert Q_{i}\rVert\leqslant 1
and
\lVert(\lambda(x)-H^{(\mathcal{V})})^{-1}\rVert\lesssim 1
by (
4.3
).
We denote the right-hand side of (
3.2
) by
r
. In the following, we always assume that

n+1\leqslant r
and tacitly use the graph properties listed in Proposition
3.2
.
As in the proof of Proposition
4.1
(see (
4.8
) and use
q_{x}\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
by (
4.1
)),
we find some constant
\varepsilon>0
such that if
n+1\leqslant r
then

\frac{\lVert(H^{(\mathcal{V})})^{n+1}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}\rVert}{\sqrt{d}\lambda(x)^{n+1}}\lesssim\sqrt{\alpha_{x}}(1-\varepsilon)^{n+1}\,.

(4.11)

We shall use the following result, whose proof is given at the end of this subsection.

Claim 4.4
.

Suppose that all vertices in
B_{r-1}(x)\setminus\{x\}
have degree at most
\tau d
, where
2\leqslant 2\sqrt{\tau}<\lambda(x)
. Then for all
n\leqslant r-1
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
we have on
\Omega

\frac{1}{\lambda(x)\sqrt{d}}\biggl\lVert Q_{i}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigg(\frac{H^{(\mathcal{V})}}{\lambda(x)}\bigg)^{k}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}\biggr\rVert\lesssim\sqrt{\alpha_{x}}\bigg(\frac{2}{\lambda(x)+\sqrt{\lambda(x)^{2}-4\tau}}\bigg)^{i+1}\,.

(4.12)

We now explain how we choose
\tau
and
n
in the subcritical and the critical regimes in order to deduce
Proposition
4.3
from Claim
4.4
.
In the subcritical regime, i.e. for the proof of
(i)
, we
choose
\tau\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\alpha^{*}(1-3\varepsilon)
and
n\leqslant\varepsilon\frac{\log N}{\log d}
for some small enough constant
\varepsilon>0
.
By arguing similarly as in the proof of Corollary
A.3
below, we
find a constant
T\geqslant 1
such that

\frac{4\tau}{\lambda(x)^{2}}\leqslant\frac{4\alpha^{*}(1-\varepsilon)(1+o(1))}{\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\mu))^{2}}=O(\varepsilon)<1

(4.13)

by (
3.3
) and (
2.2
) if
\frac{\log N}{d}\geqslant T
.
Here, the last inequality holds if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small. Hence, the assumption on
\tau
in Claim
4.4
holds.
Moreover, with our choice of
\tau
, the degrees in
B_{n}(x)\setminus\{x\}
are bounded by
\tau d

due to Proposition
3.2

(iv)

and our assumption on
n
.
Hence, the assumptions of Claim
4.4
hold.
As
\lambda(x)\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{d}}
by (
3.3
)
and Proposition
3.1

(i)
,
the right-hand side of (
4.11
)
is bounded by the right-hand side of (
4.12
)
if
n=iC\log\big(\frac{10\log N}{d}\big)
for some sufficiently large constant
C
. Since we need that
n+1\leqslant r
, this yields the upper bound on
i
in
(i)
.
From (
4.13
) and (
3.3
), we deduce that

\lambda(x)+\sqrt{\lambda(x)^{2}-4\tau}=\lambda(x)\big(1+\sqrt{1-\frac{4\tau}{\lambda(x)^{2}}}\big)=\Lambda(\alpha_{x})(1+o(1))(2+O(\varepsilon))\geqslant 2\sqrt{\alpha_{x}-1}(1+O(\varepsilon))^{-1}
,
which completes the proof of
(i)
.

For the proof of
(ii)
, we note that
\mu\leqslant c\varepsilon^{2}T^{-1}<1/3
for a sufficiently small constant
c>0
since
T\geqslant 1
,
\varepsilon\leqslant c\kappa
and
\kappa\leqslant 1
.
We choose
\tau=1+\varepsilon
for some constant
\varepsilon>0
.
As
\lambda(x)^{2}-4\tau=(\lambda(x)^{2}-4)(1-\frac{4\varepsilon}{\lambda(x)^{2}-4})>0

due to (
3.3
) and (
2.2
)
if
\varepsilon\leqslant c\kappa
with a small enough constant
c>0
. This establishes the first condition for (
4.12
).
We set
R\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{c\varepsilon^{2}}{T}\frac{\log N}{\log d}
and conclude from
Bennett’s inequality (Lemma
D.1
below) and Proposition
3.2

that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\alpha_{y}\leqslant\tau\text{ for all }x\in\mathcal{W}\text{ and for all }y\in B_{R}(x)\setminus\{x\}\Big)\leqslant\exp\bigg(\bigg(\mu+R\frac{\log d}{\log N}-\frac{d}{\log N}h(\varepsilon)\bigg)\log N\bigg)\,,

where
h(\varepsilon)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(1+\varepsilon)\log(1+\varepsilon)-\varepsilon
.
Since
h(\varepsilon)\geqslant 3c\varepsilon^{2}
for some small enough constant
c>0
, the upper bound on
\mu
imposed in the
statement, the definition of
R
and
\frac{d}{\log N}\geqslant\frac{1}{T}
imply that the factor in front of
\log N
is negative.
Therefore, with high probability, we can apply (
4.12
) simultaneously
for all
x\in\mathcal{W}
.
We choose
n=C(i+1)

and deduce
that if
C>0
is a large enough constant then
the error in (
4.11
) is dominated by the right-hand side of (
4.12
)
as
\lambda(x)\lesssim 1
for
\frac{\log N}{d}\leqslant T
.
We recall that
r
denotes the right-hand side of (
3.2
) and note that the condition of (
4.11
),

n+1=C(i+1)+1\leqslant r
,
can be satisfied by possibly decreasing the constant
c>0
.
Finally, we obtain
\lambda(x)+\sqrt{\lambda(x)^{2}-4\tau}=\lambda(x)+\sqrt{\lambda(x)^{2}-4\tau}+O(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\lambda(x)^{2}-4}})=2\sqrt{\alpha_{x}-1}(1+O(\varepsilon))^{-1}

similarly as argued above and in the proof of
(i)
using
\varepsilon\leqslant c\kappa

and
\kappa\leqslant 1
.
This proves
(ii)
and, thus, Proposition
4.3
.
∎

Proof of Claim
4.4
.

For
y\in S_{1}(x)
we denote by
B_{n}^{+}(y)
the ball of radius
n
around
y
in the graph
\mathbb{G}|_{[N]\setminus\{x\}}
, and we write
S_{n}^{+}(y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=B_{n}^{+}(y)\setminus B_{n-1}^{+}(y)
.
Since
\mathbb{G}|_{B_{n+1}(x)\setminus\mathcal{V}}
is a forest on
\Omega
and for each
y\in S_{1}(x)
,
\mathbb{G}|_{B_{n}^{+}(y)}
is a tree, we obtain

(A^{(\mathcal{V})})^{k}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}=\sum_{y\in S_{1}(x)}\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}\sum_{z\in S_{j-1}^{+}(y)}\mathbf{1}_{z}N_{k}(y,z)\,,

where
N_{k}(y,z)
denotes the number of walks on
\mathbb{G}|_{B_{n}^{+}(y)}
of length
k
between
y
and

z
.

Thus, for
i\geqslant 0
, we conclude

\displaystyle Q_{i}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{\lambda(x)^{k}d^{k/2}}(A^{(\mathcal{V})})^{k}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}

\displaystyle=Q_{i}\sum_{y\in S_{1}(x)}\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\sum_{z\in S_{j-1}^{+}(y)}\sum_{k=j-1}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{z}\frac{N_{k}(y,z)}{\lambda(x)^{k}d^{k/2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in S_{1}(x)}\sum_{j=i+1}^{n+1}\sum_{z\in S_{j-1}^{+}(y)}\mathbf{1}_{z}\bigg(\sum_{k=j-1}^{n}\frac{N_{k}(y,z)}{\lambda(x)^{k}d^{k/2}}\bigg)\,,

where the second step follows from the definition of
Q_{i}
. Since the sets
\{B_{n}^{+}(y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}y\in S_{1}(x)\}
, are disjoint, we conclude that

\biggl\lVert Q_{i}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigg(\frac{H^{(\mathcal{V})}}{\lambda(x)}\bigg)^{k}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}\biggr\rVert^{2}=\sum_{y\in S_{1}(x)}\sum_{j=i+1}^{n+1}\sum_{z\in S_{j-1}^{+}(y)}\bigg(\sum_{k=j-1}^{n}\frac{N_{k}(y,z)}{\lambda(x)^{k}d^{k/2}}\bigg)^{2}\,.

(4.14)

Next, let
z\in S_{j-1}^{+}(y)
for some
y\in S_{1}(x)
.
We note that
N_{k}(y,z)=0
if
k-(j-1)
is odd due to the bipartite structure of a tree.
If this difference is even, then we now show that

N_{k}(y,z)\leqslant(M^{k})_{1j}(\tau d)^{(k-(j-1))/2}\,,

(4.15)

where
M
is the adjacency matrix of
\mathbb{N}^{*}
(regarded as a graph where consecutive numbers are adjacent), see (
D.2
) below for a precise definition,
under the assumption that the degree of each vertex in
\mathbb{G}|_{B_{n}^{+}(y)}
is bounded by
\tau d
.

For the proof of (
4.15
), we introduce the set of walks on
\mathbb{N}^{*}

W_{k}(j)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\text{walks }\gamma\text{ on }\mathbb{N}^{*}\text{ of length }k\text{ such that }\gamma(0)=1\text{ and }\gamma(k)=j\}\,,

and for each
\gamma\in W_{k}(j)
we introduce the set of walks on
\mathbb{G}|_{B_{n}^{+}(y)}
that project down to
\gamma
,

W_{k}(y,z;\gamma)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\text{walks }\Gamma\text{ on }\mathbb{G}|_{B_{n}^{+}(y)}\text{ of length }k\text{ such that }\Gamma(0)=y,\Gamma(k)=z\text{ and }\mathrm{d}(x,\Gamma(\,\cdot\,))=\gamma\}\,.

By definition, any walk
\Gamma\in W_{k}(y,z;\gamma)
projects down to a walk
\gamma\in W_{k}(j)
, which implies

N_{k}(y,z)\leqslant\sum_{\gamma\in W_{k}(j)}\lvert W_{k}(y,z;\gamma)\rvert\,.

(4.16)

In order to prove (
4.15
), we fix
\gamma\in W_{k}(j)
and estimate
\lvert W_{k}(y,z;\gamma)\rvert
.
For
i\in[j]
, let
T_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\max\{t\in\{0,\dots,k\}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\gamma(t)=i\}
, i.e.
T_{i}
is the last time
when
\gamma
hits
i
. Clearly,
T_{1}<T_{2}<\ldots<T_{j-1}<T_{j}=k
. See Figure
4.1
for an illustration of the walks
\gamma
and
\Gamma
. At each time
T_{i}
with
i\in[j-1]
, the walk
\gamma
takes a step to the right, and by definition of the times
T_{i}
, any walk
\Gamma\in W_{k}(y,z;\gamma)
takes a step outwards on the geodesic from
y
to
z
. This means that
j-1
of the
k
steps of
\Gamma
are fixed. Of the remaining
k-(j-1)
steps, half correspond to steps to the left of
\gamma
, which again correspond to a uniquely determined step of
\Gamma
along the unique path back towards
y
. Hence, only
(k-(j-1))/2
of the
k
steps of
\Gamma
are free to choose. Since the degrees in
\mathbb{G}|_{B_{n}^{+}(y)}
are bounded by
\tau d
, we obtain

\lvert W_{k}(y,z;\gamma)\rvert\leqslant(\tau d)^{(k-(j-1))/2}
. Plugging this estimate into (
4.16
) implies (
4.15
), since
\lvert W_{k}(j)\rvert=(M^{k})_{1j}
by definition of
M
.

Figure 4.1:
An illustration of two walks
\gamma\in W_{k}(j)
(bottom) and
\Gamma\in W_{k}(y,z;\gamma)
(top). Here
k=21
and
j=6
. By definition of
W_{k}(y,z;\gamma)
, for each
t\in\{0,\dots,k\}
we have
\gamma(t)=\mathrm{d}(x,\Gamma(t))
. The time
T_{i}
is the last time when
\gamma
hits
i
. We draw an edge
\{\Gamma(t),\Gamma(t+1)\}
in red if the choice of the vertex
\Gamma(t+1)
is uniquely determined by
\gamma
and in blue otherwise. In the latter case, there are at most
\tau d
possible choices for
\Gamma(t+1)
. Red edges arise in two ways: (i) a step to the right in
\gamma
following a time
T_{i}
(
j-1
in total, in
\Gamma
corresponding to a step towards
z
along the geodesic from
y
to
z
); (ii) a step to the left in
\gamma
(
(k-(j-1))/2
in total, in
\Gamma
corresponding to a step towards
y
).

Next, applying (
4.15
) to (
4.14
), we obtain

\displaystyle\biggl\lVert Q_{i}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigg(\frac{H^{(\mathcal{V})}}{\lambda(x)}\bigg)^{k}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(x)}\biggr\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j=i+1}^{n+1}\lvert S_{j}(x)\rvert\bigg(\sum_{k=j-1}^{n}\frac{(M^{k})_{1j}(\tau d)^{(k-(j-1))/2}}{\lambda(x)^{k}d^{k/2}}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j=i+1}^{n+1}\lvert S_{j}(x)\rvert\frac{1}{(\tau d)^{j-1}}\bigg(\sum_{k=j-1}^{n}\frac{(M^{k})_{1j}\tau^{k/2}}{\lambda(x)^{k}}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j=i+1}^{n+1}\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert\frac{1}{\tau^{j-1}}\bigg(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(M^{k})_{1j}\tau^{k/2}}{\lambda(x)^{k}}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=i+1}^{n+1}\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert\frac{1}{\tau^{j-1}}\bigg(\bigg(1-\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{\lambda(x)}M\bigg)^{-1}_{1j}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=i+1}^{n+1}\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert\lambda(x)^{2}\bigg(\frac{2}{\lambda(x)+\sqrt{\lambda(x)^{2}-4\tau}}\bigg)^{2j}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert\lambda(x)^{2}\bigg(\frac{2}{\lambda(x)+\sqrt{\lambda(x)^{2}-4\tau}}\bigg)^{2(i+1)}\,.

Here, in the third step, we used that
\lvert S_{j}(x)\rvert\lesssim\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert d^{j-1}
by Proposition
3.1

(ii)

and that
(M^{k})_{1j}\geqslant 0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
The fourth step follows from the condition
2\sqrt{\tau}/\lambda(x)<1
, the invertibility and the Neumann series representation of
M
in Lemma
D.5
below with
t=\lambda(x)/\sqrt{\tau}
.
The fifth step is a consequence of the representation of
(1-\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{\lambda(x)}M)^{-1}_{1j}

in Lemma
D.5
below.
In the last step, we used that
\lambda(x)=\Lambda(\alpha_{x})+o(1)\geqslant 2+\kappa/4
to sum up the geometric series and conclude that the series is
\lesssim 1
.
This completes the proof of (
4.12
) and, thus, the one of
Claim
4.4
.
∎

5. Approximate eigenvalues – proof of Proposition
2.3

In this section we prove Proposition
2.3
, by showing that in the interval
\mathcal{I}
there is a one-to-one correspondence between eigenvalues of
H
and the points
\lambda(x)
for
x\in\mathcal{W}
, up to a polynomially small error term.

5.1.  Proof of Proposition
2.3

We recall
\mathbf{w}_{1}
from Definition
3.3
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\Pi
be the orthogonal projection onto
\operatorname{span}\big(\{\mathbf{w}_{1}\}\cup\{\mathbf{u}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in{\mathcal{W}}\}\big)
and
\overline{\Pi}\!\,\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1-\Pi
.

Note that the set
\{\mathbf{w}_{1}\}\cup\{\mathbf{u}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in{\mathcal{W}}\}
in the definition of
\Pi
is not orthogonal. Throughout the proof, we regard
H
as a block matrix associated with the orthogonal sum decomposition
\ran\Pi\oplus(\ran\Pi)^{\perp}
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Suppose (
1.5
).
Fix
\mu\in[0,1/3)
and
\zeta\in[0,1/2-\mu)
.
With high probability, the following holds.

(i)

\lVert(H-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)\rVert\leqslant N^{-\zeta}
for all
x\in\mathcal{W}
.

(ii)

If
\zeta<1/2-3\mu/2
then

\spec(\Pi H\Pi)\setminus\{0\}=\{\lambda_{1}(H)\}\cup\{{\lambda}(x)+\varepsilon_{x}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in\mathcal{W}\}

counted with multiplicity, where
|\varepsilon_{x}|\leqslant N^{-\zeta}
for all
x\in\mathcal{W}
and
\lambda_{1}(H)=\sqrt{d}(1+o(1))
.

(iii)

If
\zeta<1/2-3\mu/2
then

\|\overline{\Pi}H\Pi\|\leqslant N^{-\zeta}
.

(iv)

If
\mu<1/4
then
\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}H\overline{\Pi})\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa/2+o(1)
.

Proof of Proposition
2.3
.

Owing to the block decomposition
H=\Pi H\Pi+\overline{\Pi}\!\,H\overline{\Pi}\!\,+\overline{\Pi}\!\,H\Pi+\Pi H\overline{\Pi}\!\,
,
Proposition
5.2

(iii)
yields

\spec(H)\setminus\{0\}=\big\{\lambda+\varepsilon_{\lambda}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\lambda\in(\spec(\Pi H\Pi)\cup\spec(\overline{\Pi}H\overline{\Pi}))\big\}\setminus\{0\}

counted with multiplicities, where
|\varepsilon_{\lambda}|\leqslant 2\|\overline{\Pi}H\Pi\|\leqslant N^{-\zeta}
for all
\lambda
.
Therefore, Proposition
2.3
follows from the definition of
\mathcal{I}
as well as Proposition
5.2

(ii)
,
(iv)
, and
(i)
.
∎

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
5.2
. We assume the condition (
1.5
) throughout. We recall the definition of the high probability event
\Omega
from Definition
3.5
. For any event
A
and random variable
X
we write

\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(A)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{P}(\Omega\cap A)\,,\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[X]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{E}[X\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}]\,.

(5.1)

5.2.  Proof of Proposition
5.2

(i)

The proof of Proposition
5.2

(i)
relies on the following result, whose proof is given at the end of this subsection.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\mu\in[0,1/3)
.
For any
x\in{\mathcal{W}}
, we have the decomposition

(H-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)=\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus\{x\}}\varepsilon_{y}(x)\mathbf{1}_{y}\,.

(5.2)

Moreover, for any
x,y\in[N]
, we have the estimate

\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\mathbbm{1}_{y\in{\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}}}\,\varepsilon_{y}(x)^{2}]\leqslant d^{-1}(10\log N)^{2}N^{2\mu-3}\,.

From Proposition
5.3
, for any
x\in[N]
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\|(H-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)\|^{2}\bigr]\leqslant d^{-1}(10\log N)^{2}N^{2\mu-2}\,.

(5.3)

Proof of Proposition
5.2

(i)
.

From (
5.3
), a union bound, and Chebyshev’s inequality,
we conclude that

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\exists x\in\mathcal{W},\,\|(H-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)\|>N^{-\zeta}\bigr)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\Omega^{c})+N^{2\zeta+2\mu-1+o(1)}

for any
\zeta>0
.
This proves Proposition
5.2

(i)
since
\zeta<1/2-\mu
by assumption, and
\mathbb{P}(\Omega^{c})=o(1)
by Propositions
3.1
,

3.2
and
3.4
.
∎

We shall need modifications of the sets
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
defined in (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), respectively. For
X\subset[N]
we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(X)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\biggl\{y\in[N]\setminus X\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\frac{\lvert S_{1}(x)\setminus X\rvert}{d}\geqslant\alpha^{*}(\mu)\biggr\}\,,

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{(X)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\biggl\{y\in\mathcal{V}^{(X)}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\Lambda\biggl(\frac{\lvert S_{1}(x)\setminus X\rvert}{d}\biggr)\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\mu))+\kappa/2\biggr\}\,.

(5.5)

The point of these definitions is that
\mathcal{V}^{(X)}
and
\mathcal{W}^{(X)}
depend only on the edges in
\mathbb{G}|_{X^{c}}
.
The following remark states that on the event
\Omega
the effect of the upper index in these definitions amounts simply to excluding vertices.

Remark 5.4
.

On
{\Omega}\cap\{y\in\mathcal{V}\}
, owing to Proposition
3.2

(iii)
,
we have
\mathcal{V}^{(y)}=\mathcal{V}\setminus\{y\}
and
\mathcal{W}^{(y)}=\mathcal{W}\setminus\{y\}
.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Note that
H-H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}
is
d^{-1/2}
times the adjacency matrix of the subgraph of
\mathbb{G}
containing the edges incident to
\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}
.
Hence, because
(H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus\{x\})}-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)=0
and
\supp\mathbf{u}(x)\subset(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})^{c}
, we find

(H-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)=(H-H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})})\mathbf{u}(x)=\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus\{x\}}\varepsilon_{y}(x)\mathbf{1}_{y}\,,\qquad\varepsilon_{y}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{t\in S_{1}(y)}\langle\mathbf{1}_{t}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle\,.

(5.6)

See Figure
5.1
for an illustration.

Figure 5.1:
An illustration of the identity (
5.6
). The set
\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}
is drawn in green and its complement
(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})^{c}
in blue. The blue neighbours of the green vertices are drawn explicitly, and the remaining blue vertices are represented by the shaded blue region. The edges of incident to
\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}
are drawn in red. The adjacency matrix of these red edges is
A-A^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})}
. The vector
\mathbf{u}(x)
is supported on the blue vertices, and hence the vector
(A-A^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\})})\mathbf{u}(x)
is supported on the green vertices. Its value at a green vertex
y
equals the sum of the entries of
\mathbf{u}(x)
at the blue vertices adjacent to
y
.

The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality implies

\varepsilon_{y}(x)^{2}\leqslant\frac{|S_{1}(y)|}{d}\sum_{t\in S_{1}(y)}\langle\mathbf{1}_{t}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle^{2}\leqslant\frac{10\log N}{d}\sum_{t\in S_{1}(y)}\langle\mathbf{1}_{t}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle^{2}

(5.7)

on
{\Omega}
due to Proposition
3.1

(i)
.
From now on we fix
x,y\in[N]
.
Moreover, let

\tilde{\mathbf{u}}(x)
be the eigenvector of
H^{(\mathcal{V}^{(y)}\cup\{y\}\setminus\{x\})}
associated
with its second largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}(H^{(\mathcal{V}^{(y)}\cup\{y\}\setminus\{x\})})
and satisfying
\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}(x)\rangle\geqslant 0
.
On
{\Omega}\cap\{y\in\mathcal{V}\}
, we have
{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{V}}^{(y)}\cup\{y\}
by Remark
5.4
and, thus,
\mathbf{u}(x)=\tilde{\mathbf{u}}(x)
. As
x\neq y
, on
\Omega\cap\{y\in\mathcal{V}\}
, the events
\{x\in\mathcal{W}\}
and
\{x\in\mathcal{W}^{(y)}\}
coincide by Remark
5.4
.
Therefore, since
\mathcal{W}^{(y)}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}(x)
are
\sigma(A^{(y)})
-measurable, we obtain

\displaystyle\mspace{-20mu}\mathbb{E}_{{\Omega}}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{x\in{\mathcal{W}}}\mathbbm{1}_{y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}}\sum_{t\in S_{1}(y)}\langle\mathbf{1}_{t}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{x\in{{\mathcal{W}}^{(y)}}}\sum_{t\in[N]}\langle\mathbf{1}_{t}\mspace{2mu},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}(x)\rangle^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\alpha^{*}(\mu)d\leqslant\lvert S_{1}(y)\rvert\leqslant 10\log N}\mathbbm{1}_{t\in S_{1}(y)}\big|A^{(y)}\big]\bigg]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}(x\in\mathcal{W}^{(y)})\max_{t\in[N]}\mathbb{P}\big(t\in S_{1}(y),\,\alpha^{*}(\mu)d\leqslant\lvert S_{1}(y)\rvert\leqslant 10\log N\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}(x\in{\mathcal{W}}^{(y)})\sum_{\alpha^{*}(\mu)d\leqslant k\leqslant 10\log N}\mathbb{P}(\lvert S_{1}(y)\rvert=k)\frac{k}{N}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}(x\in{\mathcal{V}})\mathbb{P}(y\in\mathcal{V})\frac{10\log N}{N}\,.

(5.8)

Here, in the first step, in addition, we spelled out the condition
y\in\mathcal{V}
as
\lvert S_{1}(y)\rvert\geqslant\alpha^{*}(\mu)d
, used that
\lvert S_{1}(y)\rvert\leqslant 10\log N
on
\Omega

by Proposition
3.1

(i)

and then dropped the indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}
.
The second step follows from the independence of the event
\{\alpha^{*}(\mu)d\leqslant\lvert S_{1}(y)\rvert\leqslant 10\log N,t\in S_{1}(y)\}
from
\sigma(A^{(y)})
and

\sum_{t\in[N]}\langle\mathbf{1}_{t}\mspace{2mu},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}(x)\rangle^{2}=\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}(x)\|^{2}=1
.
(For these two steps, see also (
2.9
).)
For the third step, we conditioned on
\lvert S_{1}(y)\rvert
and used that if
\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert=k
then
t
lies in a uniformly
distributed subset of
[N]\setminus\{y\}
with
k
elements. For the last step, we used that
\mathcal{W}^{(y)}\subset\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}
.

Finally, applying (
5.8
)
to (
5.7
) and using the estimate
\mathbb{P}(x\in\mathcal{V})\leqslant N^{\mu-1}
(by the definitions (
2.1
) and (
1.6
)) concludes the proof of Proposition
5.3
.
∎

5.3.  Proof of Proposition
5.2

(ii)
,
(iii)

In this section, we conclude Proposition
5.2

(ii)

and
(iii)
from the following result, which is also proved in this section.

Definition 5.5
.

Order the elements of
\mathcal{W}
in some arbitrary fashion, and denote by
(\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x))_{x\in\mathcal{W}}
the Gram-Schmidt orthonormalization of
(\mathbf{u}(x))_{x\in\mathcal{W}}
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\mu\in[0,1/3)
. Then the following holds with high probability.
For any
x\in\mathcal{W}
, we have

\|(H-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)\|\lesssim N^{\mu-1/2+o(1)}\,.

(5.9)

More generally, denoting
\mathcal{D}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\lambda(x)\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)(\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x))^{*}
we have

\|(H-\mathcal{D})\mathbf{u}\|\lesssim N^{3\mu/2-1/2+o(1)}\|\mathbf{u}\|

(5.10)

for all
\mathbf{u}\in\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf{u}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in\mathcal{W}\}
.

Proof of Proposition
5.2

(ii)
.

By Definition
5.1
,
\mathbf{w}_{1}
is an eigenvector of
\Pi H\Pi
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}(H)
.
Let
\zeta<1/2-3\mu/2
.
From (
5.10
), we conclude that, for each
x\in\mathcal{W}
, there is
\varepsilon_{x}\in[-N^{-\zeta},N^{-\zeta}]

such that
\{\lambda(x)+\varepsilon_{x}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in\mathcal{W}\}\subset\spec(\Pi H\Pi)\setminus\{0\}
counted with multiplicity.
By Proposition
3.4

(ii)
,
\lambda_{1}(H)=\sqrt{d}(1+o(1))
.
Hence, by (
3.1
) and (
3.3
),

\lambda_{1}(H)\gg\Lambda(\alpha_{x})+o(1)=\lambda(x)

(5.11)

for any
x\in\mathcal{W}
.
Therefore, we have found
1+\lvert\mathcal{W}\rvert
non-zero eigenvalues of
\Pi H\Pi
(counted with multiplicity).
Since the dimension of
\ran\Pi
is at most
1+\lvert\mathcal{W}\rvert
, this completes the proof of Proposition
5.2

(ii)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.2

(iii)
.

In order to estimate
\lVert\overline{\Pi}\!\,H\Pi\rVert
, let
\mathbf{v}\in\ran\Pi
.
We decompose
\mathbf{v}=\alpha\mathbf{w}_{1}+\mathbf{u}
from some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{u}\in\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf{u}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in\mathcal{W}\}
. Let
\zeta<1/2-3\mu/2
. From (
5.10
), we obtain that

\overline{\Pi}\!\,H\mathbf{v}=\overline{\Pi}\!\,\alpha\lambda_{1}(H)\mathbf{w}_{1}+\overline{\Pi}\!\,\mathcal{D}\mathbf{u}+o(N^{-\zeta}\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert)=o(N^{-\zeta}\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert)\,,

(5.12)

where the last steps follows from
\mathbf{w}_{1}\in\ran\Pi
and
\ran\mathcal{D}\subset\ran\Pi
due to the definitions of
\mathcal{D}
and
\Pi
.
It remains to show that
\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert
.
From (
5.10
), we also conclude

\lambda_{1}(H)\langle\mathbf{w}_{1}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{1}\mspace{2mu},H\mathbf{u}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{1}\mspace{2mu},\mathcal{D}\mathbf{u}\rangle+o(N^{-\zeta}\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert)\,.

(5.13)

Since

\lVert\mathcal{D}\rVert\leqslant\max_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\lambda(x)\ll\lambda_{1}(H)
by (
5.11
), we obtain from (
5.13
) that
\lvert\langle\mathbf{w}_{1}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}\rangle\rvert\ll\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert

and, thus,

2\lvert\alpha\rvert\lvert\langle\mathbf{w}_{1}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}\rangle\rvert\leqslant\alpha^{2}+\lvert\langle\mathbf{w}_{1}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}\rangle\rvert^{2}=\alpha^{2}+o(\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert^{2})
.
Therefore,

\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert^{2}=\alpha^{2}+\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert^{2}+2\alpha\langle\mathbf{w}_{1}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}\rangle\geqslant\alpha^{2}+\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert^{2}-2\lvert\alpha\rvert\lvert\langle\mathbf{w}_{1}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}\rangle\rvert\geqslant\alpha^{2}/2+(1-o(1))\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert^{2}\,.

(5.14)

Hence,
\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert\leqslant(1-o(1))\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert
which completes the proof of Proposition
5.2

(iii)
due to (
5.12
).
∎

The main ingredient in the proof of Proposition
5.6
is the following result. It uses the following orthogonal projection.

Definition 5.7

(
\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}
)
.

For any
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{W}
, we denote by
\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}
the orthogonal projection onto
\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf{u}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in\mathcal{X}\}
and we define
\overline{\Pi}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=I-\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Proposition 5.8
.

If
\mu\in[0,1/3)
then

\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}\bigg[\max_{\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{W}}\|\overline{\Pi}_{\mathcal{X}}H\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}\|^{2}\bigg]\lesssim d^{-1}(\log N)^{2}N^{2\mu-1}\,.

Before proving Proposition
5.8
we deduce Proposition
5.6
from it.

Proof of Proposition
5.6
.

Recall from Definition
5.5
that the elements of
\mathcal{W}
are ordered in an arbitrary fashion.
For
x\in\mathcal{W}
, let
\Pi_{<x}
be the orthogonal projection onto
\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf{u}(y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}y\in\mathcal{W},\,y<x\}
and
\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{<x}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1-\Pi_{<x}
.
Then for any
x\in\mathcal{W}
we have

\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)=\frac{\mathbf{u}(x)-\Pi_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x)}{\|\mathbf{u}(x)-\Pi_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x)\|}=\frac{\overline{\Pi}_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x)}{\|\overline{\Pi}_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x)\|}\,.

(5.15)

In order to estimate
(H-\lambda(x))\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x)
, we conclude
from the definitions of
\Pi_{<x}
and
\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{<x}
that

H\overline{\Pi}_{<x}=\overline{\Pi}_{<x}H\overline{\Pi}_{<x}+\Pi_{<x}H\overline{\Pi}_{<x}=\overline{\Pi}_{<x}H-\overline{\Pi}_{<x}H\Pi_{<x}+\Pi_{<x}H\overline{\Pi}_{<x}\,,

which then yields

(H-\lambda(x))\overline{\Pi}_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x)=\overline{\Pi}_{<x}(H-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)+\Pi_{<x}H\overline{\Pi}_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x)-\overline{\Pi}_{<x}H\Pi_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x)\,.

Hence,

\|(H-\lambda(x))\overline{\Pi}_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x))\|\leqslant\|(H-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)\|+2\|\overline{\Pi}_{<x}H\Pi_{<x}\|\,.

From (
5.3
) combined with a union bound over
x
, Proposition
5.8
, and Chebyshev’s inequality, we deduce that with high probability

\max_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\|(H-\lambda(x))\overline{\Pi}_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x))\|\leqslant N^{\mu-1/2+o(1)}\,.

Moreover, since
\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(y)\rangle=0
for
x
,
y\in\mathcal{W}
satisfying
y<x
, we find that with high probability, for all
x\in\mathcal{W}
,

\|\overline{\Pi}_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x)\|^{2}=\|\mathbf{u}(x)-\Pi_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x)\|^{2}\geqslant\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)-\Pi_{<x}\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle^{2}=\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle^{2}\gtrsim 1\,,

where the last step follows from Corollary
3.7
and the fact that
\mathbb{P}(\Omega)=1-o(1)
. Plugging these two estimates into (
5.15
) yields (
5.9
).

For the proof of (
5.10
), we write

\mathbf{u}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{W}}a_{x}\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)
and apply (
5.9
) and Cauchy-Schwarz,

\|(H-\mathcal{D})\mathbf{u}\|=\biggl\lVert\sum_{x\in\mathcal{W}}a_{x}(H-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)\biggr\rVert\leqslant N^{\mu-1/2+o(1)}|\mathcal{W}|^{1/2}\bigg(\sum_{x\in\mathcal{W}}a_{x}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\,.

Hence, (
5.10
)
follows from
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}
and Proposition
3.2

(i)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.8
.

Let
\mathbf{v}\in\ran\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}
. We write
\mathbf{v}=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}a_{x}\mathbf{u}(x)
with

a_{x}\in\mathbb{R}
, use (
5.2
) and
\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{\mathcal{X}}\mathbf{u}(x)=0

for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
to obtain

\displaystyle\|\overline{\Pi}_{\mathcal{X}}H\mathbf{v}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\biggl\lVert\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}a_{x}\overline{\Pi}_{\mathcal{X}}\bigg(\lambda(x)\mathbf{u}(x)+\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}}}\varepsilon_{y}(x)\mathbf{1}_{y}\bigg)\biggr\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\biggl\lVert\overline{\Pi}_{\mathcal{X}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X},y\in{\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}}}\varepsilon_{y}(x)a_{x}\mathbf{1}_{y}\biggr\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{V}}}\bigg(\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}\setminus\{y\}}}\varepsilon_{y}(x)a_{x}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\bigg(\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}a_{x}^{2}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{W}},\,y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}}\varepsilon_{y}(x)^{2}\bigg)\,.

Moreover, since
\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle=0
for any
y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}
, on
\Omega
we have

\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}\geqslant\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}|\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{v}\rangle|^{2}=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}a_{x}^{2}\lvert\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(x)\rangle\rvert^{2}\geqslant c\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}a_{x}^{2}

for some constant
c>0
by Corollary
3.7
.
Therefore, on
\Omega
we have

\displaystyle\|\overline{\Pi}_{\mathcal{X}}H\mathbf{v}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{c}\bigg(\sum_{x\in\mathcal{W},\,y\in{\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}}}\varepsilon_{y}(x)^{2}\bigg)\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}

and, in particular,
\|\overline{\Pi}_{\mathcal{X}}H\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}\|^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{c}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{x\in\mathcal{W},\,y\in{\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}}}\varepsilon_{y}(x)^{2}}}\right)
for any
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{W}
.
By Proposition
5.3
, we therefore conclude

\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{x\in\mathcal{W},\,y\in{\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}}}\varepsilon_{y}(x)^{2}}}\right]=\sum_{x,y\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\mathbbm{1}_{y\in{\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}}}\,\varepsilon_{y}(x)^{2}\big]\lesssim d^{-1}(\log N)^{2}N^{2\mu-1}\,,

as claimed.
∎

5.4.  Proof of Proposition
5.2

(iv)

For the proof of Proposition
5.2

(iv)
, we shall need several notions from the works
ADK19
;
ADK20
.
As in
(
ADK20
, eq. (1.8))
, we set

r_{\star}=\lfloor c\sqrt{\log N}\rfloor

(5.16)

for the constant

from
ADK20
.
Following
(

\xi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\sqrt{\log N}}{d}\log d\,,\qquad\qquad\xi_{u}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\sqrt{\log N}}{d}\frac{1}{u}

(5.17)

for
u>0
.
For any
\tau\in[1+\xi^{1/2},2]
, we denote by
\mathbb{G}_{\tau}
the
pruned graph
introduced in

(
ADK20
, Proposition 3.1)
.
We denote the balls and spheres in
\mathbb{G}_{\tau}
around a vertex
x\in[N]
by
B_{i}^{\tau}(x)
and
S_{i}^{\tau}(x)
, respectively.
The pruned graph
\mathbb{G}_{\tau}
is a subgraph of
\mathbb{G}
, which possesses a number of useful properties listed
in
(
ADK20
, Proposition 3.1)
. In particular, the balls
B_{2r_{\star}}^{\tau}(x)
and

in

are disjoint if
x
,

satisfy
x\neq y
and
\min\{\alpha_{x},\alpha_{y}\}\geqslant\tau
.

Recalling the definition of
u_{i}(\alpha)
from (
1.10
), for any
x\in[N]
with

\alpha_{x}\geqslant 2+\xi^{1/4}
and
\sigma=\pm
, as in
(
ADK20
, eq. (3.5))
, we define

\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}^{\tau}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{i=0}^{r_{\star}}\sigma^{i}u_{i}(\alpha_{x})\frac{\mathbf{1}_{S_{i}^{\tau}(x)}}{\lVert\mathbf{1}_{S_{i}^{\tau}(x)}\rVert}\,,

(5.18)

where, for the last coefficient

we make the special choice
u_{r_{\star}}(\alpha_{x})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=u_{r_{\star}-1}(\alpha_{x})/\sqrt{\alpha_{x}}
, and
u_{0}(\alpha_{x})>0
is chosen such that
\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}^{\tau}(x)
is normalized, i.e.
\sum_{i=0}^{r_{\star}}u_{i}^{2}(\alpha_{x})=1
.

Remark 5.9
.

The family
(\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}^{\tau}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in[N],\alpha_{x}\geqslant 2+\xi^{1/4},\,\sigma=\pm)
is orthonormal. See
(
ADK20
, Remark 3.3)

As in
(
ADK20
, Definition 3.6)
, we denote the adjacency matrix of
\mathbb{G}_{\tau}
by
A^{\tau}
and define the
matrix

H^{\tau}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(A^{\tau}-\chi^{\tau}(\mathbb{E}A)\chi^{\tau})/\sqrt{d}\,,

(5.19)

where
\chi^{\tau}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf{1}_{y}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}y\notin\bigcup_{x\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\alpha_{x}\geqslant\tau}B_{2r_{\star}}^{\tau}(x)\}
.
Moreover, we recall
(
ADK20
, Definition 3.10)
.

Definition 5.10

(
\Pi^{\tau}
,
\widehat{H}^{\tau}
)
.

Define the orthogonal projections (see Remark

)

\Pi^{\tau}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{x\,:\,\alpha_{x}\geqslant 2+\xi^{1/4}}\sum_{\sigma=\pm}\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}^{\tau}(x)\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}^{\tau}(x)^{*}\,,\qquad\qquad\overline{\Pi}\!\,^{\tau}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1-\Pi^{\tau}

and the associated block matrix (recall (
5.19
))

\widehat{H}^{\tau}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{x\,:\,\alpha_{x}\geqslant 2+\xi^{1/4}}\sum_{\sigma=\pm}\sigma\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}^{\tau}(x)\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}^{\tau}(x)^{*}+\overline{\Pi}\!\,^{\tau}H^{\tau}\overline{\Pi}\!\,^{\tau}\,.

(5.20)

We note that, for any
\tau\in[1+\xi^{1/2},2]
, by (
1.5
) we have

\xi=o(1)\,,\qquad\qquad\xi_{\tau-1}=o(1)\,.

(5.21)

For any
\tau\in[1+\xi^{1/2},2]
, the definition of
\widehat{H}^{\tau}
in (
5.20
) and
(
ADK20
, Proposition 3.12)
yield that,
with high probability,

\lVert\widehat{H}^{\tau}\rVert\leqslant\max\bigg\{\max_{x\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\alpha_{x}\geqslant 2+\xi^{1/4}}\Lambda(\alpha_{x}),\,2\tau+C\xi\bigg\}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{d}}\ll\sqrt{d}\,,

(5.22)

where we used (
3.1
),
(
5.21
) and (
1.5
) in the last two steps.

Owing to (
5.21
),
(
ADK20
, Lemmas 3.8, 3.11)
9
9

9

We stress that the definition of
H
in
ADK20
differs from that in the current paper by
\mathbb{E}H
; see
(
ADK20
, Definition 3.6)
.
,
with high probability, we have

\lVert H-\mathbb{E}H-\widehat{H}^{\tau}\rVert=o(1)\,.

(5.23)

From (
5.23
) and (
5.22
),
we conclude that, with high probability,

\lVert H-\mathbb{E}H\rVert\ll\sqrt{d}\,.

(5.24)

After these preparations, we can start the proof of Proposition
5.2

(iv)
. We begin with the following definition.

Definition 5.11

(
Q_{r}
)
.

For
r\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
Q_{r}
the orthogonal projection defined by restriction to the set
(\bigcup_{y\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}}}B_{r}(y))^{c}
.

Definition 5.12

(
Q
,
\Pi_{Q}
)
.

Let
r_{\star}
be as in (
5.16
).
Set
Q\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=Q_{2r_{\star}-1}
and
define

\Pi_{Q}
as the orthogonal projection onto
\operatorname{span}(\{Q\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in{\mathcal{W}}\}\cup\{Q\mathbf{w}_{1}\})
, and write
\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1-\Pi_{Q}
.

Then

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}H\overline{\Pi})

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}(H-\mathbb{E}H)\overline{\Pi})+\|\overline{\Pi}(\mathbb{E}H)\overline{\Pi}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\overline{\Pi}_{Q})+2\lVert H-\mathbb{E}H\rVert\|\Pi-\Pi_{Q}\|+\|H-\mathbb{E}H-\widehat{H}^{\tau}\|+\lVert\overline{\Pi}\!\,(\mathbb{E}H)\overline{\Pi}\!\,\rVert

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\overline{\Pi}_{Q})+2\lVert H-\mathbb{E}H\rVert\|\Pi-\Pi_{Q}\|+o(1)\,,

(5.25)

whose last step follows from (

) and

(5.26)

where we introduced
\mathbf{e}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=N^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{[N]}
, used
\Pi\mathbf{w}_{1}=\mathbf{w}_{1}
in the second step, and in the last step we used Proposition

(iv)
to estimate
\lVert\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{w}_{1}\rVert\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{w}_{1}-\mathbf{q}\rVert+\lVert\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{e}\rVert\lesssim d^{-1/2}
.

Fix
\mu\in[0,1/4)
. We then claim that with high probability

\|\Pi-\Pi_{Q}\|\lesssim d^{-1}

(5.27)

and

\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q})\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa/2\,.

(5.28)

Using (

5.5.  Proof of (
5.27
)

Clearly,

\lVert\Pi-\Pi_{Q}\rVert=\lVert\Pi\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}-\overline{\Pi}\!\,\Pi_{Q}\rVert\leqslant\lVert\Pi\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\rVert+\lVert\overline{\Pi}\!\,\Pi_{Q}\rVert=\lVert\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\Pi\rVert+\lVert\overline{\Pi}\!\,\Pi_{Q}\rVert\,,

(5.29)

where in the last step we used that
(\Pi\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q})^{*}=\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\Pi
.
In order to estimate the terms on the right-hand side, we continue with

\lVert\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\Pi\rVert^{2}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{v}\in\ran\Pi\\
\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\lVert\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\mathbf{v}\rVert^{2}\leqslant\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{v}\in\ran\Pi\\
\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\ran\Pi_{Q}\\
\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\lVert\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}\rVert^{2}\,.

(5.30)

We now apply the next lemma, whose proof is given in Section
5.7
below.

Lemma 5.13
.

Fix
\mu\in[0,1/3)
.
With high probability,
for all
x\in\mathcal{W}
,
y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
r\ll\frac{\log N}{\log d}
,
we have

\|\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)|_{B_{r}(y)}\|\lesssim N^{-1/2+\mu+o(1)}\,.

Owing to Lemma
5.13
as well as parts
(iii)
and
(i)
of Proposition
3.2
, we obtain

\lVert\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)-Q\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)\rVert=\Bigg(\sum_{y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}}\lVert\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)|_{B_{2r_{\star}-1}(y)}\rVert^{2}\Bigg)^{1/2}\leqslant N^{-1/2+3\mu/2+o(1)}\,.

(5.31)

Let
\mathbf{q}
be as in Proposition
3.4

(iv)
with
r=2r_{\star}-2\ll\frac{d}{\log\log N}
by (
1.5
).
Since
\supp\mathbf{q}\subset\big(\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{V}}B_{r+1}(x)\big)^{c}=\big(\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{V}}B_{2r_{\star}-1}(x)\big)^{c}
,
we conclude from Proposition
3.4

(iv)
and the definition of
Q
that

\|\mathbf{w}_{1}-Q\mathbf{w}_{1}\|=\lVert(1-Q)(\mathbf{w}_{1}-\mathbf{q})\rVert\leqslant\|\mathbf{w}_{1}-\mathbf{q}\|\lesssim d^{-1}\,.

(5.32)

For
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{x}\in\mathbb{R}
for
x\in\mathcal{W}
write

\mathbf{v}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\gamma_{1}\mathbf{w}_{1}+\sum_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\gamma_{x}\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)\in\ran\Pi\,,\qquad\mathbf{u}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\gamma_{1}Q\mathbf{w}_{1}+\sum_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\gamma_{x}Q\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)\in\ran\Pi_{Q}\,.

(5.33)

Then

\lVert\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}\rVert\lesssim d^{-1}\lvert\gamma_{1}\rvert+N^{-1/2+3\mu/2+o(1)}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\lvert\gamma_{x}\rvert\lesssim d^{-1}\lvert\gamma_{1}\rvert+N^{-1/2+2\mu+o(1)}\bigg(\sum_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\lvert\gamma_{x}\rvert^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\lesssim d^{-1}\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert\,.

Here, we used in (
5.32
) and (
5.31
) in the
first step, Proposition
3.2

(i)
in the second step
and, in the fourth step,
\mu<1/4
as well as

\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert^{2}\asymp\lvert\gamma_{1}\rvert^{2}+\sum_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\lvert\gamma_{x}\rvert^{2}

(the inequality
\gtrsim
follows from (
5.14
) and the orthogonality of
(\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x))_{x\in\mathcal{W}}
; the inequality
\lesssim
is trivial).
Hence, if
\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert=1
then
\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert=1+O(d^{-1})
and, thus,
\lVert\mathbf{v}-\frac{\mathbf{u}}{\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert}\rVert\lesssim d^{-1}
.
Therefore,
\lVert\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\Pi\rVert\lesssim d^{-1}
by (
5.30
).

Finally, similarly to (
5.30
), we have

\lVert\overline{\Pi}\!\,\Pi_{Q}\rVert^{2}\leqslant\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\ran\Pi_{Q}\\
\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{v}\in\ran\Pi\\
\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\lVert\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}\rVert^{2}\,,

and the same argument as above, with the representation (
5.33
), implies that the right-hand side is
O(d^{-1})
.
By (
5.29
), we therefore conclude (
5.27
).

5.6.  Proof of (
5.28
)

We begin by introducing another orthogonal projection.

Definition 5.14

(
\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}
)
.

Let
\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}
be the orthogonal projection onto
\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf{v}^{\tau}_{+}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}z\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\}
.

For any
z\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}
, we have
\supp\mathbf{v}^{\tau}_{\pm}(z)\subset B_{r_{\star}}(z)

and, thus, by definition of
Q
,
\supp\mathbf{v}^{\tau}_{\pm}(z)\cap\supp Q\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)=\emptyset
for any
x\in\mathcal{W}
and

\supp\mathbf{v}^{\tau}_{\pm}(z)\cap\supp Q\mathbf{w}_{1}=\emptyset
.
Therefore,
\mathbf{v}^{\tau}_{\pm}(z)
is orthogonal to
\ran\Pi_{Q}
, i.e.
\Pi_{Q}\mathbf{v}_{\pm}^{\tau}(x)=0
.
That implies
\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}\Pi_{Q}=0=\Pi_{Q}\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}
and, in particular,
\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}

and
\Pi_{Q}
commute.
Since
\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}
and
\widehat{H}^{\tau}
commute and
\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}
and
\Pi_{Q}
commute, we obtain

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\overline{\Pi}_{Q})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}\overline{\Pi}_{Q}+\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\overline{\Pi}_{\mathbf{v}}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\overline{\Pi}_{\mathbf{v}}\overline{\Pi}_{Q})

(5.34)

\displaystyle=\max\{\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}\overline{\Pi}_{Q}),\,\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\overline{\Pi}_{\mathbf{v}}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\overline{\Pi}_{\mathbf{v}}\overline{\Pi}_{Q})\}\,.

By the definition of
\widehat{H}^{\tau}
in (
5.20
), we have

\lambda_{1}(\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\Pi_{\mathbf{v}})=\max_{x\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}}}\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa/2\,.

(5.35)

What remains, therefore, is to estimate
\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\overline{\Pi}_{\mathbf{v}}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\overline{\Pi}_{\mathbf{v}}\overline{\Pi}_{Q})
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{w}
is a normalized eigenvector of
\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\overline{\Pi}_{\mathbf{v}}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\overline{\Pi}_{\mathbf{v}}\overline{\Pi}_{Q}
such that the associated eigenvalue
\lambda
satisfies
\lambda\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa/2
.
We now check that the next lemma, whose proof is given in Section
5.7
below, is applicable to
\mathbf{w}
and
\lambda
for any
x\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
r_{\star}\in\mathbb{N}
be as in (
5.16
). In particular,
r_{\star}\asymp\sqrt{\log N}
.
Suppose
\tau\in[1+\xi^{1/2},2]
.

There is a constant
C>0
such that the following holds with high probability
10
10

10

We note that the statement actually holds with
very high probability
, meaning that for each
\nu>0
, there is a constant
C\equiv C_{\nu}>0
such that (
5.37
) holds with probability at least
1-N^{-\nu}
for all sufficiently large

.
.
Let

,

and

(5.36)

If
\alpha_{x}\geqslant 2+\xi^{1/4}
and
\mathbf{v}_{-}^{\tau}(x)\perp\mathbf{w}\perp\mathbf{v}_{+}^{\tau}(x)
or

2+\xi^{1/4}>\alpha_{x}\geqslant\tau
then

\frac{\lvert\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle\rvert}{\lVert\mathbf{w}|_{B_{2r_{\star}}^{\tau}(x)}\rVert}\lesssim\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(\lambda-2\tau-C\xi)^{2}}\bigg(\frac{2\tau+C\xi}{\lambda}\bigg)^{r_{\star}}\,.

(5.37)

An analogous result holds if
\lambda<-2\tau-C\xi
.

Choosing

and taking

, we now verify the conditions of Lemma
5.15
for
\mathbf{w}
and
\lambda
.
Owing to (
5.21
), there is a constant

such that

\frac{2\tau+C\xi}{\lambda}\leqslant 1-c

in (

).
In particular,
\lambda>2\tau+C\xi
.
From
\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\overline{\Pi}_{\mathbf{v}}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\overline{\Pi}_{\mathbf{v}}\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\mathbf{w}=\lambda\mathbf{w}
we conclude that
\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}
and
\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}
.
Thus, as
\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}
and
\widehat{H}^{\tau}
commute, we get
\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\mathbf{w}=\lambda\mathbf{w}
.
Restricting both sides in the last identity to
