yields
(\widehat{H}^{\tau}\mathbf{w})|_{B_{2r_{\star}-1}^{\tau}(x)}=\lambda\mathbf{w}|_{B^{\tau}_{2r_{\star}-1}(x)}
as
Q
is the restriction to
\big(\bigcup_{y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}}B_{2r_{\star}-1}(y)\big)^{c}
and
B_{2r_{\star}-1}^{\tau}(x)\subset B_{2r_{\star}-1}(x)
by
(
ADK20
, Proposition 3.1 (iv))
.
This proves (
5.36
).
Note that
\alpha_{x}\geqslant 2+\xi^{1/4}
.
From
\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}
, we conclude
\mathbf{w}\perp\mathbf{v}^{\tau}_{+}(x)
.
Since
(\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}^{\tau}(y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\alpha_{y}\geqslant 2+\xi^{1/4},\,\sigma=\pm)
is an orthonormal family by Remark
5.9
,
the definition of
\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}
implies
\Pi_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{v}_{-}^{\tau}(y)=0
for all
y\in\mathcal{V}
.
Therefore, as moreover
\Pi_{Q}\mathbf{v}_{-}^{\tau}(x)=0
, we have
\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{\mathbf{v}}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{\mathbf{v}}\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\mathbf{v}_{-}^{\tau}(x)=-\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\mathbf{v}_{-}^{\tau}(x)
.
Hence,
\mathbf{w}\perp\mathbf{v}_{-}^{\tau}(x)
.
Therefore, we have verified all assumptions of Lemma
5.15
with
\tau=1+\xi^{1/2}

for
\mathbf{w}
,
\lambda
, and any
x\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}
.

Since
\frac{2\tau+C\xi}{\lambda}\leqslant 1-c
as shown above, for the right-hand side of (
5.37
), we get

\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(\lambda-2\tau-C\xi)^{2}}\bigg(\frac{2\tau+C\xi}{\lambda}\bigg)^{r_{\star}}=\bigg(1-\frac{2\tau+C\xi}{\lambda}\bigg)^{-2}\bigg(\frac{2\tau+C\xi}{\lambda}\bigg)^{r_{\star}}\leqslant c^{-2}(1-c)^{r_{\star}}\ll d^{-1/2}

as
r_{\star}\asymp\sqrt{\log N}
.
Therefore, Lemma
5.15
, the disjointness of the balls
(B_{2r_{\star}}^{\tau}(x))_{x\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}}
(see the paragraph after (
5.17
) as well as
(
ADK20
, Proposition 3.1 (i))
) and
\lVert\mathbf{w}\rVert^{2}=1
imply

\lVert\mathbf{w}|_{\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}}\rVert^{2}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}}\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}\ll d^{-1}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}}\lVert\mathbf{w}|_{B_{2r_{\star}}^{\tau}(x)}\rVert^{2}\leqslant d^{-1}\,.

(5.38)

We recall from Definition
5.11
that
Q_{0}
denotes the orthogonal projection defined by restriction to the set
(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})^{c}
.
Since
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}|_{\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}}+Q_{0}\mathbf{w}
, we obtain
from (
5.38
),
Q_{0}\widehat{H}^{\tau}Q_{0}=(\widehat{H}^{\tau})^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}
, and
\lVert\widehat{H}^{\tau}\rVert\ll\sqrt{d}
by (
5.22
) that

\displaystyle\lambda=\langle\mathbf{w}\mspace{2mu},\widehat{H}^{\tau}\mathbf{w}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}\mspace{2mu},(\widehat{H}^{\tau})^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\mathbf{w}\rangle+o(1)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}\mspace{2mu},\overline{\Pi}_{Q}(\widehat{H}^{\tau})^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\mathbf{w}\rangle+o(1)

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}_{Q}(\widehat{H}^{\tau})^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\overline{\Pi}_{Q})+o(1)

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}(\widehat{H}^{\tau}+(\mathbb{E}H))^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\overline{\Pi})+o(1)

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\overline{\Pi})+o(1)\,.

(5.39)

Here, the third step is a consequence of
\overline{\Pi}\!\,_{Q}\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}
.
In the fifth step, we used (
5.26
) and (
5.27
) and the
sixth step follows from (
5.23
) and
\lVert M^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}\rVert\leqslant\lVert M\rVert
for any matrix
M
.

We now apply the next result, whose proof is given in Section
5.7
below.

Lemma 5.16
.

Fix
\mu\in[0,1/3)
. With high probability,

\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\overline{\Pi})\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+o(1).

From Lemma
5.16
and (
5.39
),
we deduce that
\lambda\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+o(1)
, in contradiction with the assumption
\lambda\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa/2
. We conclude that
\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}_{Q}\overline{\Pi}_{{\mathbf{v}}}\widehat{H}^{\tau}\overline{\Pi}_{{\mathbf{v}}}\overline{\Pi}_{Q})\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa/2
.
Owing to (
5.34
) and (
5.35
),
this proves (
5.28
).

5.7.  Proofs of auxiliary results

In this final subsection we prove Lemmas
5.13
,
5.15
, and
5.16
.

Proof of Lemma
5.13
.

For fixed
y\in[N]
, on the event
\Omega\cap\{y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\}
, if
x\in\mathcal{W}^{(y)}
, let
\mathbf{u}^{(y)}(x)
be the eigenvector of
H^{((\mathcal{V}^{(y)}\cup\{y\})\setminus\{x\})}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}(H^{((\mathcal{V}^{(y)}\cup\{y\})\setminus\{x\})})
and satisfying
\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}^{(y)}(x)\rangle>0
. See Corollaries
3.6
and
3.7
for the existence and uniqueness of
\mathbf{u}^{(y)}(x)
.

In analogy to Definition
5.5
, let
((\mathbf{u}^{(y)})^{\perp}(x))_{x\in\mathcal{W}^{(y)}}
be the Gram-Schmidt orthonormalization of
(\mathbf{u}^{(y)}(x))_{x\in\mathcal{W}^{(y)}}
.
On
\Omega\cap\{y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\}
, we have
\mathcal{V}^{(y)}\cup\{y\}=\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}^{(y)}=\mathcal{W}
by Remark
5.4
.
Therefore,
\mathbf{u}^{(y)}(x)=\mathbf{u}(x)
and, thus,
(\mathbf{u}^{(y)})^{\perp}(x)=\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{W}^{(y)}=\mathcal{W}
on
\Omega\cap\{y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\}
.
Hence, for fixed
x
,
y\in[N]
with
x\neq y
, we estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{W},\,y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}}\lVert\mathbf{u}^{\perp}(x)|_{B_{r}(y)}\rVert^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{W}^{(y)}}\sum_{a\in[N]\setminus\{y\}}\langle\mathbf{1}_{a}\mspace{2mu},(\mathbf{u}^{(y)})^{\perp}(x)\rangle^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{a\in B_{r}(y)}\mathbbm{1}_{y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leqslant\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{W}^{(y)}}\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{r}^{(y)}}\sum_{a\in[N]\setminus\{y\}}\langle\mathbf{1}_{a}\mspace{2mu},(\mathbf{u}^{(y)})^{\perp}(x)\rangle^{2}\sum_{b\in B_{r-1}^{(y)}(a)}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{b\in S_{1}(y)}\mathbbm{1}_{\alpha^{*}(\mu)d\leqslant\lvert S_{1}(y)\rvert\leqslant 10\log N}\,\Big|\,A^{(y)}\Big]\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leqslant\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{W}^{(y)}}\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{r}^{(y)}}\sum_{a\in[N]\setminus\{y\}}\langle\mathbf{1}_{a}\mspace{2mu},(\mathbf{u}^{(y)})^{\perp}(x)\rangle^{2}\lvert B_{r-1}^{(y)}(a)\rvert\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\max_{b\in[N]\setminus\{y\}}\mathbb{P}(b\in S_{1}(y),\alpha^{*}(\mu)d\leqslant\lvert S_{1}(y)\rvert\leqslant 10\log N)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\lesssim(\log N)d^{r-1}\mathbb{P}(x\in\mathcal{W}^{(y)})\max_{b\in[N]\setminus\{y\}}\mathbb{P}(b\in S_{1}(y),\,\alpha^{*}(\mu)d\leqslant\lvert S_{1}(y)\rvert\leqslant 10\log N)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\lesssim\frac{(\log N)^{2}d^{r-1}}{N}\mathbb{P}(x\in\mathcal{V})\mathbb{P}(y\in\mathcal{V})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leqslant N^{-3+2\mu+o(1)}\,.

Here, in the first step, we also used that
\langle\mathbf{1}_{y}\mspace{2mu},(\mathbf{u}^{(y)})^{\perp}(x)\rangle=0
for all
y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}
and
x\in\mathcal{W}^{(y)}
.
In the second step, we conditioned on
A^{(y)}
,
employed the notations

\displaystyle B_{i}^{(y)}(a)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\text{ball of radius $i$ around $a$ in the graph $\mathbb{G}|_{[N]\setminus\{y\}}$}

\displaystyle\Omega^{(y)}_{r}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\lvert B_{r}^{(y)}(z)\rvert\lesssim(\log N)d^{r-1}\text{ for all }z\in[N]\setminus\{y\}\}

and used that
\mathcal{W}^{(y)}
,
(\mathbf{u}^{(y)})^{\perp}(x)
, and
B_{r-1}^{(y)}(a)
are
A^{(y)}
-measurable,
that
\Omega\subset\Omega_{r}^{(y)}
by Proposition
3.1
, as

r\ll\frac{\log N}{\log d}
, and
that
a\in B_{r}(y)
is equivalent to
b\in S_{1}(y)
for some
b\in B_{r-1}^{(y)}(a)
.
The third step follows from the independence of
b\in S_{1}(y)
and
\lvert S_{1}(y)\rvert
from
A^{(y)}
.
The normalization of
(\mathbf{u}^{(y)})^{\perp}(x)
and the definition of
\Omega_{r}^{(y)}
imply the fourth step.
In the fifth step, we argued as in the last steps of (
5.8
) and, finally,
we used
r\ll\frac{\log N}{\log d}
as well as the definitions of
\mathcal{V}
in (
2.1
) and of
\alpha^{*}
in (
1.6
).

Therefore, a union bound over
x
,
y\in[N]
with
x\neq y
and Chebyshev’s inequality complete
the proof of Lemma
5.13
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.15
.

In order to prove Lemma
5.15
, we follow
(
ADK20
, Proof of Proposition 3.14 (i))
11
11

11

Note that
{\mathcal{V}}_{\tau}=\{x\in[N]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\alpha_{x}\geqslant\tau\}
and
\mathcal{V}={\mathcal{V}}_{2+\xi^{1/4}}
in
ADK20
, see
(

, eq. (3.3))
, which differs from the definition of
\mathcal{V}
in the present paper,
see (
2.1
).
,
whose assumptions are all satisfied apart from the eigenvalue-eigenvector relation

.

We now explain the necessary, minor, modifications.
We start with the case
\alpha_{x}\geqslant 2+\xi^{1/4}
and
\mathbf{w}\perp\mathbf{v}_{\pm}^{\tau}(x)

in

, Proposition 3.14)
.

.

.

P_{2r_{\star}-1}

.
For any

, since
\supp\mathbf{g}_{i}\subset B_{2r_{\star}-1}^{\tau}(x)

,

.
Therefore, for any

,

\displaystyle\lambda u_{i}=\langle\mathbf{g}_{i}\mspace{2mu},\lambda P_{2r_{\star}-1}\mathbf{w}\rangle=\langle(\widehat{H}^{\tau}-\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\mathbf{v}_{+}^{\tau}(x)\mathbf{v}_{+}^{\tau}(x)+\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\mathbf{v}_{-}^{\tau}(x)\mathbf{v}_{-}^{\tau}(x))\mathbf{g}_{i}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle=\langle\widehat{H}^{\tau,x}\mathbf{g}_{i}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle\,.

Here, we used (

) and

Proof of Lemma
5.16
.

We write
H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}
in the block decomposition

H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}=\Pi H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\Pi+\overline{\Pi}H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\overline{\Pi}+\overline{\Pi}H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\Pi+\Pi H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\overline{\Pi}\,.

The nonzero eigenvalues of the block diagonal arise as the eigenvalues of the individual diagonal blocks, i.e.

\spec(\Pi H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\Pi+\overline{\Pi}H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\overline{\Pi})\setminus\{0\}=\big(\spec(\Pi H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\Pi)\cup\spec(\overline{\Pi}H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\overline{\Pi})\big)\setminus\{0\}\,,

counted with multiplicity.
Therefore, for any
1\leqslant i
,
j\leqslant N
there are at least

i+j
eigenvalues of the block diagonal larger than
\min\{\lambda_{i}(\overline{\Pi}H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\overline{\Pi}),\,\lambda_{j}(\Pi H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\Pi)\}
provided this number is positive.
Hence, we conclude

\min\{\lambda_{1}(\overline{\Pi}H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\overline{\Pi}),\,\lambda_{1+|{\mathcal{W}}|}(\Pi H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\Pi)\}\leqslant\lambda_{2+|{\mathcal{W}}|}(H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})})+2\|\overline{\Pi}\!\,H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}{\Pi}\|\,.

(5.40)

Moreover, using eigenvalue interlacing (Lemma
D.3
) and Proposition
3.4

(i)
, we obtain

\lambda_{2+|{\mathcal{W}}|}(H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})})\leqslant\lambda_{2}(H^{({\mathcal{V}})})\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+o(1)\,.

(5.41)

Lemma
5.16
follows from (
5.40
) and (
5.41
)
provided that we show that there is a constant
c>0
such that

\lambda_{1+|{\mathcal{W}}|}(\Pi H^{({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{W}})}\Pi)\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+c\,,

(5.42)

and

\lVert\overline{\Pi}\!\,H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}\Pi\rVert=o(1)\,.

(5.43)

For the proof of (
5.42
), we recall the projections
\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}
from Definition
5.7
and
Q_{0}
from Definition
5.11
.
By the definition of
\mathbf{u}(x)
for
x\in\mathcal{W}
, we have
\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}=Q_{0}\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}
.
Hence, (
5.10
) implies
(H-\mathcal{D})Q_{0}\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}=o(1)
.
Applying
Q_{0}
to the last relation yields

H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}=\mathcal{D}\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}+o(1)

(5.44)

as
Q_{0}HQ_{0}=H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}

and
Q_{0}\mathcal{D}Q_{0}=\mathcal{D}
.
Since
\ran\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}\subset\ran\Pi
and
\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}\mathcal{D}\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}=\mathcal{D}
, the definition of
\mathcal{D}
and (
5.44
) imply that

\Pi H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}\Pi
has at least
\lvert\mathcal{W}\rvert
many eigenvalues in

[\min_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\lambda(x)-o(1),\max_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\lambda(x)+o(1)]
.
Note that
\min_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\lambda(x)\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+c
for some constant
c>0
by
(
3.3
) and (
2.2
).

Furthermore, let
\mathbf{q}
be as in Proposition
3.4

(iv)
with
r=1
.
From
\lVert H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}\rVert\leqslant\lVert H\rVert\lesssim\sqrt{d}
by (
5.24
)
and
\lVert\mathbb{E}H\rVert\lesssim\sqrt{d}
,
Proposition
3.4

(iv)
and

H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}\mathbf{q}=H\mathbf{q}
, we deduce

H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}\mathbf{w}_{1}=H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}\mathbf{q}+o(1)=H\mathbf{q}+o(1)=H\mathbf{w}_{1}+o(1)=\lambda_{1}(H)\mathbf{w}_{1}+o(1)\,.

(5.45)

Hence,
\Pi H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}\Pi
has an eigenvalue at
\lambda_{1}(H)+o(1)=\sqrt{d}(1+o(1))\gtrsim\sqrt{d}\gg\max_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\lambda(x)+o(1)

by Proposition
3.4

(ii)
, (
3.1
) and (
3.3
).
Therefore,
\Pi H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})}\Pi
has
1+\lvert\mathcal{W}\rvert
many eigenvalues
larger or equal to
\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+c
for some constant
c>0
.
This proves (
5.42
).

Finally, (
5.43
) follows from
(
5.44
),
\ran\mathcal{D}\subset\ran\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}\subset\ran\Pi
and
(
5.45
).
This completes the proof of Lemma
5.16
.
∎

6. Eigenvalue spacing – proof of Proposition
2.4

We recall the definition of the high-probability event
\Omega
from
Definition
3.5
.
In this section we use the notation from (
5.1
), as well as the conditional versions

\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(A\,|\,\mathcal{F})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{P}(\Omega\cap A\,|\,\mathcal{F})\,,\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[X\,|\,\mathcal{F}]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{E}[X\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\,|\,\mathcal{F}]\,.

Throughout this section, we assume that

d
satisfies (
1.5
),
\mu\in(0,1/3)
and that
\eta
satisfies

0<\frac{\eta}{2}<\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{6},\frac{1}{5}-\frac{\mu}{4},\frac{1}{3}-\mu\bigg\}\,.

(6.1)

Proposition
2.4
follows directly from the following result.

Proposition 6.1
.

For any
a\neq b\in[N]
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(a,b\in\mathcal{W},\,|\lambda(a)-\lambda(b)|\leqslant N^{-\eta})\leqslant N^{-2+2\mu-\eta/4+o(1)}\,.

(6.2)

Remark 6.2
.

If we restrict ourselves to the critical regime
d\asymp\log N
, then Proposition
6.1
can be improved by replacing the factor
N^{-\eta}
inside the probability in (
6.2
) by
N^{-\eta/2}
. See Remark
6.27
below for more details.

Proof of Proposition
2.4
.

The condition (
6.1
) holds by assumptions on
\mu
and
\eta
.
A union bound and Proposition
6.1
yield

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\exists x\neq y\in\mathcal{W}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}|\lambda(x)-\lambda(y)|\leqslant N^{-\eta})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\Omega^{c})+\binom{N}{2}\sup_{a\neq b\in[N]}\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}\big(a,b\in\mathcal{W},\lvert\lambda(a)-\lambda(b)\rvert\leqslant N^{-\eta}\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant o(1)+N^{2\mu-\eta/4+o(1)}\,,

where we used that
\mathbb{P}(\Omega^{c})=o(1)
by definition of
\Omega
(recall Definition
3.5
).
As
\eta>8\mu
, we conclude that the right-hand side is
o(1)
.
∎

6.1.  Key tools of the proof of Proposition
6.1

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
6.1
.
Throughout, we fix deterministic vertices
a\neq b\in[N]
and suppose that
\eta
satisfies (
6.1
). We use the following definitions. Let

r\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigg\lfloor\frac{\eta}{2}\,\frac{\log N}{\log d}\bigg\rfloor-1\,.

(6.3)

In particular,
d^{r+1}\leqslant N^{\eta/2}<d^{r+2}
.
Note that
r
from (
6.3
) satisfies the condition of Proposition
3.1

(ii)
and (
3.2
), i.e. the condition of Proposition
3.2
.

Figure 6.1:
An illustration of the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{i}
. Here
i=3
, and the vertex
b
is drawn in green. Conditioning on
\mathcal{F}_{i}
means that the graph is fixed in the ball
B_{i}(b)
and its complement, drawn in grey. The only randomness is the choice of the edges from
S_{i}(b)
to
B_{i}(b)^{c}
, drawn in blue. By Lemma
6.4
, these edges are chosen independently with probability
d/N
.

Definition 6.3
.

We define the
\sigma
-algebra

\mathcal{F}_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sigma(B_{i}(b),A|_{B_{i}(b)},A|_{B_{i}(b)^{c}})

for
0\leqslant i\leqslant r
, and we abbreviate
\mathcal{F}\equiv\mathcal{F}_{r}
.

More explicitly, we define
\mathcal{F}_{i}
inductively through the filtration
\mathcal{G}_{1}\subset\mathcal{G}_{2}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{G}_{i}
, where
\mathcal{G}_{1}=\sigma(A|_{\{b\}})
and
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}=\sigma(\mathcal{G}_{k},A|_{S_{k}(b)})
, since by construction
S_{k}(b)
is
\mathcal{G}_{k}
-measurable. Then we set
\mathcal{F}_{i}=\sigma(\mathcal{G}_{i},A|_{B_{i}(b)^{c}})
, using that
B_{i}(b)
, and hence
B_{i}(b)^{c}
, is
\mathcal{G}_{i}
-measurable. See Figure
6.1
for an illustration of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
. The following lemma is an immediate consequence of the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
and the independence of the family
(A_{xy})
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{i}
, the random variables
(A_{xy}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in S_{i}(b),y\in B_{i}(b)^{c})
are independent Bernoulli random variables with mean
d/N
.

Definition 6.5
.

For
0\leqslant i\leqslant r
define
H(i)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=H^{(B_{i}(b)\cup\mathcal{V}^{(B_{i}(b))})}
and
G(i,z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(H(i)-z)^{-1}
.

By definition,
H(i)
is
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-measurable.
We shall need the following regularization of the function
t\mapsto t^{-1}
.

Definition 6.6
.

Define
\iota\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
through

\iota(t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}t^{-1}&\text{if }t\in[T^{-1},T]\\
-t+T+T^{-1}&\text{otherwise}\,,\end{cases}

(6.4)

with
T\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=10\max\{\sqrt{d^{-1}\log N},\kappa^{-1}\}
.

Remark 6.7
.

The function
\iota
is an involution on
\mathbb{R}
with Lipschitz constant
T^{2}
.

Definition 6.8
.

For
x\in S_{i}(b)
we define
S_{1}^{+}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=S_{1}(x)\cap S_{i+1}(b)
.

Definition 6.9
.

For each
z\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the family
(g_{x}(z))_{x\in B_{r}(b)\setminus\{b\}}
recursively through

\begin{cases}g_{x}(z)=-\iota\Big(z+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\setminus\mathcal{V}^{(B_{r}(b))}}G_{yy}(r,z)\Big)&\text{ if }x\in S_{r}(b)\\
g_{x}(z)=-\iota\Big(z+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}g_{y}(z)\Big)&\text{ if }x\in B_{r-1}(b)\setminus\{b\}\,.\end{cases}

(6.5)

The following remark contains a crucial independence property of the family
(g_{x}(z))_{x}
.

Remark 6.10
.

Conditioned on
\mathcal{F}
,
if
\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(b)}
is a tree and
z
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable, then for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
the family
(g_{x}(z))_{x\in S_{i}(b)}
is independent.
To see this, we first note that
(g_{x}(z))_{x\in S_{r}(b)}
is independent conditioned on
\mathcal{F}
because of Lemma
6.4
.
For
i\leqslant r-1
, the statement follows inductively by using the tree structure of
\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(b)}
.

Remark 6.11
.

The function
\iota\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a regularized version of the function
t\mapsto t^{-1}
as a function
\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The regularization acts on both small values of
t
(for
t<T^{-1}
) and large values of
t
(for
t>T
). The former regularization is needed to ensure the Lipschitz continuity of the function
\iota
, which is used in the proof of Proposition
6.15
to ensure the stability of
g_{x}(z)
under change of the argument
z
. The latter regularization is needed to ensure the Lipschitz continuity of the function
\iota^{-1}
, which is used in the proof of Proposition
6.26
below to ensure that anticoncentration of a random variable is preserved, up to a factor
T^{2}
, by applying
\iota
. In the proof of Proposition
6.15
below, we show and use that with high probability the argument of
\iota
is always contained in the interval
[T^{-1},T]
where
\iota
coincides with
t\mapsto t^{-1}
. Moreover, we choose the lower and upper bounds,
T^{-1}
and
T
, to be each other’s inverses for convenience, since in that case
\iota
is an involution. Actually, an inspection of our proof shows that the lower bound
T^{-1}
could be replaced with the larger value
\kappa/10
. Finally, we note that in the critical regime
d\asymp\log N
, the parameter
T
is of order one. This observation can be used to improve Proposition
6.1
somewhat in that regime; see Remarks
6.2
and
6.27
.

We now state the three key propositions that underlie the proof of Proposition
6.1
– Propositions
6.12
,
6.14
, and
6.15
. Their proofs are postponed to Sections
6.5
,
6.3
, and
6.4
, respectively.

Using the independence from Remark
6.10
, we obtain the following anticoncentration estimate for the family
(g_{x}(z))_{x\in B_{r}(b)\setminus\{b\}}
.

Proposition 6.12

(Anticoncentration of
g_{x}
)
.

Let
z\in\mathcal{J}
be
\mathcal{F}
-measurable and
(g_{x}(z))_{x}
be defined as in Definition
6.9
. Then for any
a,b\in[N]
we have

\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}\bigg(a,b\in\mathcal{W},\,\biggl\lvert\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x\in S_{1}(b)}g_{x}(z)+z\biggr\rvert\leqslant N^{-\eta}\bigg)\leqslant N^{-2+2\mu-\eta/4+o(1)}\,.

(6.6)

For each
0\leqslant i\leqslant r
we shall need the following
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-measurable approximation of
\lambda(a)
.

Definition 6.13
.

For
i\in[r]
we abbbreviate
\lambda(a,i)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\lambda_{2}(H^{((\mathcal{V}^{(B_{i}(b))}\cup B_{i}(b))\setminus\{a\})})
.

By definition,
\lambda(a,i)
is
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-measurable. The next results states that
\lambda(a,i)
is with high probability close to
\lambda(a)
.

Proposition 6.14

(Comparison of
\lambda(a)
and
\lambda(a,i)
)
.

For any small enough
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}\bigl(a,b\in\mathcal{W},\,\exists i\in[r],\,\lvert\lambda(a)-\lambda(a,i)\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon\bigr)\leqslant\varepsilon^{-2}N^{-3+2\mu+\eta/2+o(1)}\,.

The next result states that, when choosing the spectral parameter
z=\lambda(a,r)
, the Green function entries of
H^{(\mathcal{V})}
on
S_{1}(b)
are well approximated by the family
(g_{x})
from Definition
6.9
.

Proposition 6.15

(Approximation of Green function by
g_{x}
)
.

Let
(g_{x}(z))_{x}
be defined as in Definition
6.9
with
z\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\lambda(a,r)
.
For any constant
c>0
and any
\varepsilon\leqslant N^{-c}
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}\Bigl(a,b\in\mathcal{W},\,\exists x,y\in S_{1}(b),\,|(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-z)^{-1}_{xy}-g_{x}(z)\mathbbm{1}_{x=y}|\geqslant\varepsilon\Bigr)\leqslant\varepsilon^{-2}N^{-3+2\mu+2\eta+o(1)}\,.

6.2.  Proof of Proposition
6.1

In this subsection we prove Proposition
6.1
. We begin by introducing the following events that we use throughout this section. Recall the definitions of the sets
\mathcal{V}^{(X)}
and
\mathcal{W}^{(X)}
from (
5.4
) and (
5.5
).

Definition 6.16
.

We define

\Xi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{a,b\in\mathcal{W}\}\,,

and,
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
,

\Xi_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{a\in\mathcal{W}^{(B_{i}(b))},b\in\mathcal{W}\}\,.

Remark 6.17
.

We record the following straightforward properties of
\Xi
and
\Xi_{i}
.

(i)

\Xi_{i}
is
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-measurable.

(ii)

\Xi_{i}\subset\Xi
(by definition of
\mathcal{W}^{(B_{i}(b))}
).

(iii)

On
\Omega
, for any
b\in\mathcal{W}
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
we have
\mathcal{V}^{(B_{i}(b))}=\mathcal{V}\setminus\{b\}
and
\mathcal{W}^{(B_{i}(b))}=\mathcal{W}\setminus\{b\}
(see Proposition
3.2

(iii)
). In particular,
\Omega\cap\Xi_{i}=\Omega\cap\Xi
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
.

Next, we state a basic result that, together with Lemma
3.8
, is used throughout this section to establish the boundedness of the Green function for certain spectral parameters.

Lemma 6.18
.

On
\Omega\cap\Xi
we have
\lambda(a,i)=\Lambda(\alpha_{a})+o(1)
and
\lambda(a,i)\in\mathcal{J}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
.

Proof.

By Remark
6.17

(iii)
, on
\Omega
the assumptions of Corollary
3.6
are satisfied for
x=a
and

X=(\mathcal{V}^{(B_{i}(b))}\cup B_{i}(b))\setminus\{a\}
.
Therefore,
\lambda(a,i)=\Lambda(\alpha_{a})+o(1)
, from which we conclude that
\lambda(a,i)\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa/4
by the definition (
2.2
) of
\mathcal{W}
as well as
\lambda(a,i)\ll\sqrt{d}
by (
3.1
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

By spectral decomposition of
H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{b\})}
, we have
\im(H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{b\})}-\lambda(b)-\mathrm{i}t)_{bb}^{-1}\geqslant\langle\mathbf{1}_{b}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(b)\rangle^{2}/t
for any
t>0
.
By Corollary
3.7
, on
\Omega
we have
\langle\mathbf{1}_{b}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(b)\rangle\neq 0
, which implies

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{(H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{b\})}-\lambda(b)-\mathrm{i}t)^{-1}_{bb}}=0\,,

and hence Schur’s complement formula yields

\lambda(b)+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x,y\in S_{1}(b)}(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-\lambda(b))_{xy}^{-1}=0\,.

Therefore, with
z=\lambda(a,r)
, we obtain from the definition of the family
(g_{x}(\lambda(a,r)))_{x}
in Definition
6.9
that

\displaystyle\biggl\lvert\lambda(a,r)+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x\in S_{1}(b)}g_{x}(\lambda(a,r))\biggr\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant|\lambda(a,r)-\lambda(b)|+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x,y\in S_{1}(b)}\bigl\lvert(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-\lambda(a,r))^{-1}_{xy}-(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-\lambda(b))^{-1}_{xy}\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x,y\in S_{1}(b)}|g_{x}(\lambda(a,r))\mathbbm{1}_{x=y}-(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-\lambda(a,r))^{-1}_{xy}|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\kappa^{-2}(\log N)^{2}(|\lambda(a)-\lambda(b)|+|\lambda(a,r)-\lambda(a)|)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x,y\in S_{1}(b)}|g_{x}(\lambda(a,r))\mathbbm{1}_{x=y}-(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-\lambda(a,r))^{-1}_{xy}|

on the event
\Omega
, where we used (
3.5
) and Proposition
3.2

(i)
for the second inequality. Here
C
is some positive constant.
Thus, for any
\gamma\geqslant 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mspace{-20mu}\mathbb{P}_{\Omega\cap\Xi}(C\kappa^{-2}(\log N)^{2}|\lambda(b)-\lambda(a)|\leqslant\gamma)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}_{\Omega\cap\Xi}\bigg(\biggl\lvert\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x\in S_{1}(b)}g_{x}(\lambda(a,r))+\lambda(a,r)\biggr\rvert\leqslant 3\gamma\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{P}_{\Omega\cap\Xi}\big(C\kappa^{-2}(\log N)^{2}|\lambda(a)-\lambda(a,r)|\geqslant\gamma\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{P}_{\Omega\cap\Xi}\bigg(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x,y\in S_{1}(b)}\Bigl\lvert(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-\lambda(a,r))^{-1}_{xy}-g_{x}(\lambda(a,r))\mathbbm{1}_{x=y}\Bigr\rvert\geqslant\gamma\bigg)\,.

Now Proposition
6.1
follows with the choice
\gamma\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=N^{-\eta}/3
, applying Proposition
6.12
to the first line, Proposition
6.14
to the second line, and Proposition
6.15
combined with Proposition
3.2

(i)
on
\Omega
to the third line. Here we used Lemma
6.18
to ensure that
\lambda(a,i)\in\mathcal{J}
.
∎

6.3.  Proof of Proposition
6.14

In this subsection we prove Proposition
6.14
.

Proof of Proposition
6.14
.

We follow the proof of Proposition
5.3
.
Let
\mathbf{u}(a,i)
be a normalized eigenvector of
H^{((\mathcal{V}^{(B_{i}(b))}\cup B_{i}(b))\setminus\{a\})}
associated with the
eigenvalue
\lambda(a,i)
.
Then, as
\supp\mathbf{u}(a,i)\subset(\mathcal{V}^{(B_{i}(b))}\cup B_{i}(b))^{c}\cup\{a\}
,
on the event
\Omega\cap\{a,b\in\mathcal{W}\}
, using Remark
6.17

(iii)
we obtain

(H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus{\{a\}})}-\lambda(a,i))\mathbf{u}(a,i)=(H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{a\})}-H^{((\mathcal{V}^{(B_{i}(b))}\cup B_{i}(b))\setminus\{a\})})\mathbf{u}(a,i)=\sum_{y\in S_{i}({b})}\varepsilon_{ay}(i)\mathbf{1}_{y}\,,

(6.7)

where
\varepsilon_{ay}(i)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{v\in S_{1}^{+}(y)}\langle\mathbf{1}_{v}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(a,i)\rangle
.
From Lemma
6.4
we conclude, using Cauchy-Schwarz,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}\Bigg[\sum_{y\in S_{i}({b})}(\varepsilon_{ay}(i))^{2}\,\bigg|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{y\in S_{i}(b)}\frac{\log N}{d}\sum_{v\in B_{i}(b)^{c}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbbm{1}_{v\in S_{1}^{+}(y)}\langle\mathbf{1}_{v}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(a,i)\rangle^{2}\,\big|\,\mathcal{F}_{i}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in S_{i}(b)}\frac{\log N}{d}\sum_{v\in B_{i}(b)^{c}}\mathbb{P}(v\in S_{1}^{+}(y)\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i})\,\langle\mathbf{1}_{v}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{u}(a,i)\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\log N}{N}\lvert S_{i}(b)\rvert\,,

(6.8)

where we used that on
\Omega
we have
\lvert S_{1}^{+}(y)\rvert\lesssim\log N
, that
\mathbf{u}(a,i)
is
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-measurable, and that by Lemma
6.4
we have
\mathbb{P}(v\in S_{1}^{+}(y)\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i})=\frac{d}{N}
for any
y\in S_{i}(b)
and
v\notin B_{i}(b)
.

By the definition (
2.2
) of
\mathcal{W}
, we have
\Lambda(\alpha_{a})\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa/2
, and on
\Omega
we have
\Lambda(\alpha_{a})\ll\sqrt{d}
(recall (
3.1
)).
Hence, by Corollary
3.6
, for any small enough
\varepsilon>0
, if there are a scalar
\widehat{\lambda}
and a normalized vector
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}
such that,
for some
a\in\mathcal{W}
,

\lVert(H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{a\})}-\widehat{\lambda})\widehat{\mathbf{u}}\rVert\leqslant\varepsilon
and

\widehat{\lambda}=\Lambda(\alpha_{a})+o(1)
, then

\lvert\lambda(a)-\widehat{\lambda}\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon
(as
\lambda(a)=\lambda_{2}(H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{a\})})
).

We apply this observation to the choices
\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda(a,i)
and
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}=\mathbf{u}(a,i)
, for which Lemma
6.18
yields
\lambda(a,i)=\Lambda(\alpha_{a})+o(1)
.
Hence, for any small enough
\varepsilon>0
, we have
\lvert\lambda(a)-\lambda(a,i)\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon
provided that

\lVert(H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{a\})}-\lambda(a,i))\mathbf{u}(a,i)\rVert\leqslant\varepsilon
.
We can then estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(a,b\in\mathcal{W},\,|\lambda(a)-\lambda(a,i)|>\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Xi_{i}\cap\Bigl\{\bigl\lVert(H^{(\mathcal{V}\setminus{\{a\}})}-\lambda(a,i))\mathbf{u}(a,i)\bigr\rVert^{2}>\varepsilon^{2}\Bigr\}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{-2}\,\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\Xi_{i}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lvert S_{i}(b)\rvert\leqslant N^{\eta/2+o(1)}}\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}\Bigg[\sum_{y\in S_{i}({b})}(\varepsilon_{ay}(i))^{2}\,\bigg|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\Bigg]\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{-2}\,\mathbb{P}(\Xi_{i})\,\frac{\log N}{N}N^{\eta/2+o(1)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{-2}\,N^{2\mu+\eta/2-3+o(1)}\,,

where in the first step we used Remark
6.17

(iii)
, in the second step (
6.7
), Remark
6.17

(i)
, and the estimate
\lvert S_{i}(b)\rvert\leqslant N^{\eta/2+o(1)}
on
\Omega
by Proposition
3.2

(ii)
and (
6.3
), in the third step (
6.8
), and the fourth step Remark
6.17

(ii)
and Lemma
6.19
below.
Now Proposition
6.14
follows from a union bound over
i\in[r]
with
r\lesssim\log N
.
∎

The following result is used throughout the rest of this section.

Lemma 6.19
.

For any
a\neq b\in[N]
we have
\mathbb{P}(a,b\in\mathcal{W})\leqslant N^{-2+2\mu}
.

Proof.

Since
0\leqslant\Lambda^{\prime}(\alpha)=\frac{\alpha-2}{2(\alpha-1)^{3/2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
for all
\alpha\geqslant 2
, we have

\displaystyle P(a,b\in\mathcal{W})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\Big(\min\{|S_{1}(a)|,|S_{1}(b)|\}\geqslant(\alpha^{*}+\kappa)d\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbb{P}\Big(\min\{|S_{1}(a)|,|S_{1}(b)|\}\geqslant(\alpha^{*}+\kappa)d\,\Big|\,A_{ab}\Big)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\big(|S_{1}(a)|\geqslant(\alpha^{*}+\kappa)d-1\big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\big(\lvert S_{1}(a)\rvert\geqslant\alpha^{*}d\big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant N^{-2+2\mu}\,,

where we used in the third step that conditionally on
A_{ab}
,
S_{1}(a)
and
S_{1}(b)
are independent and in the last step the definition of
\alpha^{*}
.
∎

6.4.  Proof of Proposition
6.15

This subsection is devoted to the proof of Proposition
6.4
. We begin with the following result, which contains two estimates. The first one is an approximate version of Schur’s complement formula, where
G(i-1,z)
is related to
G(i,z)
at the cost of an error term; this amounts to removing not just the vertex
x\in S_{i}(b)
at which the Green function is evaluated, but the entire ball
B_{i}(b)
. The second estimate provides an upper bound on the off-diagonal entries of the Green function.

Lemma 6.20
.

(i)

For
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
let
z_{i}\in\mathcal{J}
be
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-measurable and
x\in S_{i}(b)
. Then

-\frac{1}{G_{xx}(i-1,z_{i})}=z_{i}+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}G_{yy}(i,z_{i})+\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)

(6.9)

where the error term
\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)
satisfies, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)}}\right|>\varepsilon\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i}}}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{b\in\mathcal{W}}\lesssim d^{2}\kappa^{-4}(\log N)^{2}\lvert S_{i}(b)\rvert^{2}N^{-1}\varepsilon^{-2}\,.

(6.10)

(ii)

Let
z\in\mathcal{J}
be
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-measurable. For any
x\neq y\in[N]
and
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}\big(\lvert(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-z)^{-1}_{xy}\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{1}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{b\in\mathcal{W}}\mathbbm{1}_{x,y\in S_{1}(b)}\lesssim\kappa^{-4}(\log N)N^{-1}\varepsilon^{-2}\,.

(6.11)

Proof of Proposition
6.15
.

We choose
z_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\lambda(a,i)
, which is
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-measurable, and set
z=z_{r}
.
For
\varepsilon>0
we introduce the event
\Theta\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Theta_{1}\cap\Theta_{2}\cap\Theta_{3}
given by

\displaystyle\Theta_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\forall i\leqslant r,\forall x\in S_{i}(b),|\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)|\leqslant\varepsilon\}\,,

\displaystyle\Theta_{2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\forall i\leqslant r,|z-z_{i}|\leqslant\varepsilon\}\,,

\displaystyle\Theta_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\forall x\neq y\in S_{1}(b),\lvert(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-z_{1})^{-1}_{xy}\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon\}\,,

with
\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)
defined as in Lemma
6.20
. We estimate the probability of
\Theta_{1}^{c}
using
\Xi_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{i}
, Lemmas
6.20

(i)
and
6.19
, Proposition
3.2

(ii)
, we well as Remark
6.17
as

\mathbb{P}_{\Omega\cap\Xi_{i}}(\exists x\in S_{i}(b),|\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)|>\varepsilon)\leqslant\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(\Xi)N^{-1+3\eta/2+o(1)}\varepsilon^{-2}\leqslant N^{-3+2\mu+3\eta/2+o(1)}\varepsilon^{-2}\,.

Similarly, we find using Lemma
6.20

(ii)
that

\mathbb{P}_{\Omega\cap\Xi_{1}}(\Theta_{3}^{c})\leqslant\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(\Xi)N^{-1+\eta+o(1)}\varepsilon^{-2}\leqslant N^{-3+2\mu+\eta/2+o(1)}\varepsilon^{-2}\,.

Hence, using Remark
6.17
and Proposition
6.14
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(\Theta^{c}\cap\Xi)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{r}\bigg(\mathbb{P}_{\Omega\cap\Xi_{i}}(\exists x\in S_{i}(b),|\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)|>\varepsilon)+\mathbb{P}_{\Omega\cap\Xi}(|z-z_{i}|>\varepsilon)\bigg)+\mathbb{P}_{\Omega\cap\Xi_{1}}\big(\Theta_{3}^{c}\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant N^{-3+2\mu+2\eta+o(1)}\varepsilon^{-2}\,.

(6.12)

We shall show below that there is a constant
C>0
such that on the event
\Omega\cap\Theta\cap\Xi
we have

|g_{x}(z)-G_{xx}(i-1,z)|\leqslant C\varepsilon\sum_{j=0}^{r-i+1}(C\kappa^{-4}d^{-1})^{j}|S_{j}^{+}(x)|\leqslant N^{o(1)}\varepsilon

(6.13)

for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
and all
x\in S_{i}(b)
.
The second inequality in (
6.13
) follows from
r\ll\log N
and
d^{-j}|S_{j}^{+}(x)|\leqslant d^{-j}|B_{j}(x)|\lesssim\log N
for all
x\in[N]
by Proposition
3.2

(ii)
.

Before proving (
6.13
), we conclude Proposition
6.15

from (
6.13
) (after renaming
\varepsilon\mapsto N^{-o(1)}\varepsilon
) with
i=1
, the definition of
\Theta_{3}

and (
6.12
),
where we used that
G(0,z)=(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-z)^{-1}
on
\Omega\cap\{b\in\mathcal{W}\}
as
\mathcal{V}^{(B_{i}(b))}=\mathcal{V}\setminus\{b\}
.

What remains, therefore, is the proof of (
6.13
). We prove it by inductively decreasing
i
starting from
i=r+1
.
By convention, for any
x\in S_{r+1}(b)
we denote
g_{x}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=G_{xx}(r,z)
, so that (
6.13
) trivially holds for
i=r+1
.
Therefore, we can assume throughout the following argument that
g_{x}(z)
is defined by the second case in Definition
6.9
for all
x\in B_{r}(b)\setminus\{b\}
(note that on the event
\Omega\cap\Xi
we have
S_{1}^{+}(x)\setminus\mathcal{V}^{(B_{r}(b))}=S_{1}^{+}(x)
for any
x\in S_{r}(b)
, by Proposition
3.2

(iii)
).

To verify the induction step, we assume that (
6.13
) holds on
S_{j}(b)
for all
i+1\leqslant j\leqslant r+1
and consider
x\in S_{i}(b)
. We first show that on
\Omega\cap\Xi\cap\Theta

g_{x}(z)=-\bigg(z+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}g_{y}(z)\bigg)^{-1}\,.

(6.14)

To that end, we conclude from the induction hypothesis, (
3.5
), the definition of
\Theta_{2}

and Proposition
3.2

(i)

that

\displaystyle\biggl\lvert\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}G_{yy}(i,z_{i})-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}g_{y}(z)\biggr\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|G_{yy}(i,z)-g_{y}(z)|+|G_{yy}(i,z_{i})-G_{yy}(i,z)|}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant d^{-1}\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert\bigg(\max_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}|G_{yy}(i,z)-g_{y}(z)|+(8/\kappa)^{2}|z-z_{i}|\bigg)

\displaystyle\leqslant N^{o(1)}\varepsilon\,.

(6.15)

By Lemma
6.18
, on
\Omega\cap\Xi
we have
z_{i}\in\mathcal{J}
.
We recall from Lemma
3.9
that all Green function entries
G_{xx}(i-1,z_{i})
and
G_{yy}(i,z_{i})
are negative for
z_{i}\in\mathcal{J}
. We use the upper bound (
3.4
) for
-G_{xx}(i-1,z_{i})
as well as (
6.9
) to obtain, on
\Omega\cap\Xi\cap\Theta_{1}\cap\Theta_{2}
,

\frac{10}{9T}\leqslant\frac{\kappa}{9}\leqslant-\frac{1}{G_{xx}(i-1,z_{i})}-\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)=z_{i}+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}G_{yy}(i,z_{i})\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha_{a})+o(1)\leqslant\sqrt{2\alpha_{a}}+o(1)\leqslant\frac{T}{2}\,.

(6.16)

Here, we also used Corollary
3.6
, which is applicable as

\mathcal{V}^{(B_{i}(b))}=\mathcal{V}\setminus\{b\}
on
\Omega\cap\{b\in\mathcal{W}\}
by Proposition
3.2

(iii)
,

\alpha_{a}\leqslant 10d^{-1}\log N
on
\Omega
by Proposition
3.2

(i)
, and the definitions of
\Lambda
and
T
.
Then, by the assumption
\varepsilon\leqslant N^{-c}
, we find that (
6.16
) and (
6.15
)
imply
T^{-1}\leqslant z+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}g_{y}(z)\leqslant T
, which yields
(
6.14
) by the definitions of
\iota
and
g_{x}(z)
in (
6.4
)
and Definition
6.9
, respectively. This concludes the proof of (
6.14
).

Finally, we find on
\Omega\cap\Xi\cap\Theta

\displaystyle|g_{x}(z)-G_{xx}(i-1,z)|

\displaystyle\leqslant g_{x}(z)G_{xx}(i-1,z_{i})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|g_{x}(z)^{-1}-G_{xx}(i-1,z_{i})^{-1}}}\right|+(8/\kappa)^{2}|z-z_{i}|

\displaystyle\leqslant(8/\kappa)^{2}\bigg(\biggl\lvert\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}G_{yy}(i,z_{i})-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}g_{y}(z)\biggr\rvert+|z-z_{i}|+\lvert{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(x)\rvert\bigg)

\displaystyle\leqslant(8/\kappa)^{2}\bigg(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}\sum_{j=0}^{r-i}(C\kappa^{-4}d^{-1})^{j}|S_{j}^{+}(y)|C\varepsilon+((8/\kappa)^{2}d^{-1}|S_{1}^{+}(x)|+1)|z-z_{i}|+\lvert{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(x)\rvert\bigg)

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{-1}\bigg(64\sum_{j=1}^{r-i+1}(C\kappa^{-4}d^{-1})^{j}|S_{j}^{+}(x)|+64^{2}\kappa^{-4}(d^{-1}|S_{1}^{+}(x)|+2)\bigg)C\varepsilon\,.

Here we used (
3.5
)
in the first inequality,
(
6.9
) and (
6.14
) in the second, (
3.4
) and the iteration hypothesis in the third and that
|S_{j+1}^{+}(x)|=\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}|S_{j}^{+}(y)|
and the definitions of
\Theta_{1}
and
\Theta_{2}
in the last one. We conclude
(
6.13
) for large enough
C
.
∎

What remains is the proof of Lemma
6.20
.

Proof of Lemma
6.20
.

We begin with
(i)
.
Throughout the proof, we fix
i
and we condition on
\mathcal{F}_{i}
.
We always assume that
b\in\mathcal{W}
, which is an
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-measurable event since
i\geqslant 1
.

We start by observing that the event

\Gamma_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\mathcal{V}^{(B_{i-1}(b))}=\mathcal{V}^{(B_{i}(b))}\}\cap\{A|_{S_{i}(b)}=0\}

satisfies
\Omega\subset\Gamma_{i}
, by Proposition
3.2

(ii)
,
(iii)
. See Figure

for an illustration of
\Gamma_{i}
.

Figure 6.2:
An illustration of the event
\Gamma_{i}
for

. The vertices of

For the proof we abbreviate

(6.17)

so that on the event
\Gamma_{i}
we have
H(i-1)=H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i})}
.
From Schur’s complement formula we get on the event
\Gamma_{i}

-\frac{1}{G_{xx}(i-1,z_{i})}=z_{i}+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u,v\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{x\})}-z_{i})^{-1}_{uv}\,,

(6.18)

where we used that
x
has no neighbours in
S_{i}(b)
by definition of
\Gamma_{i}
.

We now decompose the error term
\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)
from (
6.9
) into several summands estimated separately.
To that end, let
\{x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{\lvert S_{i}(b)\rvert-1}\}=S_{i}(b)
be an enumeration of
S_{i}(b)
.
We set
y_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=x
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u,v\in S_{1}^{+}(x),\,u\neq v}\big(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{x\})}-z_{i}\big)^{-1}_{uv},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(j)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}\Big(\big(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}\})}-z_{i}\big)^{-1}_{uu}-\big(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j-1}\})}-z_{i}\big)^{-1}_{uu}\Big)\,.

for

.
Then, from (
6.18
) we conclude that (

) holds with

\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(0)}-\sum_{j=1}^{\lvert S_{i}(b)\rvert-1}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(j)}\,.

Chebyshev’s and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities yield

\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(\lvert\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)\rvert>\varepsilon\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i})\leqslant\varepsilon^{-2}\,\lvert S_{i}(b)\rvert\bigg(\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[\lvert\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(0)}\rvert^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i}]+\sum_{j=1}^{\lvert S_{i}(b)\rvert-1}\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[\lvert\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(j)}\rvert^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i}]\bigg)\,.

(6.19)

Therefore, it remains to estimate
\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[\lvert\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(j)}\rvert^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i}]
for all
j=0,\ldots,\lvert S_{i}(b)\rvert-1
.
To that end, we introduce a
\sigma
-algebra refining
\mathcal{F}_{i}
from Definition
6.3
.
For any subset
X\subset S_{i}(b)
we define the
\sigma
-algebra
12
12

12

Somewhat more carefully (since the set
X
is random), the precise definition of
\mathcal{F}_{i}(X)
is as follows. As the underlying probability space is finite, any
\sigma
-algebra, in particular
\mathcal{F}_{i}
, is atomic. Conditioning on
\mathcal{F}_{i}
means that we restrict ourselves to a single atom
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
. On this atom
S_{i}(b)
and
X
are deterministic. Then
\mathcal{F}_{i}(X)
is by definition the smallest
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathcal{A}
such that
S_{1}^{+}(y)
is measurable for all
y\in S_{i}(b)\setminus X
.

\mathcal{F}_{i}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sigma\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{i},(S_{1}^{+}(y))_{y\in S_{i}(b)\setminus X}\bigr)\,,

using that
S_{i}(b)
is
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-measurable.
See Figure
6.3
for an illustration of
\mathcal{F}_{i}(X)
.
Moreover, for
X\subset S_{i}(b)
we define the event

\Delta_{i}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Bigl\{\bigl\lVert(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup X)}-z_{i})^{-1}\bigr\rVert\leqslant 8\kappa^{-1}\Bigr\}\,.

We note that for any
X\subset S_{i}(b)
, the event
\Delta_{i}(X)
lies in
\mathcal{F}_{i}(X)
, since
H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup X)}
is
\mathcal{F}_{i}(X)
-measurable (see the definition (
6.17
) and Figure
6.3
).
Furthermore, by Lemma
3.8
for
z_{i}\in\mathcal{J}
we have

\Omega\subset\bigcap_{X\subset S_{i}(b)}\Delta_{i}(X)\,.

(6.20)

Figure 6.3:
An illustration of the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{i}(X)
. Here
i=3
, the vertex
b
is drawn in green, and the set
X\subset S_{i}(b)
is drawn in blue. Conditioning on
\mathcal{F}_{i}(X)
means that we fix all edges within
B_{i}(b)
, within
B_{i}(b)^{c}
, and connecting
S_{i}(b)\setminus X
with
B_{i}(b)^{c}
. The only randomness is the choice of the edges from
X
to
B_{i}(b)^{c}
, drawn in blue. After removal of the vertices in
\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup X
(see (
6.17
)), only black edges and edges within

remain, which shows that

is
\mathcal{F}_{i}(X)
-measurable. Note that for

we have
\mathcal{F}_{i}(X)=\mathcal{F}_{i}
, and we recover the illustration from Figure
