.

For the estimate of
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(0)}
, we introduce the sets

\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{(u,v)\in(B_{i}(b)^{c})^{2}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}u\neq v\}
and
Q\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{Q}\cap(S_{1}^{+}(x))^{2}
and
the family
(Z_{q})_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}
defined by

Z_{(u,v)}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{x\})}-z_{i})^{-1}_{uv}
.
We first note that, for any
q=(u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(q\in Q\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i}(\{x\}))=\mathbb{P}(u\in S_{1}^{+}(x),v\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i})=\frac{d^{2}}{N^{2}}\,.

(6.21)

In the last step we used that, by Lemma
6.4
, for any
X\subset S_{i}(b)
, conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{i}(X)
, the random variables
(A_{xy}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in X,y\in B_{i}(b)^{c})
are independent Bernoulli-
\frac{d}{N}
random variables. Moreover, we get

\sum_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\lvert Z_{q}\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{i}(\{x\})}\leqslant\operatorname{Tr}((H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{x\})}-z_{i})^{-2})\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{i}(\{x\})}\lesssim N\kappa^{-2}\,.

(6.22)

Since
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(0)}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{q\in Q}Z_{q}
, the inclusion
\Omega\subset\Gamma_{i}\cap\Delta_{i}(\{x\})
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality imply

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}\big[\lvert\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(0)}\rvert^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{i}(\{x\})\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{d^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}\bigg[\lvert Q\rvert\sum_{q\in Q}\lvert Z_{q}\rvert^{2}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{i}(\{x\})\bigg]

(6.23)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{(\log N)^{2}}{d^{2}}\,\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{q\in Q}\lvert Z_{q}\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{i}(\{x\})}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{i}(\{x\})\bigg]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{(\log N)^{2}}{d^{2}}\max_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{P}(q\in Q\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i}(\{x\}))\sum_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\lvert Z_{q}\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{i}(\{x\})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{(\log N)^{2}}{\kappa^{2}N}\,,

where we used that
\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert\lesssim\log N
on
\Omega
, by Proposition
3.2

(i)
, as well
as the
\mathcal{F}_{i}(\{x\})
-measurability of
\lvert Z_{q}\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{i}(\{x\})}
.
The last step follows from (
6.21
) and (
6.22
).

To bound
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(j)}
for a fixed
j\in\{1,\ldots,\lvert S_{i}(b)\rvert-1\}
, we conclude
on the event
\Gamma_{i}
from the resolvent identity that

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(j)}=\frac{1}{d^{3/2}}\sum_{u\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}\big(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j-1}\})}-z_{i}\big)^{-1}_{uy_{j}}\sum_{v\in S_{1}^{+}(y_{j})}\big(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}\})}-z_{i}\big)^{-1}_{vu}\,.

Therefore, by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality twice and using (
6.20
) with
X=\{y_{0},\dots,y_{j}\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(j)}\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\frac{\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\rvert}{d^{3}}\sum_{u\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}\lvert(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j-1}\})}-z_{i})^{-1}_{uy_{j}}\rvert^{2}\biggl\lvert\sum_{v\in S_{1}^{+}(y_{j})}(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}\})}-z_{i})^{-1}_{vu}\biggr\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\frac{\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\rvert\lvert S_{1}^{+}(y_{j})\rvert}{d^{3}\kappa^{2}}\sum_{u\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}\lvert(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j-1}\})}-z_{i})^{-1}_{uy_{j}}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\frac{(\log N)^{2}}{d^{3}\kappa^{2}}\sum_{u\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}\lvert(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j-1}\})}-z_{i})^{-1}_{uy_{j}}\rvert^{2}\,,

(6.24)

where in the last step we used
\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert+\lvert S_{1}(y_{j})\rvert\lesssim\log N
from
Proposition
3.2

(i)
on
\Omega
.

We now set
Y_{u}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{i}\cup\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j-1}\})}-z_{i})^{-1}_{uy_{j}}
for
u\in B_{i}(b)^{c}
,

U\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=S_{1}^{+}(x)
and
\mathcal{U}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=B_{i}(b)^{c}
.
We apply
\mathbb{E}[\,\cdot\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i}(\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j-1}\})]
to (
6.24
)
and, similarly as in (
6.23
), obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[\lvert\mathcal{E}_{i}^{(j)}\rvert^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i}(\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j-1}\})]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{(\log N)^{2}}{d^{3}\kappa^{2}}\max_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{P}(u\in U\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i}(\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j-1}\}))\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\lvert Y_{u}\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{i}(\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j-1}\})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{(\log N)^{2}}{d^{2}\kappa^{4}N},

(6.25)

where we used that
(Y_{u})_{u\in\mathcal{U}}
is
\mathcal{F}_{i}(\{y_{0},\dots,y_{j-1}\})
-measurable, and that
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\lvert Y_{u}\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{i}(\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j-1}\})}=\lVert(Y_{u})_{u\in B_{i}(b)^{c}}\rVert^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{i}(\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{j-1}\})}\lesssim\kappa^{-2}
. We also used the remark following (
6.21
).

Finally, using the estimates (
6.23
) and (
6.25
)
in (
6.19
) together with the
tower property of the conditional expectation complete the proof of (
6.9
)
and (
6.10
). This concludes the proof of
(i)
.

Next, we prove
(ii)
.
For the proof of (
6.11
), we fix
x,y\in S_{1}(b)
and conclude from the resolvent identity

(
BenyachKnowles2017
, eq. (3.5))
that

\displaystyle(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-z)^{-1}_{xy}

\displaystyle=-(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-z)^{-1}_{xx}\sum_{u\notin\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\}}H_{xu}(H^{(\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\})}-z)^{-1}_{uy}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-z)^{-1}_{xx}\sum_{u\notin\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\}}\mathbbm{1}_{u\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}(H^{(\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\})}-z)^{-1}_{uy}\,.

Therefore, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Lemma
3.8
imply

\lvert(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-z)^{-1}_{xy}\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\lesssim\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\frac{\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\rvert}{\kappa^{2}d}\sum_{u\notin\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\{x\}}\mathbbm{1}_{u\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}\lvert(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\{x\})}-z)^{-1}_{uy}\rvert^{2}\,,

where we also used that

\mathcal{V}=\{b\}\cup\mathcal{V}^{(B_{1}(b))}=\mathcal{T}_{1}
on
\Omega
by Proposition
3.2

(iii)
.
Hence, since
\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert\lesssim\log N
on
\Omega
by Proposition
3.2

(i)
,
\Omega\subset\Delta_{1}(\{x\})
and
\Delta_{1}(\{x\})\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(\{x\})
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\big[\lvert(H^{(\mathcal{V})}-z)^{-1}_{xy}\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{1}(\{x\})\big]\\
\lesssim\frac{\log N}{\kappa^{2}d}\max_{u\notin\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\{x\}}\mathbb{P}(u\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{1}(\{x\}))\sum_{u\notin\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\{x\}}\lvert(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\{x\})}-z)^{-1}_{uy}\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{1}(\{x\})}\lesssim\frac{\log N}{\kappa^{4}N}\,.

Here, in the last step, we used
\mathbb{P}(u\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{1}(\{x\}))\leqslant d/N
and
\sum_{u\notin\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\{x\}}\lvert(H^{(\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\{x\})}-z)^{-1}_{uy}\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{1}(\{x\})}\lesssim\kappa^{-2}
.
Thus, Chebyshev’s inequality and the tower property of the conditional expectation complete the proof of (
6.11
) and,
therefore, the one of Lemma
6.20
.
∎

6.5.  Proof of Proposition
6.12

This subsection is devoted to the proof of Proposition
6.12
. We start by introducing the notion of a robust vertex.

Figure 6.4:
An illustration of the set
\mathcal{R}\equiv\mathcal{R}_{r}(\mathbb{G},b)
of robust vertices. Here
r=3
and
d=4
. We draw the ball
B_{r}(b)
around the root
b
. The robust vertices are drawn in blue and the non-robust vertices in white. In this example, the root
b
is robust.

Definition 6.21
.

Let
b\in[N]
and
r\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
We call a vertex
y\in B_{r}(b)

robust
if

(a)

y\in S_{r}(b)
or

(b)

y\in B_{r-1}(b)
and at least
d/2
vertices in
S_{1}^{+}(y)
are robust.

We denote by
\mathcal{R}\equiv\mathcal{R}_{r}(\mathbb{G},b)\subset B_{r}(b)
the set of robust vertices.

Note that
\mathcal{R}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-measurable random set. See Figure
6.4
for an illustration of Definition
6.21
. The following result states that with high probability the root
b
is robust, conditioned on
S_{1}(a)
and
S_{1}(b)
.

Proposition 6.22

(The root is robust)
.

Suppose that
\sqrt{\log N}\ll d\lesssim\log N
and that
r
satisfies (
3.2
). Then
\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(b\notin\mathcal{R}\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))\lesssim N^{-1/2}
whenever
a,b\in\mathcal{W}
.

The proof of Proposition
6.22
is given at the end of this subsection. From now on, we choose
r
as in (
6.3
).

Definition 6.23
.

Let
z\in\mathcal{J}
be an
\mathcal{F}
-measurable real random variable.
We introduce the event
\Upsilon
on which the following conditions hold.

(A)

\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(b)}
is a tree.

(B)

b\in\mathcal{R}
.

(C)

-G_{yy}(r,z)\geqslant(3z)^{-1}
for all
y\in(B_{r}(b)\cup\mathcal{V}^{(B_{r}(b))})^{c}
.

(D)

\lvert B_{r}(b)\cup\mathcal{V}^{(B_{r}(b))}\rvert\leqslant N^{1/2}
.

Lemma 6.24
.

We have
\Upsilon\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(\Upsilon^{c}\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))=O(N^{-1/2})
whenever
a,b\in\mathcal{W}
.

Proof.

That
\Upsilon\in\mathcal{F}
follows from the Definitions
6.5
and
6.21
. The estimate follows from Proposition
6.22
and the facts that on
\Omega
the conditions
(A)
,
(C)
, and
(D)
hold surely. That
(C)
holds surely on
\Omega
follows from Lemma
3.9
and the observation that
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}^{(B_{r}(b))}
on
\Omega
(see Proposition
3.2

(iii)
). That
(D)
holds surely on
\Omega
follows from the statements
(i)
,
(i)
,
(ii)
of Proposition
3.2
(recall the choice (
6.3
)).
∎

We recall the definition of Lévy’s concentration function
Q(X,L)
from (
2.13
).

Remark 6.25
.

The concentration function has the following obvious properties.

(i)

For any
u>0
we have
Q(uX,uL)=Q(X,L)
. More generally, if
f
is a continuous bijection on
\mathbb{R}
such that
f^{-1}
is
K
-Lipschitz, then
Q(f(X),L)\leqslant Q(X,KL)
.

(ii)

If
X
and
Y
are independent then
Q(X+Y,L)\leqslant\min\{Q(X,L),Q(Y,L)\}
.

Property
(ii)
is in general not sharp, and in some situations it can be improved considerably; see Proposition
2.8
. Nevertheless, in some situations

(ii)
gives a better bound than Proposition
2.8
; this is due to the minimum in
(ii)
as opposed to the maximum in Proposition
2.8
. An important theme in the proof of Proposition
6.12
is a judicious mix of
(ii)
and Proposition
2.8
. How to do this mix is encoded by the set of robust vertices from Definition
6.21
.

We denote by
Q^{\mathcal{F}}
Lévy’s concentration function with respect to the probability measure
\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\,|\,\mathcal{F})
. The main tool behind the proof of Proposition
6.12
is the following anticoncentration estimate for
g_{x}(z)
.

Proposition 6.26
.

Let
z\in\mathcal{J}
be a
\mathcal{F}
-measurable real random variable.
There exists a constant
\chi>0
such that, on the event
\Upsilon
, for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
and
x\in S_{i}(b)\cap\mathcal{R}
we have

\displaystyle Q^{\mathcal{F}}\bigg(g_{x}(z),\frac{1}{8z(T^{2}d)^{r-i+1}}\bigg)\leqslant\frac{1}{2(\chi d)^{(r-i)/2}}\,.

Before proving Proposition
6.26
, we use it to conclude the proof of Proposition
6.12
.

Proof of Proposition
6.12
.

We estimate

\displaystyle\mspace{-20mu}\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}\bigg(a,b\in\mathcal{W},\biggl\lvert\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x\in S_{1}(b)}g_{x}(z)+z\biggr\rvert\leqslant N^{-\eta}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}\bigg(\Xi_{r}\cap\Upsilon\cap\biggl\{\biggl\lvert\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x\in S_{1}(b)}g_{x}(z)+z\biggr\rvert\leqslant N^{-\eta}\biggr\}\bigg)+\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(\Xi\cap\Upsilon^{c})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\mathbbm{1}_{\Xi_{r}}\,\mathbbm{1}_{\Upsilon}\,\mathbb{P}\biggl(\biggl\lvert\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x\in S_{1}(b)}g_{x}(z)+z\biggr\rvert\leqslant N^{-\eta}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}\biggr)\biggr]+\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(\Xi\cap\Upsilon^{c})\,,

(6.26)

where we used Remark
6.17

(i)
and
(iii)
as well as
\Upsilon\in\mathcal{F}
by Lemma
6.24
. The second term on the right-hand side of (
6.26
) is estimated as

\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(\Xi\cap\Upsilon^{c})=\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{a\in\mathcal{W}}\,\mathbbm{1}_{b\in\mathcal{W}}\,\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(\Upsilon^{c}\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))]\lesssim N^{-1/2}\,\mathbb{P}(a,b\in\mathcal{W})\,,

by Lemma
6.24
.

To estimate the first term on the right-hand side of (
6.26
), we use Proposition
6.26
with
i=1
and the estimate

8zd(T^{2}d)^{r}\leqslant d^{2r+3/2}\leqslant N^{\eta}

(6.27)

where we used the definitions of
r
and
T
from (
6.3
) and Definition
6.6
, as well as
d\gg\sqrt{\log N}
. On
\Upsilon
we have
b\in\mathcal{R}
and hence, by Definition
6.21
, there exists
x_{*}\in S_{1}(b)\cap\mathcal{R}
. Using that
(g_{x}(z))_{x\in S_{1}(b)}
is an independent family, on the event
\Upsilon
conditionally on
\mathcal{F}
, this yields, on the event
\Upsilon
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\biggl\lvert\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x\in S_{1}(b)}g_{x}(z)+z\biggr\rvert\leqslant N^{-\eta}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leqslant Q^{\mathcal{F}}\biggl(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x\in S_{1}(b)}g_{x}(z)\,,\,\frac{1}{8zd(T^{2}d)^{r}}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leqslant Q^{\mathcal{F}}\biggl(\frac{1}{d}\,g_{x_{*}}(z)\,,\,\frac{1}{8zd(T^{2}d)^{r}}\biggr)

\displaystyle=Q^{\mathcal{F}}\biggl(g_{x_{*}}(z)\,,\,\frac{1}{8z(T^{2}d)^{r}}\biggr)\,,

where in the second step we used Remark
6.25

(ii)
and in the last step Remark
6.25

(i)
. From Proposition
6.26
we therefore conclude that the first term on the right-hand side of (
6.26
) is bounded by

\frac{1}{2(\chi d)^{(r-i)/2}}\,\mathbb{P}(\Xi_{r})\leqslant N^{-\eta/4+o(1)}\,\mathbb{P}(a,b\in\mathcal{W})\,,

where we used the definition
6.3
and Remark
6.17

(ii)
.
The claim now follows from Lemma
6.19
.
∎

Remark 6.27
.

If we restrict ourselves to the critical regime
d\asymp\log N
, then the factor
N^{-\eta}
inside the probability in (
6.6
) can be improved to
N^{-\eta/2}
. To see this, we note that in this regime the parameter
T
from Definition
6.6
satisfies
T=10\kappa^{-1}
since, in the critical regime, the estimate (
6.16
) with small enough
\kappa
remains valid for this smaller choice of
T
. Thus, the estimate (
6.27
) in the proof of Proposition
6.12
can be replaced with
8zd(T^{2}d)^{r}\leqslant(Cd)^{r}
, which is bounded by
N^{\eta/2+o(1)}
.

The key tool behind the proof of Proposition
6.26
is Proposition
2.8
due to Kesten.

Proof of Proposition
6.26
.

Throughout the proof, the argument
z
of
g_{x}(z)
for any
x\in B_{r}(b)
will always be the random variable

z
from Definition
6.23
. Therefore, we omit this argument from our notation and write
g_{x}\equiv g_{x}(z)
.

For
i\in[r]
we define

L_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{8zT^{2}d}\biggl(\frac{1}{T^{2}d}\biggr)^{r-i}\,,\qquad P_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{2}\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{2}K}{d^{1/2}}\biggr)^{r-i}\,.

where
K
is the universal constant from Proposition
2.8
. We prove Proposition
6.26
by showing that, for all
i\in[r]
and
x\in S_{i}(b)\cap\mathcal{R}
we have

Q^{\mathcal{F}}\big(g_{x},L_{i}\big)\leqslant P_{i}\,.

(6.28)

We show (
6.28
) by induction on
i=r,r-1,\dots,1
.

We start the induction at
i=r
. Abbreviate
\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(B_{r}(b)\cup\mathcal{V}^{(B_{r}(b))})^{c}
, which is an
\mathcal{F}
-measurable set. For
x\in S_{r}(b)
, conditioned on
\mathcal{F}
,
(\mathbbm{1}_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)})_{y\in\mathcal{X}}
are i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables. Hence, conditioned on
\mathcal{F}
we have

\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\setminus\mathcal{V}^{(B_{r}(b))}}G_{yy}(r,z)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\sum_{k=0}^{|\mathcal{X}|}\mathbbm{1}_{|S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{X}|=k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}G_{\sigma(i)\sigma(i)}(r,z)\,,

(6.29)

where
\sigma
is a uniform random enumeration of
\mathcal{X}
(i.e. a bijection
[\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert]\to\mathcal{X}
) that is independent of
|S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{X}|
. Because of the condition
(C)
in Definition
6.23
, for any
k\neq l
,
|\sum_{i=1}^{l}G_{\sigma(i)\sigma(i)}(r,z)-\sum_{i=1}^{k}G_{\sigma(i)\sigma(i)}(r,z)|\geqslant(3z)^{-1}
.
Therefore, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
we get on
\Upsilon

\mathbb{P}\Biggl(\Biggl\lvert\sum_{k=0}^{|\mathcal{X}|}\mathbbm{1}_{|S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{X}|=k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}G_{\sigma(i)\sigma(i)}(r,z)-t\Biggr\rvert\leqslant\frac{1}{8z}\,\bigg|\,\sigma,\mathcal{F}\Biggr)\leqslant\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant|\mathcal{X}|}\mathbb{P}(|S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{X}|=k\,|\,\mathcal{F})\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\,,

where in the last step we used that
|S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{X}|\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\operatorname{Binom}(\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert,d/N)
conditioned on
\mathcal{F}
, that
\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert\geqslant N-N^{1/2}
by the condition
(D)
in Definition
6.23
, and that
d\gg 1
. From (
6.29
) we therefore conclude that

Q^{\mathcal{F}}\Biggl(\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\setminus\mathcal{V}^{(B_{r}(b))}}G_{yy}(r,z),\frac{1}{8z}\Biggr)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\,.

Hence, Remarks
6.7
and
6.25

(i)
imply

Q^{\mathcal{F}}\biggl(g_{x},\frac{1}{8zT^{2}d}\biggr)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\,,

which is (
6.28
) for
i=r
.

For the induction step, we let
i<r
, choose
x\in S_{i}(b)\cap\mathcal{R}
, and assume that

Q^{\mathcal{F}}(g_{y},L_{i+1})\leqslant P_{i+1}

for all
y\in S_{i+1}(b)\cap\mathcal{R}
. Note that
S_{1}^{+}(x)
and
\mathcal{R}
are
\mathcal{F}
-measurable, and that the family
(g_{y})_{y\in S_{1}+(x)}
is independent on
\Upsilon
conditioned on
\mathcal{F}
, by Remark
6.10
and Definition
6.23

(A)
. Hence, we can apply Proposition
2.8
to the concentration function
Q^{\mathcal{F}}
to obtain

Q^{\mathcal{F}}\Biggl(\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{R}}g_{y},L_{i+1}\Biggr)\leqslant\frac{K}{\sqrt{\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{R}\rvert}}P_{i+1}\leqslant\frac{K\sqrt{2}P_{i+1}}{d^{1/2}}\,,

(6.30)

where the last inequality follows from
\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{R}\rvert\geqslant d/2
, by Definition
6.21
.
Moreover, the conditional independence of the sums
\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{R}}g_{y}
and
\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\setminus\mathcal{R}}g_{y}
combined with Remark
6.25

(i)
and
(ii)
yields

\displaystyle Q^{\mathcal{F}}\Biggl(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}g_{y},\frac{L_{i+1}}{d}\Biggr)

\displaystyle=Q^{\mathcal{F}}\Biggl(\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)}g_{y},L_{i+1}\Biggr)

\displaystyle=Q^{\mathcal{F}}\Biggl(\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{R}}g_{y}+\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\setminus\mathcal{R}}g_{y},L_{i+1}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\leqslant Q^{\mathcal{F}}\Biggl(\sum_{y\in S_{1}^{+}(x)\cap\mathcal{R}}g_{y},L_{i+1}\Biggr)\,.

Hence, by Remark
6.7
, Remark
6.25

(i)
, Definition
6.9
, and (
6.30
), we obtain

\displaystyle Q^{\mathcal{F}}\Biggl(g_{x},\frac{L_{i+1}}{T^{2}d}\Biggr)\leqslant\frac{K\sqrt{2}P_{i+1}}{d^{1/2}}\,,

which is (
6.28
).
This completes the proof of (
6.28
) and, hence, the one of Proposition
6.26
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.22
.

The proof proceeds in two steps: first by establishing the claim for the root of a Galton-Watson branching process with Poisson offspring distribution with mean
d
, and then concluding by a comparison argument.

Denote by
\mathcal{P}_{s}
a Poisson random variable with expectation
s
. Let
W
denote the Galton-Watson branching process with Poisson offspring distribution
\mathcal{P}_{d}
, which we regard as a random rooted ordered tree
13
13

13

A rooted ordered tree (also called plane tree) is a rooted tree in which an ordering is specified among the children of each vertex.
whose root we call
o
. We use the graph-theoretic notations (such as
S_{i}(x)
) from Section
2.1
also on rooted ordered trees. Moreover, we extend Definition
6.21
to a rooted ordered tree
T
in the obvious fashion, and when needed we use the notation
\mathcal{R}\equiv\mathcal{R}_{r}(T,o)
to indicate the radius
r
, the tree
T
, and the root
o
explicitly.

We define the parameter
\delta\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{P}_{3d/4}\leqslant\frac{d}{2})
.
By Bennett’s inequality (see Lemma
D.1
below), we find that
\delta\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-cd}
for some universal constant
c>0
. We shall show by induction on
i
that

\mathbb{P}(o\notin\mathcal{R}_{i}(W,o))\leqslant\delta

(6.31)

for all
i\geqslant 0
.
For
i=0
we have
\mathbb{P}(o\notin\mathcal{R}_{i}(W,o))=0

since
o\in\mathcal{R}_{0}(W,o)
by (the analogue of) Definition
6.21
, and (
6.31
) is trivial.

To advance the induction, we suppose that (
6.31
) holds for some
i\geqslant 0
. By Definition
6.21
,

\mathbb{P}(o\notin\mathcal{R}_{i+1}(W,o))=\mathbb{P}\Biggl(\sum_{x\in S_{1}(o)}\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}(W,o)}<\frac{d}{2}\Biggr)\,.

By definition of the branching process
W
, conditioned on
S_{1}(o)
, the random variables
(\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}(W,o)})_{x\in S_{1}(o)}
are independent Bernoulli random variables with expectation

\mathbb{P}(x\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}(W,o)|S_{1}(o))=\mathbb{P}(o\in\mathcal{R}_{i}(W,o))=\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}1-\zeta_{i}\,,

where
x\in S_{1}(o)
.
We conclude that
\sum_{x\in S_{1}(o)}\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}(W,o)}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\mathcal{P}_{d(1-\zeta_{i})}
. Using the induction assumption
\zeta_{i}\leqslant\delta
from (
6.31
) and the bound
\delta\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-cd}<1/4
for large enough
d
, we therefore conclude that

\mathbb{P}(o\notin\mathcal{R}_{i+1}(W))=\mathbb{P}\biggl(\mathcal{P}_{d(1-\mathbb{P}(o\notin\mathcal{R}_{i}(W,o)))}<\frac{d}{2}\biggr)\leqslant\mathbb{P}\biggl(\mathcal{P}_{3d/4}\leqslant\frac{d}{2}\biggr)=\delta\,.

This concludes the proof of (
6.31
) for all
i\geqslant 0
.

Hence, denoting by
\mathcal{B}_{n,p}
a random variable with law
\operatorname{Binom}(n,p)
, we conclude that if
\lvert S_{1}(o)\rvert\geqslant d
then

\mathbb{P}(o\notin\mathcal{R}_{r}(W,o)\,|\,S_{1}(o))=\mathbb{P}\Biggl(\sum_{x\in S_{1}(o)}\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{R}_{r-1}(W,o)}<\frac{d}{2}\,\bigg|\,S_{1}(o)\Biggr)=\mathbb{P}\biggl(\mathcal{B}_{\lvert S_{1}(o)\rvert,1-\zeta_{r-1}}<\frac{d}{2}\,\bigg|\,S_{1}(o)\biggr)\\
\leqslant\mathbb{P}\biggl(\mathcal{B}_{d,1-\delta}<\frac{d}{2}\biggr)=\mathbb{P}\biggl(\mathcal{B}_{d,\delta}\geqslant\frac{d}{2}\biggr)\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-cd\delta\frac{1}{2\delta}\log\frac{1}{2\delta}}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-cd^{2}}\leqslant N^{-1}

(6.32)

for some universal constant
c>0
,
where in the third step we used that
\lvert S_{1}(o)\rvert\geqslant d
and
\zeta_{r-1}\leqslant\delta
by (
6.31
), in the fifth step Bennett’s inequality (see Lemma
D.1
below), in the sixth step that
\delta\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-cd}
, and in the last step the assumption
d\gg\sqrt{\log N}
. This concludes the estimate for the Galton-Watson process
W
.

Next, we analyse
\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(b\notin\mathcal{R}_{r}(\mathbb{G},b)\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))
. We note first that we can assume that
\lvert B_{1}(a)\rvert\leqslant 1+10\log N
and that
B_{1}(a)
and
B_{1}(b)
are disjoint, for otherwise the above probability vanishes by definition of
\Omega
and Proposition
3.2
.

We observe that a rooted ordered tree can be regarded as an equivalence class of (labelled) rooted trees up to a relabelling of the vertices that preserves the ordering of the children of each vertex. We denote by
[\mathbb{T},x]
the equivalence class of the labelled rooted tree
(\mathbb{T},x)
, where
x
is the root. By convention, if
\mathbb{T}
is not a tree (i.e. if it contains a cycle) then its equivalence class is the empty tree. We denote by
\mathfrak{T}_{r}
the set of rooted ordered trees of depth
r
. Moreover, we denote by
\mathfrak{T}_{r}^{*}\subset\mathfrak{T}_{r}
the subset of rooted ordered trees with at most
N^{1/5}
vertices and whose root is a robust vertex with
\lvert S_{1}(b)\rvert
children. Abbreviating
\Delta\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{B_{r}(b)\subset[N]\setminus B_{1}(a)\}
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}(b\notin\mathcal{R}_{r}(\mathbb{G},b)\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\Omega}([\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(b)},b]\notin\mathfrak{T}_{r}^{*}\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\{[\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(b)},b]\notin\mathfrak{T}_{r}^{*}\}\cap\Delta\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))\,,

(6.33)

where we used that, since
a,b\in\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}
, Proposition
3.2
implies that on the event
\Omega
the graph
\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(b)}
is a tree with at most
N^{1/5}
vertices, and that
\Omega\subset\Delta
.

For the following, let
T\in\mathfrak{T}_{r}^{*}
and denote by
o
its root. For
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
we introduce the event

\Theta_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{[\mathbb{G}|_{B_{i}(b)},b]=T|_{B_{i}(o)}\}\cap\{B_{i}(b)\subset[N]\setminus B_{1}(a)\}\,.

In particular,
\mathbb{P}(\Theta_{1}\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))=1
because of the assumed disjointness of
S_{1}(a)
and
S_{1}(b)
.
We now estimate

\mathbb{P}(\{[\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(b)},b]=T\}\cap\Delta\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))=\mathbb{P}(\Theta_{r}\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))

(6.34)

recursively, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1
, using the expression

\frac{\mathbb{P}(\Theta_{i+1}|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))}{\mathbb{P}(\Theta_{i}|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))}\\
=\frac{(N^{\prime}-\lvert B_{i}(o)\rvert)!}{(N^{\prime}-\lvert B_{i+1}(o)\rvert)!\prod_{x\in S_{i}(o)}\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\rvert!}\prod_{x\in S_{i}(o)}\biggl(\frac{d}{N}\biggr)^{\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\rvert}\biggl(1-\frac{d}{N}\biggr)^{N-\lvert B_{i}(o)\rvert-\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\rvert}\,,

where
N^{\prime}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=N-\lvert B_{1}(a)\rvert
, and the graph-theoretic quantities on the left-hand side are in terms of
\mathbb{G}
and on the right-hand side in terms of the deterministic rooted ordered tree
T
.
Here, the multinomial factor in front arises from a choice of the
\lvert S_{i}(o)\rvert
disjoint subsets representing the children of the vertices in
S_{i}(o)
from
N^{\prime}-\lvert B_{i}(o)\rvert
available vertices, and the remaining product follows by independence of the edges in
\mathbb{G}
. We deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}(\Theta_{i+1}|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))}{\mathbb{P}(\Theta_{i}|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))}

\displaystyle=(1+O(N^{-3/4}))\frac{(N^{\prime}-\lvert B_{i}(o)\rvert)!}{(N^{\prime}-\lvert B_{i+1}(o)\rvert)!\,N^{\lvert S_{i+1}(o)\rvert}}\prod_{x\in S_{i}(o)}\frac{d^{\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\rvert}}{\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\rvert!}\mathrm{e}^{-d}

\displaystyle=(1+O(N^{-3/5}))\prod_{x\in S_{i}(o)}\frac{d^{\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\rvert}}{\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\rvert!}\mathrm{e}^{-d}\,,

where we used that
N-N^{\prime}\leqslant 1+10\log N
and
\lvert B_{r}(o)\rvert\leqslant N^{1/5}
. By induction on
i
and comparison with the Galton-Watson tree
W
, using that

\frac{\mathbb{P}(W|_{B_{i+1}(o)}=T|_{B_{i+1}(o)}\,|\,S_{1}(o))}{\mathbb{P}(W|_{B_{i}(o)}=T|_{B_{i}(o)}\,|\,S_{1}(o))}=\prod_{x\in S_{i}(o)}\frac{d^{\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\rvert}}{\lvert S_{1}^{+}(x)\rvert!}\mathrm{e}^{-d}\,,

as well as
\mathbb{P}(\Theta_{1}|S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))=1
, we therefore conclude from (
6.34
) that if
\lvert S_{1}(b)\rvert=\lvert S_{1}(o)\rvert
then

\mathbb{P}(\{[\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(b)},b]=T\}\cap\Delta\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))=(1+O(N^{-1/2}))\,\mathbb{P}(W|_{B_{r}(o)}=T\,|\,S_{1}(o))

for all
T\in\mathfrak{T}_{r}^{*}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\{[\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(b)},b]\notin\mathfrak{T}_{r}^{*}\}\cap\Delta\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))

\displaystyle\leqslant 1-\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{T}_{r}^{*}}\mathbb{P}(\{[\mathbb{G}|_{B_{r}(b)},b]=T\}\cap\Delta\,|\,S_{1}(a),S_{1}(b))

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{T}_{r}^{*}}\mathbb{P}(W|_{B_{r}(o)}=T\,|\,S_{1}(o))+O(N^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(o\notin\mathcal{R}_{r}(W,o)\,|\,S_{1}(o))+O(N^{-1/2})\,.

The claim now follows from (
6.32
) and (
6.33
), noting that if
b\in\mathcal{W}
then
\lvert S_{1}(b)\rvert=\lvert S_{1}(o)\rvert\geqslant d
.
∎

Appendix A
Quantitative behaviour of
\alpha^{*}

We recall the definition of
\alpha^{*}(\mu)
from (
1.6
) and remark that, besides
\mu
,
it depends on
N
and
d
.
Our analysis of
\alpha^{*}(\mu)
is based on quantitatively approximating the distribution of
\alpha_{1}
by
a Poisson distribution.
Owing to the Poisson approximation of binomial random variables (see e.g.
(
ADK20
, Lemma A.6)
)
and the Stirling approximation of factorials,
we have

\mathbb{P}(d\alpha_{1}=k)=\frac{d^{k}}{k!}\mathrm{e}^{-d}\bigg(1+O\bigg(\frac{k^{2}}{N^{2}}+\frac{d^{2}}{N}\bigg)\bigg)=\exp(-f_{d}(k/d)+O(k^{-1}))

(A.1)

for
k\leqslant\sqrt{N}
,
where
f_{d}(\alpha)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=d(\alpha\log\alpha-\alpha+1)+\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\alpha d)
for
\alpha>0
.

Lemma A.1
.

If
\mu\in[0,1-\varepsilon]
for some constant
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
then there is a constant
T\geqslant 2
such that if
d\geqslant 1
and
t\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\log N}{d}\geqslant T
then

\alpha^{*}(\mu)=\frac{(1-\mu)t}{\log t}\bigg(1+O\bigg(\frac{\log\log t}{\log t}\bigg)\bigg)\,.

(A.2)

Lemma
A.1
is proved at the end of this appendix. We use it to derive two simple consequences.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
1\leqslant d\leqslant 3\log N
and
\mu\in[0,1-\varepsilon]
for some constant
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.
If
x\in\mathcal{V}
then

\alpha_{x}\gtrsim\frac{\log N}{d\log\big(\frac{10\log N}{d}\big)}\,.

Proof.

Let
T
be as in Lemma
A.1
. If
\frac{\log N}{d}\geqslant T
then Lemma
A.2
follows directly from Lemma
A.1
and the definition of
\mathcal{V}

in (
2.1
).
If
\frac{\log N}{d}<T\lesssim 1
then
\alpha_{x}\geqslant\alpha^{*}(\mu)\geqslant 2+\kappa
directly implies
the lemma as
\frac{\log N}{d\log\big(\frac{10\log N}{d}\big)}\asymp 1
.
∎

Corollary A.3
.

Let
\mu
,
\nu\in[0,1-\varepsilon]
for some constant
\varepsilon>0
.
Then there is a constant
T\equiv T(\varepsilon)\geqslant 2
such that if
t\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\log N}{d}\geqslant T
then

\frac{\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\nu))}{\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\mu))}=\sqrt{\frac{1-\nu}{1-\mu}}\bigg(1+O\bigg(\frac{\log\log t}{\log t}\bigg)\bigg)\,.

Proof.

Choose
T
as in Lemma
A.1
, which then yields that

\frac{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}{\alpha^{*}(\mu)}=\frac{1-\nu}{1-\mu}\big(1+O\big(\frac{\log\log t}{\log t}\big)\big)
.
Hence, using
\min\{\alpha^{*}(\nu),\alpha^{*}(\mu)\}\gtrsim\frac{t}{\log t}
, we get

\frac{\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\nu))}{\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\mu))}=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}{\alpha^{*}(\mu)}}\bigg(1+O\bigg(\frac{1}{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{*}(\mu)}\bigg)\bigg)=\sqrt{\frac{1-\nu}{1-\mu}}\bigg(1+O\bigg(\frac{\log\log t}{\log t}\bigg)\bigg)\,.\qed

Proof of Lemma
A.1
.

Fix
\mu\in[0,1-\varepsilon]
for some constant
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.
Throughout the proof we abbreviate
\alpha^{*}\equiv\alpha^{*}(\mu)
.
A simple application of Bennett’s inequality (see Lemma
D.1
below) shows that
\alpha^{*}\lesssim(\log N)/d
.

If
Z
is a random variable with law
\mathrm{Binom}(n,d/n)
then there is a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{P}(Z\geqslant k)=\mathbb{P}(Z=k)\bigg(1+O\bigg(\frac{d}{k}\bigg)\bigg)

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
2d\leqslant k\leqslant\sqrt{n}/C
(see e.g.
(
BBK2
, eq. (3.4))
).
Therefore, for any
\alpha
satisfying
2d\leqslant\alpha d\lesssim\log N
, we obtain

\mathbb{P}(\alpha_{1}\geqslant\alpha)=\mathbb{P}(\alpha_{1}=\alpha)(1+O(\alpha^{-1}))\\
=\exp(-f_{d}(\alpha)+O((\alpha d)^{-1}))(1+O(\alpha^{-1}))=\exp(-f_{d}(\alpha)+O(\alpha^{-1}))\,,

(A.3)

where we used (
A.1
) in the second step and
incorporated the error term into the argument of the exponential in the last step.
As
\alpha^{*}\in\mathbb{N}/d
, the definition of
\alpha^{*}
in (
1.6
) implies

\mathbb{P}(\alpha_{1}\geqslant\alpha^{*})\leqslant N^{\mu-1}\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\alpha_{1}\geqslant\alpha^{*}-d^{-1})\,.

(A.4)

Since
f_{d}(\alpha^{*}-d^{-1})=f_{d}(\alpha^{*})+O((\log\alpha^{*})^{-1})
we conclude from
(
A.3
) and (
A.4
)
that

f_{d}(\alpha^{*})=(1-\mu)\log N+O\big((\log\alpha^{*})^{-1}\big)

which yields

\alpha^{*}=\frac{(1-\mu)t}{-1+\log\alpha^{*}}+O\big((\log\alpha^{*})^{-1}\big)

(A.5)

with
t\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\log N}{d}
.
We replace
\alpha^{*}
in the first term on the right-hand side of (
A.5
)
by the entire right-hand side of (
A.5
)
and choose
T\geqslant 2
a sufficiently large constant to arrive at

\alpha^{*}=\frac{(1-\mu)t}{\log t+\log(1-\mu)-\log(\log\alpha^{*}-1)+O((\alpha^{*}\log\alpha^{*})^{-1})-1}+O((\log\alpha^{*})^{-1})\,.

(A.6)

By possibly increasing the constant
T
, we see that
\alpha^{*}\gtrsim\frac{t}{\log t}
.
Using this lower bound to replace
\alpha^{*}
in the error terms of (
A.6
)
by
t
yields (
A.2
) and, thus,
completes the proof of Lemma
A.1
.
∎

Appendix B
Qualitative behaviour of degree sequence and of
\alpha^{*}

In this appendix we describe the qualitative behaviour of the normalized degree sequence
(\alpha_{x})_{x\in[N]}
and apply it to
\alpha^{*}(\mu)
from (
1.6
). For definiteness, we focus on the critical regime, where
d=b\log N
for some constant
b
, and consider the limit
N\to\infty
with
\kappa=o(1)
. For detailed proofs, we refer to
(
ADK20
, Appendix A.4)
.

For
b\geqslant 0
and
\alpha\geqslant 2
define

\theta_{b}(\alpha)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1-b(\alpha\log\alpha-\alpha+1)\,.

(B.1)

For any
b\leqslant b_{*}
, it is easy to see that for any
\mu\in\bigl[0,1-\frac{b}{b_{*}}\bigr]
the equation
\mu=\theta_{b}(\alpha)
has a unique solution
\alpha\geqslant 2
, which we denote by
\alpha_{*}(\mu)
. By Poisson approximation from (
A.1
), we deduce that, with high probability, for any
\alpha\geqslant 0
we have

\lvert\{x\in[N]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\alpha_{x}\geqslant\alpha\}\rvert=N^{\theta_{b}(\alpha)+o(1)}+o(1)\,.

(B.2)

Recalling the definition (
1.6
), we hence conclude that
\alpha^{*}(\mu)=\alpha_{*}(\mu)+o(1)
. In other words,
\alpha_{*}(\mu)
is the asymptotic value of
\alpha^{*}(\mu)
. We refer to Figure
B.1
for an illustration of the function
\alpha_{*}(\mu)
.

Figure B.1:
An illustration of the function
\mu\mapsto\alpha_{*}(\mu)
, the asymptotic version of
\alpha^{*}(\mu)
. We plot it for the values
b=2.0,2.1,\dots,2.5
, corresponding to the graphs from top to bottom. Each graph crosses the horizontal axis at
\mu=1-\frac{b}{b_{*}}
and the vertical axis at
\alpha=\alpha_{\max}(b)
.

Next, we define

\alpha_{\max}(b)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\alpha_{*}(0)\,,\qquad\lambda_{\max}(b)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Lambda(\alpha_{\max}(b))\,,

(B.3)

which, by (
B.2
) and Remark
1.2
,
have the interpretation of the asymptotic largest normalized degree and largest nontrivial eigenvalue of
H
, respectively. Moreover, for
b<b_{*}
we define

\rho_{b}(\lambda)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\theta_{b}(\Lambda^{-1}(\lambda))_{+}&\text{if }\lvert\lambda\rvert\geqslant 2\\
1&\text{if }\lvert\lambda\rvert<2\,,\end{cases}

(B.4)

where
\Lambda^{-1}(\lambda)=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}(1+\sqrt{1-4/\lambda^{2}})
for
\lvert\lambda\rvert\geqslant 2
. Then with high probability the density of states around energy
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
equals
N^{\rho_{b}(\lambda)+o(1)}
. For
\lambda>2
, this follows from Remark
1.2
and (
B.2
). The function
\rho_{b}
is illustrated in Figure
B.2
below.

Figure B.2:
The behaviour of the exponent
\rho_{b}
of the density of states from (
B.4
), as a function of the energy
\lambda
. Here
d=b\log N
with
b=1
and
\lambda_{\max}(b)\approx 2.0737
. We only plot a neighbourhood of the threshold energy
2
. The jump at
2
of
\rho_{b}
is from
\rho_{b}(2^{-})=1
to
\rho_{b}(2^{+})=1-b/b_{*}=2-2\log 2
.

Appendix C
Largest eigenvalues of
H
and some submatrices – proof of Proposition
3.4

In this section we prove Proposition
3.4
. We start with the following auxiliary result.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
d
satisfy (
1.5
).
With high probability, for all
\nu\in[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert(H-\mathbb{E}H)^{(\{x\,:\,\alpha_{x}\geqslant\alpha^{*}(\nu)\})}\rVert\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\nu))+o(1)\,.

(C.1)

Before giving the proof of Lemma
C.1
, we use it to establish Proposition
3.4
.
Lemma
C.1
is the core ingredient in the proof of Proposition
3.4

(i)
.
The main ideas for the proofs of Proposition
3.4

(i)
and Lemma
C.1

stem from
ADK19
, where a version of Proposition
3.4

(i)
was shown in

(
ADK19
, eq. (8.11))
. However,
(
ADK19
, eq. (8.11))
does not cover the full range of

required in Proposition
3.4

and does not have precise enough error bounds. Therefore, we apply the results from
ADK20
,
which refined the ideas from

.

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

For the proof of
(i)
,
we choose

in (

), observe that
\{x\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\alpha_{x}\geqslant\alpha^{*}(\mu)\}=\mathcal{V}
and use eigenvalue interlacing (Lemma

) to obtain
(i)
since
(\mathbb{E}H)^{(\mathcal{V})}
is a positive semidefinite rank-one matrix.

For the proof of

, we regard
H^{(X)}
as perturbation of

.
We note that
\lVert H^{(X)}-(\mathbb{E}H)^{(X)}\rVert\leqslant\lVert H-\mathbb{E}H\rVert\ll\sqrt{d}

), that

\sqrt{d}\big(1-\frac{\lvert X\rvert}{N}\big)
and associated eigenvector
\frac{\mathbf{1}_{X^{c}}}{\lvert X^{c}\rvert^{1/2}}
, and that
\lvert X\rvert\leqslant\sum_{x\in\mathcal{V}}\lvert B_{r}(x)\rvert\leqslant N^{1/3+o(1)}
by Proposition
3.2

(i)
and Proposition

(ii)
, recalling the condition (
3.2
). We hence conclude

using Lemma

.

For the proof of

H^{(X)}\mathbf{v}_{r}(x)=H\mathbf{v}_{r}(x)
since

and
\supp\mathbf{v}\subset B_{r-1}(x)
. To estimate

.
From the assumption

\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert\leqslant 10\log N
by Proposition

(i)
and the lower bound in (

),
we conclude that the condition on

in
(
ADK19
, Proposition 5.1)
is satisfied
14
14

14

See
(
ADK19
, eq. (5.1))
for the definition of
r_{x}
.
.
The condition
\log d\ll r\leqslant\frac{\log N}{6\log d}
,

for

Therefore, using the lower bound on

with high probability.
Using that

We now turn to the proof of

.
For the given

\mathbf{q}

From (

.
Therefore, there is

Since

Finally, from

\mathcal{U}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\big\{x\in[N]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\alpha_{x}\geqslant 2+\xi^{1/4}\big\}&\text{ if }d>(\log N)^{3/4}\\
\big\{x\in[N]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\alpha_{x}\geqslant\mathfrak{a}/5\big\}&\text{ if }d\leqslant(\log N)^{3/4}\end{cases}

(C.2)

with

We now show that

For

This implies

(iv)
.
∎

Remark C.2
.

We note that in Proposition
3.4

(iv)
,
actually
\supp\mathbf{q}\subset\big(\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{U}}B_{r+1}(x)\big)^{c}
holds
as shown in the above proof.

Proof of Lemma
C.1
.

We first recall
\xi
and
\xi_{u}
from (
5.17
).
Throughout the proof, we choose
\tau\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1+\xi^{1/2}
.
Note that
\xi=o(1)
,
\tau=1+o(1)
and
\xi_{\tau-1}=o(1)
by (
5.21
).

The proof relies on an analogous argument in
(
ADK20
, Proof of Proposition 3.12)
,
to which we refer in the following. We begin by introducing some notation. Following
(
ADK20
, Section 3.1)
, we define
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{x\in[N]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\alpha_{x}\geqslant\alpha\}
for
\alpha>0
.
We set
\mathcal{U}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{V}_{2+\xi^{1/4}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}
; we note that the set
\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}
was denoted by
\mathcal{V}
in
ADK20
, see
(
ADK20
, eqs. (1.9) and (3.3))
. Observe that
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}\subset\mathcal{V}_{2+\xi^{1/4}}
by the definition of
\alpha^{*}
in
(
1.6
).
From (
5.23
) and
\lVert M^{(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)})}\rVert\leqslant\lVert M\rVert
for any matrix
M
, we conclude that
(H-\mathbb{E}H)^{(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)})}=(\widehat{H}^{\tau})^{(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)})}+o(1)
, where
o(1)
is in the sense of operator norm.
Therefore, in order to prove (
C.1
), it suffices to prove that, for any constant
\delta>0
, the matrix
(\widehat{H}^{\tau})^{(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)})}
does not have an eigenvalue larger than
\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\nu))+\delta
.

Suppose, by contradiction, that
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
(\widehat{H}^{\tau})^{(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)})}
with associated normalized eigenvector
\mathbf{w}
such that
\lvert\lambda\rvert>\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\nu))+\delta
. Let
x\in\mathcal{U}
. Since
(\supp\mathbf{v}^{\tau}_{\sigma}(x))\cap\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}=\emptyset
by
the definition of
\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}^{\tau}(x)
in (
5.18
),
(
ADK20
, Proposition 3.1 (i))
and

\alpha^{*}(\nu)\geqslant 2+\kappa\geqslant\tau
, we deduce from the definition of
\widehat{H}^{\tau}
in (
5.20
) that

\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}^{\tau}(x)
is an eigenvector of
(\widehat{H}^{\tau})^{(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)})}
with eigenvalue
\sigma\Lambda(\alpha_{x})
.
As
\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\nu))
for any
x\in\mathcal{U}
, we conclude that
\mathbf{w}\perp\mathbf{v}^{\tau}_{\sigma}(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{U}
and
\sigma=\pm
. Since
\supp\mathbf{w}\subset(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)})^{c}
, we deduce from (
5.23
),

(
ADK20
, Proposition 3.13)
15
15

15

We stress that the definition of
H
in
ADK20
differs from that in the current paper; see
(
ADK20
, Definition 3.6)
.
and (
1.5
) that

\lvert\lambda\rvert=\lvert\langle\mathbf{w}\mspace{2mu},(\widehat{H}^{\tau})^{(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)})}\mathbf{w}\rangle\rvert=\lvert\langle\mathbf{w}\mspace{2mu},\widehat{H}^{\tau}\mathbf{w}\rangle\rvert\leqslant 1+\tau+(1+o(1))\sum_{x\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}}\alpha_{x}\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}+o(1)\,.

(C.3)

Note that
\tau=1+o(1)
and
\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\nu))\geqslant 2
imply
2\tau\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\nu))+o(1)
.

We will now apply Lemma
5.15
for each
x\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}
.
Note that
\lvert\lambda\rvert\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\nu))+\delta>2+o(1)=2\tau+C\xi
. In particular, there is a constant
c\equiv c_{\delta}\in(0,1)
such that

\frac{2\tau+C\xi}{\lvert\lambda\rvert}\leqslant 1-c
.
Owing to the disjointness of the balls
(B_{2r_{\star}}^{\tau}(x))_{x\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}}
(see
(
ADK20
, Proposition 3.1 (i))
)
and the locality of
\widehat{H}^{\tau}
,
we have
((\widehat{H}^{\tau})^{(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)})}\mathbf{w})|_{B_{2r_{\star}}^{\tau}(x)}=(\widehat{H}^{\tau}\mathbf{w})|_{B_{2r_{\star}}^{\tau}(x)}

for all
x\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}
. Hence, for these
x
, (
5.36
) holds.
If
x\in\mathcal{V}_{2+\xi^{1/4}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}
then
\mathbf{w}\perp\mathbf{v}_{\pm}^{\tau}(x)
as demonstrated above.
Thus, all conditions of Lemma
5.15
are satisfied for each
x\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}
.
Therefore, Lemma
5.15

and the disjointness of the balls
(B_{2r_{\star}}^{\tau}(x))_{x\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}}
(compare with the similar argument in (
5.38
))
yield

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}}\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}\leqslant\bigg(1-\frac{2\tau+C\xi}{\lvert\lambda\rvert}\bigg)^{-4}\bigg(\frac{2\tau+C\xi}{\lvert\lambda\rvert}\bigg)^{2r_{\star}}\leqslant c^{-4}(1-c)^{2r_{\star}}\ll\frac{d}{\log N}\,,

where we used
\frac{2\tau+C\xi}{\lvert\lambda\rvert}\leqslant 1-c
in the second step and
r_{\star}\asymp\sqrt{\log N}
as well as (
1.5
) in the last step.
Hence, from Proposition
3.1

(i)
, we conclude

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\alpha^{*}(\nu)}}\alpha_{x}\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}=o(1)
.
Using this in
(
C.3
) implies

\lvert\lambda\rvert\leqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\nu))+o(1)
, in contradiction with the assumption on
\lambda
.
We therefore conclude (
C.1
).
∎

Appendix D
Tools

In this appendix we summarize several well known results used throughout this paper.

D.1.  Bennett’s inequality

Define the function
h\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
by

h(a)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(1+a)\log(1+a)-a\,.

(D.1)

We use the notation
\mathcal{P}_{\mu}
to denote a Poisson random variable with parameter
\mu\geqslant 0
, and
\mathcal{B}_{n,p}
to denote a binomial random variable with parameters
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
p\in[0,1]
.
The following estimate is proved in
(
BLM13
, Section 2.7)
.

Lemma D.1

(Bennett)
.

For
0\leqslant\mu\leqslant n
and
a>0
we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{n,\mu/n}-\mu\geqslant a\mu)\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-\mu h(a)}\,,\qquad\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{n,\mu/n}-\mu\leqslant-a\mu)\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-\mu a^{2}/2}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-\mu h(a)}\,,

and
\frac{a^{2}}{2(1+a/3)}\leqslant h(a)\leqslant\frac{a^{2}}{2}
. By taking
n\to\infty
, the same estimates hold with
\mathcal{B}_{n,\mu/n}
replaced with
\mathcal{P}_{\mu}
.

D.2.  Perturbation theory

The following lemma contains simple perturbation estimates for approximate eigenvalues and eigenvectors.
Its proof can be found in
(
ADK21
, Lemma 4.10)
.

Lemma D.2
.

Let
M
be a real symmetric matrix. Let
\varepsilon
,
\Delta>0
satisfy
5\varepsilon\leqslant\Delta
.
Suppose that
M
has a unique eigenvalue,
\lambda
, in
[\widehat{\lambda}-\Delta,\widehat{\lambda}+\Delta]
for
some
\widehat{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\mathbf{w}
be a corresponding normalized eigenvector of
M
.
If there exists a normalized vector
\mathbf{v}
such that
\lVert(M-\widehat{\lambda})\mathbf{v}\rVert\leqslant\varepsilon
then, for some
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
,

\lambda-\widehat{\lambda}=\langle\mathbf{v}\mspace{2mu},(M-\widehat{\lambda})\mathbf{v}\rangle+O\bigg(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\Delta}\bigg)\,,\qquad\qquad\lVert\mathbf{w}-\sigma\mathbf{v}\rVert=O\bigg(\frac{\varepsilon}{\Delta}\bigg)\,.

D.3.  Eigenvalue interlacing

For an
N\times N
Hermitian matrix
M
, we use the convention that
\lambda_{i}(M)=-\infty
for
i>N
.

Lemma D.3

(Interlacing for minors)
.

Let
X\subset Y\subset[N]
and
M
be an
N\times N
Hermitian matrix. Then, for all
i\in[N]
,

\lambda_{i+\lvert Y\setminus X\rvert}(M^{(X)})_{+}\leqslant\lambda_{i}(M^{(Y)})_{+}\leqslant\lambda_{i}(M^{(X)})_{+}\,,

where
\lambda_{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\max\{\lambda,0\}
.

Lemma D.4

(Interlacing for rank-one perturbations)
.

Let
M
be an
N\times N
Hermitian matrix and
V
a rank-one positive semidefinite
N\times N
matrix. Then, for all
i\in[N]
,

\lambda_{i+1}(M+V)\leqslant\lambda_{i}(M)\leqslant\lambda_{i}(M+V)\,.

D.4.  Resolvent of adjacency matrix of
\mathbb{N}^{*}

Define the infinite tridiagonal matrix

M\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0&\\
1&0&1&\ddots\\
0&1&0&\ddots\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{pmatrix}\,,

(D.2)

which we regard as a bounded operator on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}^{*})
.
Note that
M
is the adjacency matrix of
\mathbb{N}^{*}
with the graph structure induced by regarding adjacent numbers as neighbours.

Lemma D.5
.

Let
t>2
. Then
1-t^{-1}M

is invertible with convergent Neumann series and explicit inverse

(1-t^{-1}M)^{-1}_{1j}=t\bigg(\frac{2}{t+\sqrt{t^{2}-4}}\bigg)^{j}

for any
j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

Proof.

By the Schur test,
\lVert M\rVert\leqslant 2
. Therefore,
t>2
implies the invertibility of

1-t^{-1}M
in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}^{*})
and the convergence of the Neumann series representation of its inverse.
In particular,

a=(a_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}^{*})
with
a_{j}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(1-t^{-1}M)^{-1}_{1j}
for
any
j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. With
e_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\delta_{1j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
, the definition of the resolvent yields

(1-t^{-1}M)a=e_{1}
and, thus,

a_{1}-t^{-1}a_{2}=1\,,\qquad\qquad\begin{pmatrix}a_{j+1}\\
a_{j}\end{pmatrix}=T(t)\begin{pmatrix}a_{j}\\
a_{j-1}\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad T(t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}t&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}

(D.3)

for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
j\geqslant 2
.
The transfer matrix
T(t)
has the two eigenvalues,
\gamma
and
\gamma^{-1}
, where

\gamma\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{2}{t+\sqrt{t^{2}-4}}\,.

As
t>2
, we have
\gamma<1
and, hence,
a\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}^{*})
implies

a_{j}=\gamma a_{j-1}=\gamma^{j-1}a_{1}
for any
j\geqslant 2
.
Together with the first relation in (
D.3
), we obtain

a_{1}=\frac{2t}{t+\sqrt{t^{2}-4}}
,
which completes the proof.
∎

Appendix E
Graph properties – proofs of Propositions
3.1
and
3.2

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

We show that each item holds individually with high probability. By Bennett’s inequality (see Lemma
D.1
below) and a union bound, we conclude that

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{x\in[N]}|S_{1}(x)|>10\log N\Bigr)\leqslant N\mathrm{e}^{-10\log N}\ll 1\,,

where we used
d\leqslant 3\log N
. This shows
(i)
.

To show
(ii)
, we abbreviate
D_{x}=\lvert S_{1}(x)\rvert
and note that by part
(i)
and a union bound, it suffices to show, for any
x\in[N]
,

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert B_{i}(x)\rvert\geqslant 2\max\{D_{x},d\}d^{i-1}\,|\,D_{x}\bigr)\leqslant N^{-2}

(E.1)

for all
i\leqslant\frac{1}{3}\frac{\log N}{\log d}
and
D_{x}\leqslant 10\log N
. If
D_{x}\geqslant d
, then (
E.1
) follows from from
(
ADK19
, eq. (5.12b))
, where the upper and lower bounds in the condition
(
ADK19
, eq. (5.13))
follow from
D_{x}\leqslant 10\log N
and the upper bound on
i
, as well as
D_{x}\geqslant d
and
d\gg\sqrt{\log N}
, respectively.

For
D_{x}\leqslant d
the bound (
E.1
) follows from the monotonicity property

\mathbb{P}(\lvert B_{i}(x)\rvert\geqslant L\,|\,D_{x}=k)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\lvert B_{i}(x)\rvert\geqslant L\,|\,D_{x}=l)

(E.2)

for any
k\leqslant l\leqslant N-1
, combined with (
E.1
) on the event
\{D_{x}=d\}
.

What remains is the proof of (
E.2
).
To that end, fix
x\in[N]
and let
k\leqslant l\leqslant N-1
.
For
y\in[N]\setminus\{x\}
, we denote by
B_{i}^{(x)}(y)
the ball in
\mathbb{G}|_{[N]\setminus\{x\}}

of radius
i
around
y
.
Moreover, let
(K_{1},K_{2})
be disjoint random uniformly chosen subsets of
[N]\setminus\{x\}
of sizes
k
and
l-k
,
respectively.
Then we estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(|B_{i}(x)|\geqslant L\big|D_{x}=k\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigg(\biggl\lvert\bigcup_{y\in S_{1}(x)}B_{i-1}^{(x)}(y)\biggr\rvert\geqslant L-1\bigg|D_{x}=k\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigg(\biggl\lvert\bigcup_{y\in K_{1}}B_{i-1}^{(x)}(y)\biggr\rvert\geqslant L-1\bigg)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\bigg(\biggl\lvert\bigcup_{y\in K_{1}\cup K_{2}}B_{i-1}^{(x)}(y)\biggr\rvert\geqslant L-1\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigg(\biggl\lvert\bigcup_{y\in S_{1}(x)}B_{i-1}^{(x)}(y)\biggr\rvert\geqslant L-1\bigg|D_{x}=l\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(|B_{i}(x)|\geqslant L|D_{x}=l)\,.

This proves (
E.2
) and, thus, completes the proof of Proposition
3.1
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

We show that each item holds individually with high probability.
For the proof of
(i)
, we estimate

\mathbb{P}(\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert\geqslant tN^{\mu})\leqslant t^{-1}N^{-\mu}\mathbb{E}\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert=t^{-1}N^{-\mu}N\mathbb{P}(\alpha_{1}\geqslant\alpha^{*})\leqslant t^{-1}

for
t>0
by the definitions of
\mathcal{V}
and
\alpha^{*}
in (
2.1
) and (
1.6
), respectively.
This proves
(i)
.
Item
(ii)
is a consequence of
(
ADK19
, Lemma 5.5)
with
k=1
, Proposition
3.1

(i)
,
r\leqslant\big(\frac{1}{5}-\frac{\mu}{4}\big)\frac{\log N}{\log d}
, and
d\geqslant\sqrt{\log N}
.
Item
(iii)
follows from
(
ADK21
, eq. (9.5))
with the choices
\tau=\alpha^{*}
and
n=2
,
\exp(-dh(\alpha^{*}-1-\frac{3}{d}))\leqslant N^{\mu-1+o(1)}
and
r\leqslant\big(\frac{1}{3}-\mu\big)\frac{\log N}{\log d}
.

To prove
(iv)
, we set
\eta/2\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=r\frac{\log d}{\log N}

and conclude
from
(i)
, Proposition
3.1

(i)
and
(ii)
that

\lvert\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{V}}B_{r}(x)\rvert\leqslant N^{\mu+\eta/2+o(1)}
.
Thus, we obtain
(iv)
by arguing similarly
as in
(
ADK21
, Proof of Proposition 4.4)
, see especially
(
ADK21
, eq. (9.10))
.
∎

Appendix F
Eigenfunction correlator and dynamical localization – proof of Corollary
1.10

Before proving (
1.16
),
we use it to show (
1.18
).
If
\mathrm{d}(x,y)\leqslant 1
then (
1.18
) holds trivially. If
\mathrm{d}(x,y)\geqslant 2
, (
1.18
) follows
directly from (
1.16
) by choosing
r=\mathrm{d}(x,y)-1

and
F(\lambda)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\lambda}
. This completes the proof of (
1.18
) assuming
(
1.16
).

We now turn to the proof of (
1.16
). First, we introduce some notation. By (
2.3
) from the proof of Theorem
1.1
, with high probability, there is a one-to-one correspondence between eigenvalues satisfying (
1.7
) and vertices
x
in some subset of
\mathcal{W}
. Under this correspondence, we denote such an eigenvalue and its corresponding eigenvector by
\lambda_{x}
and

\mathbf{w}(x)
, respectively.

Let
x\in[N]
. Let
J\subset I
be an interval, where
I\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=[\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\mu))+\kappa,\sqrt{d}/2]
. Let
F
be a function as in the statement of Corollary
1.10
. Then we obtain

\Pi_{J}(H)F(H)\mathbf{1}_{x}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{W}\colon\lambda_{a}\in J}F(\lambda_{a})\langle\mathbf{w}(a)\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{1}_{x}\rangle\mathbf{w}(a).

By following the proof of Lemma
5.13
, we see that if
\mu<\frac{1}{24}
and
r\leqslant\frac{1}{6}\frac{\log N}{\log d}
then, with high probability,

\lVert\mathbf{u}(a)|_{B_{r}(b)}\rVert\leqslant N^{-3/8}

(F.1)

for all
a\neq b\in\mathcal{W}
.

We now fix
r\geqslant 0
as in the statement.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
r\leqslant 2\frac{\log N}{\log d}
, because
\diam(\mathbb{G})\leqslant 2\frac{\log N}{\log d}
with high probability.
Suppose first that there is
b\in\mathcal{W}
such that
x\in B_{R}(b)
with
R\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\min\{\frac{r}{2},\frac{1}{6}\frac{\log N}{\log d}\}
. Note that this
b
is unique by Proposition
3.2

(iii)
and
R\geqslant r/12
as
r\leqslant 2\frac{\log N}{\log d}
.
In this case, (
1.16
) follows from

\lVert(\Pi_{J}(H)F(H)\mathbf{1}_{x})|_{B_{r}(x)^{c}}\rVert^{2}\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{w}(b)|_{B_{r}(x)^{c}}\rVert^{2}+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{W}\setminus\{b\}}\lvert\langle\mathbf{w}(a)\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{1}_{x}\rangle\rvert^{2}\\
\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-cR}+N^{-3/4+\mu}+N^{2(\eta-\zeta)+\mu}\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-cr}.

(F.2)

Here, we used that
\lVert\mathbf{w}(b)|_{B_{r}(x)^{c}}\rVert\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{w}(b)|_{B_{r}(x)^{c}}\rVert\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-cR}
,
which is a consequence of
B_{R}(b)\subset B_{r}(x)
as well as (
1.8
)
if
\alpha_{x}\leqslant 3
or
R\geqslant 10\log\log N
due to (
3.1
) and (
1.9
) otherwise.
Moreover, we employed

\lvert\langle\mathbf{w}(a)\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{1}_{x}\rangle\rvert^{2}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{w}(a)|_{B_{R}(b)}\rVert^{2}\lesssim N^{-3/4}+N^{2(\eta-\zeta)}

due to (
2.3
) and (
F.1
).
The last step in (
F.2
) follows from
r/12\leqslant R\leqslant\frac{1}{6}\frac{\log N}{\log d}
and
N^{-3/4+\mu}+N^{2(\eta-\zeta)+\mu}\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-c\log N/\log d}
by appropriate choices of
\eta
and
\zeta
if
\mu<\frac{1}{24}
.

Hence, it remains to consider the case
x\notin B_{R}(\mathcal{W})
. Under this condition, we will
show that

\lVert\Pi_{J}(H)\mathbf{1}_{x}\rVert\leqslant C\mathrm{e}^{-cr}

(F.3)

if
r\leqslant 2\frac{\log N}{\log d}
, which will directly imply (
1.16
) in this missing case.

For the proof of (
F.3
), we note that
\lVert\Pi_{J}(H)\mathbf{1}_{x}\rVert^{2}\leqslant\lVert\Pi_{I}(H)\mathbf{1}_{x}\rVert^{2}
as
J\subset I=[\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa,\sqrt{d}/2]

and, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
2k<R
, estimate

\lVert\Pi_{I}(H)\mathbf{1}_{x}\rVert^{2}(\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+\kappa)^{2k}\leqslant\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\Pi_{I}(H)H^{2k}\Pi_{I}(H)\mathbf{1}_{x}\rangle\leqslant\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},H^{2k}\mathbf{1}_{x}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},(H^{(\mathcal{W})})^{2k}\mathbf{1}_{x}\rangle,

where the last step follows from the locality of
H
,
2k<r/2
and
x\notin B_{R}(\mathcal{W})
. Moreover, by locality of
H
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},(H^{(\mathcal{W})})^{2k}\mathbf{1}_{x}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},(H^{(\mathcal{W})}-(\mathbb{E}H)^{(\mathcal{W}\cup B_{k}(x))})^{2k}\mathbf{1}_{x}\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert H^{(\mathcal{W})}-(\mathbb{E}H)^{(\mathcal{W}\cup B_{k}(x))}\rVert^{2k}

\displaystyle\leqslant\bigl(\lVert H^{(\mathcal{W})}-(\mathbb{E}H)^{(\mathcal{W})}\rVert+\lVert(\mathbb{E}H)^{(\mathcal{W})}-(\mathbb{E}H)^{(\mathcal{W}\cup B_{k}(x))}\rVert\bigr)^{2k}

\displaystyle\leqslant(\Lambda(\alpha^{*})+o(1))^{2k}

with high probability,
where we used Proposition
3.1
, Lemma
C.1
, (
3.1
) and (
2.2
) in the last step.
Combining these bounds, choosing
k\asymp r
and using
r\leqslant 2\frac{\log N}{\log d}
implies the existence
of constants
C>0
and
c>0
depending on
\kappa
such that
\lVert\Pi_{I}(H)\mathbf{1}_{x}\rVert\leqslant C\mathrm{e}^{-cr}
if
d\asymp\log N
as
\Lambda(\alpha^{*})\asymp 1
in this case.
For
d\asymp\log N
, this proves (
F.3
) since
\lVert\Pi_{J}(H)\mathbf{1}_{x}\rVert\leqslant\lVert\Pi_{I}(H)\mathbf{1}_{x}\rVert
as explained above. Otherwise, we use
T
as in Corollary
A.3
for
\varepsilon=1/2
. We choose
\nu<\frac{1}{24}
satisfying the assumptions made on
\mu
in this proof up to now, and consider
\mathcal{W}
to be defined
with respect to this
\nu
instead of
\mu
, i.e.
\mathcal{W}=\{x\in[N]\colon\Lambda(\alpha_{x})\geqslant\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\nu))+\kappa/2\}
(cf. (
2.2
)).
For any constant
\mu\in(0,\nu)
and
I=[\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\mu)+\kappa,\sqrt{d}/2]
, we obtain

\lVert\Pi_{I}(H)\mathbf{1}_{x}\rVert^{2}\leqslant\big(\frac{\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\nu))+o(1)}{\Lambda(\alpha^{*}(\mu))+\kappa}\big)^{2k}\leqslant(1-\varepsilon)^{2k}
for some constant
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, where the last step follows from
Corollary
A.3
by assuming
\frac{\log N}{d}\geqslant T
.
Since
\lVert\Pi_{J}(H)\mathbf{1}_{x}\rVert\leqslant\lVert\Pi_{I}(H)\mathbf{1}_{x}\rVert
, this completes the proof of
(
F.3
) in the missing regime and, thus,
the one of (
1.16
).

Next, we show (
1.17
).
First, we assume that
x,y\notin B_{r}(\mathcal{W})
with
r\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{20}\frac{\log N}{\log d}
.
Then (
1.17
) follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, (
F.3
) and
\mathrm{d}(x,y)\leqslant\diam(\mathbb{G})\leqslant 2\frac{\log N}{\log d}
.
Finally, we assume that
x\in B_{r}(\mathcal{W})
with
r
as above. By Proposition
3.2

(iii)
, there is a unique
b\in\mathcal{W}
such that
\mathrm{d}(x,b)\leqslant r
and
\mathrm{d}(x,a)\geqslant 2r

for all
a\in\mathcal{W}\setminus\{b\}
.
As in the second step of (
F.2
), we conclude from (
1.8
) and (
1.9
) that

\lvert\langle\mathbf{1}_{x}\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{w}(b)\rangle\rvert\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-c\mathrm{d}(x,b)}
and
\lvert\langle\mathbf{w}(b)\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{1}_{y}\rangle\rvert\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-c\mathrm{d}(y,b)}
for some constant
c>0
.
Hence, arguing similarly as in (
F.2
) as well as using

\mathrm{d}(x,b)+\mathrm{d}(y,b)\geqslant\mathrm{d}(x,y)
and
\lvert\langle\mathbf{w}(a)\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{1}_{y}\rangle\rvert\leqslant 1
for all
a\in\mathcal{W}\setminus\{b\}
yields (
1.17
).
This completes the proofs of (
1.17
) and Corollary
1.10
.

Appendix G
The expected optimal range for
\mu

In this appendix we give a heuristic argument that yields the expected optimal size of the localized phase, by providing the optimal upper bound on the exponent
\mu
. We also explain how the conclusion of this argument relates to that of
tarzia2022
.

The optimal value for
\mu
can already be seen from the simple case where
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{W}=\{x,y\}
consists of two vertices,
x
and
y
, which are are in resonance (i.e. with normalized degrees close to each other). In that case, as in Proposition
5.3
, we obtain with high probability

(H-\lambda(x))\mathbf{u}(x)=\varepsilon_{y}(x)\mathbf{1}_{y}\,,\qquad\lvert\varepsilon_{y}(x)\rvert\leqslant N^{-1/2+o(1)}\,,

where the estimate on
\varepsilon_{y}(x)
follows as in the proof of Proposition
5.3
.

We conclude that the tunnelling amplitude between
x
and
y
is with high probability bounded by
N^{-1/2+o(1)}
.
By Mott’s criterion, we therefore expect localization at vertex
x
whenever the typical eigenvalue spacing
N^{-\rho_{b}(\lambda(x))+o(1)}
is much larger than
N^{-1/2+o(1)}
, i.e.
\rho_{b}(\lambda(x))<\frac{1}{2}
. This condition holds precisely for the
N^{\mu}
largest eigenvalues of the semilocalized phase for any
\mu<\frac{1}{2}
(see Appendix
B
). This conclusion coincides with that of
tarzia2022
, obtained by a different argument.

The preceding argument has two important shortcomings. First, it only considers a single pair of resonant vertices, instead of the
N^{\mu+o(1)}
vertices associated with the localized phase. Second, it assumes that the eigenvalue spacing around
\lambda(x)
is typical, i.e.
N^{-\mu}
, which is true for most eigenvalues in the localized phase but is false for all eigenvalues simultaneously.

To address these shortcomings, we define a random matrix model that heuristically captures the main features of the localized phase. Define the
\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{W}
matrix
M=(M_{xy})_{x,y\in\mathcal{W}}
through

M_{xy}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\langle\mathbf{u}(x)\mspace{2mu},H\mathbf{u}(y)\rangle\,.

Recall from Proposition
2.3
that
(\mathbf{u}(x))_{x\in\mathcal{W}}
are approximate eigenvectors of
H
, and that all eigenvalues of
H
in the localized phase arise by perturbation from the approximate eigenvalues
(\lambda(x))_{x\in\mathcal{W}}
. Hence, localization is tantamount to the matrix
M
being close to diagonal.

We make the simplifying assumption that the vectors
(\mathbf{u}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in\mathcal{W})
are orthogonal, i.e. we neglect their overlaps for the purpose of this heuristic argument. As above, the proof of Proposition
5.3
implies that

M_{xy}\approx\delta_{xy}\,\lambda(x)+O(N^{-1/2+o(1)})

with high probability for all
x,y\in\mathcal{W}
, since (under the orthogonality assumption)

\displaystyle M_{xy}

\displaystyle\approx\delta_{xy}\,\lambda(x)+(1-\delta_{xy})\,\langle\mathbf{u}(x)\mspace{2mu},(H-\lambda(y))\mathbf{u}(y)\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta_{xy}\,\lambda(x)+(1-\delta_{xy})\,\varepsilon_{x}(y)\langle\mathbf{u}(x)\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{1}_{x}\rangle

\displaystyle\approx\delta_{xy}\,\lambda(x)+(1-\delta_{xy})\,\varepsilon_{x}(y)\,,

where we used that
\langle\mathbf{u}(x)\mspace{2mu},\mathbf{1}_{z}\rangle=0
for any
z\in\mathcal{W}\setminus\{x\}
.

Hence, the random variables
M_{xy}
are typically of order
N^{-1/2+o(1)}
. Assuming that they are mean zero, independent up to the symmetry constraint
M_{xy}=M_{yx}
, and have light enough tails, we therefore arrive at the following toy model for
M
:

M\approx M(t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=D+\sqrt{t}\,W\,,\qquad t=N^{\mu-1+o(1)}\,,

(G.1)

where
D=\diag(\lambda(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in\mathcal{W})
and
W
is an independent
\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{W}
Wigner matrix, normalized so that its entries have variance
1/\lvert\mathcal{W}\rvert
(and hence
W
has typically a norm of order
1
).

The matrix
M(t)
from (
G.1
) is a deformed Wigner matrix, the kind of which has been extensively studied in the random matrix theory literature. Assuming that
W
is a GOE matrix (i.e. with a Gaussian law), the law of
M(t)
is governed by Dyson Brownian motion at time
t
starting from the diagonal matrix
D
. We refer for instance to
VW1
;
benaych2021eigenvectors
;
benigni2020eigenvectors
;
LS1
;
von2019non
.
Although not stated explicitly in these works, the analysis of Dyson Brownian motion, as for instance in
benigni2020eigenvectors
, implies that, with high probability, for any
x\in\mathcal{W}
, the eigenvector associated with the eigenvalue
\lambda(t,x)
of the
\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{W}
matrix
M(t)
remains localized for

t\ll\min\bigl\{\Delta(x)\,,\lvert\mathcal{W}\rvert\,\Delta(x)^{2}\bigr\}\,,

(G.2)

where
\Delta(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\min_{y\neq x}\lvert\lambda(x)-\lambda(y)\rvert
is the eigenvalue spacing of
D
around
\lambda(x)
. Indeed, the eigenvalues
\lambda(t,x)
of
M(t)
satisfy

\mathrm{d}\lambda(t,x)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\lvert\mathcal{W}\rvert}}\,\mathrm{d}B(t,x)+\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{W}\rvert}\sum_{y\neq x}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{\lambda(t,x)-\lambda(t,y)}\,,

where
(B(t,x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}t\geqslant 0,x\in\mathcal{W})
is a family of independent standard Brownian motions. Heuristically, we therefore see immediately that if (
G.2
) holds then
\lvert\lambda(t,x)-\lambda(0,x)\rvert\ll\Delta(x)
, i.e. the shift in
\lambda(x)
is negligible compared to
\Delta(x)
. A similar calculation using Dyson Brownian motion for the eigenvectors (see e.g.
benigni2020eigenvectors
) shows that they remain localized under the condition (
G.2
). Another heuristic way of arriving at the condition (
G.2
) is to notice that under it all finite-order corrections in perturbation theory to
\lambda(t,x)
are
o(\Delta(x))
, and similarly all finite-order corrections in perturbation theory to the eigenvectors are
o(1)
.

We remark that the condition (
G.2
) is strictly weaker than Mott’s criterion, which reads
t\ll\Delta(x)^{2}
. Hence, for the model (
G.1
), Mott’s criterion is sufficient but not necessary for localization. Heuristically, this discrepancy stems from the special relationship between the mean-field matrix
W
and the diagonal eigenvector basis of
D
, in which all entries of
W
are of order
N^{-\mu/2}
, while the norm of
W
is of order
1
.

The typical eigenvalue spacing of
D
is
N^{-\mu+o(1)}
, i.e.
\Delta(x)=N^{-\mu+o(1)}
for most
x\in\mathcal{W}
. In contrast, we expect the minimal eigenvalue spacing
\min_{x\in\mathcal{W}}\Delta(x)
to be
N^{-2\mu+o(1)}
, as follows from basic extreme value theory (cf. the birthday paradox) and the assumption that the random variables
(\lambda(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}x\in\mathcal{W})
are independent. Using
t=N^{\mu-1+o(1)}
and
\lvert\mathcal{W}\rvert=N^{\mu+o(1)}
, from the condition (
G.2
) we therefore expect the following behaviour for the top
N^{\mu}
eigenvectors.

(i)

For
\mu<\frac{1}{2}
most eigenvectors are localized.

(ii)

For
\mu<\frac{1}{4}
all eigenvectors are localized.

For
\frac{1}{4}\leqslant\mu<\frac{1}{2}
, while most eigenvectors are localized, we expect some eigenvectors to hybridize with others associated with resonant vertices. These hybridized eigenvectors have a divergent localization length (
1.14
). In fact, a more refined analysis of Dyson Brownian motion in (
G.1
), as performed for instance in
benigni2020eigenvectors
, yields a precise picture of the hybridized structure of such eigenvectors. We shall not go into further details in this appendix.

In particular, the optimal bound for
\mu
in Theorem
1.1
is expected to be
\frac{1}{4}
.
In our proof we obtain the bound
\frac{1}{24}
because both our upper bound on the tunnelling amplitude (Proposition
2.3
) is worse than
N^{-1/2+o(1)}
and because our lower bound on the eigenvalue spacing (Proposition
2.4
) is worse than
N^{-2\mu+o(1)}
. Both of these bounds, however, can be somewhat improved by a suitable refinement of our proof.

johannes.alt@iam.uni-bonn.de
.

Raphaël Ducatez, Université Lyon 1, Institut Camille Jordan,
ducatez@math.univ-lyon1.fr
.

antti.knowles@unige.ch
.
